The intersection dual of geodesic currents
Dídac Martínez-Granado, Dylan P. Thurston

Abstract
. Geodesic currents on closed hyperbolic surfaces are measures on the unit tangent bundle invariant under geodesic flow and orientation reversal. Every geodesic current induces a dual function on curves via the geometric intersection pairing. It is natural to ask which curve functions are dual to geodesic currents, that is, which arise as intersection functionals of a geodesic current. In this paper we give a purely axiomatic and combinatorial characterization of curve functionals dual to geodesic currents. This yields a new definition of geodesic currents as curve functionals or, equivalently, as functions on surface groups, without reference to measures or flows. More precisely, we show that a function on curves arises as the geometric intersection pairing with a geodesic current if and only if it is additive under disjoint union and satisfies a simple smoothing property: it is non-increasing under surgery of essential crossings. As applications, we obtain new axiomatic characterizations of measured laminations and hyperbolic length functions, and new descriptions of small surface group actions on real trees, including a concise proof of a classical theorem of Skora. We also provide a unified framework for dual geodesic currents arising from metric structures and generalized cross-ratios, including those associated with certain Anosov representations. Our approach subsumes all previously known constructions of dual geodesic currents and yields broad new families of examples.



1.
Introduction

Geodesic currents on a hyperbolic surface are
\pi_{1}(S)
-invariant
Radon measures on the
space of unoriented geodesics on
\widetilde{S}
, forming the weak
∗

closure of the set
of weighted closed geodesics. Equivalently, from a dynamical
viewpoint, they may be regarded as measures on the unit tangent bundle
that are invariant under the geodesic flow and the orientation flip.
Since their introduction by Bonahon
[
9
]

in his study of ends of hyperbolic
3
-manifolds, they have become a
central tool in low-dimensional geometry and topology. They play a
fundamental role in compactifications of spaces of geometric
structures
[
10
,
27
,
57
,
17
]
, in curve counting
problems
[
71
,
28
,
29
]
, in marked length spectrum
rigidity
[
63
,
24
,
40
,
22
]
, and more recently in
geometric group theory
[
42
,
20
,
19
]
.

The geometric intersection number of multi-curves (finite unions of closed curves on
S
) extends continuously to a bilinear function of geodesic currents
i(\cdot,\cdot)
by a result of Bonahon
[
9
, Proposition 4.5]
. Moreover, by Otal’s work
[
63
, Théorème 2]
, a geodesic current
\mu
is uniquely determined by the functional
i(\mu,\cdot)\colon\mathcal{C}(S)\to\mathbb{R}
, where
\mathcal{C}(S)
denotes the space of multi-curves. Thus geodesic currents embed into the space of real-valued functionals on curves via intersection.

Many natural curve functionals arise in this way. Hyperbolic length

\ell_{\Sigma}
is given by intersection with the Liouville
current
[
10
, Theorem 10]
; the same holds
for negatively curved
[
63
]

and non-positively
curved Riemannian metrics
[
24
]
.
Beyond examples arising from metrics on the
surface, word length with respect to simple generating sets admits a
dual current
[
30
, Theorem 1.2]
, and length
functions associated to certain higher-rank representations of surface
groups (such as Hitchin representations) also arise from
currents
[
57
,
17
]
.

From this perspective, geodesic currents form a distinguished subclass of curve functionals. The ambient space is vast and includes many natural examples not arising from currents (Section
2.3
). We give a necessary and sufficient intrinsic axiomatic characterization of those curve functionals that arise as intersection with a geodesic current.

Theorem A
.

A curve functional
f\colon\mathcal{C}(S)\to\mathbb{R}
is dual to a
geodesic current
\mu_{f}
in the sense that

i(\mu_{f},C)=f(C)\quad\text{for all curves }C

if and only if it satisfies the

smoothing property

(1.1)

f\!\left(\vbox{\hbox{$\includegraphics[]{curves-0}$}}\right)\geq\max\!\left\{f\!\left(\vbox{\hbox{$\includegraphics[]{curves-1}$}}\right),f\!\left(\vbox{\hbox{$\includegraphics[]{curves-2}$}}\right)\right\},

at every essential crossing of a multi-curve and is
additive
on connected components
(see Section
2.1
). If the dual current
\mu_{f}

exists, it is unique.

This theorem may be regarded as an analogue of Poincaré duality
(see Section
11.8
), in that it identifies
the space of curve functionals arising from geodesic currents via
intersection.

An essential crossing is, informally, an intersection
that cannot be removed by homotopy (see
Definition
4.3
). Such crossings include both self-intersections of a single component
and intersections between distinct components.

The hypotheses of smoothing and additivity are
independent and necessary (see
Section
2.3
).

Theorem
A
produces numerous new dual currents and unifies several constructions. In particular, it applies to curve lengths arising from arbitrary Riemannian metrics on
S
, and more generally from any length (pseudo-)metric on
S
, including Finsler metrics (see Corollary
8.10
).

Corollary B
.

Let
d
be a length (pseudo-)metric on
S
, with induced length on curves
\ell_{d}
. Then there exists a geodesic current
\mu_{d}
dual to
\ell_{d}
.

This extends earlier constructions for negatively curved, non-positively curved, and locally
\CAT(0)
or
\CAT(-1)
metrics
[
63
,
24
,
27
,
40
,
4
,
22
]
.

Theorem
A
further yields dual currents (in fact, dual multi-curves) for stable
lengths associated to embedded graphs. Let
\iota\colon\mathcal{G}\hookrightarrow S
be a filling embedded graph, in the sense that the complementary
regions are disks.
Endow
\mathcal{G}
with the edge-metric
g
(i.e., each edge has
length 1). Then any closed multi-curve
C
on
S
can be homotoped so
that it factors through
\mathcal{G}
.
Let
\ell_{\mathcal{G}}(C)
be the length of the shortest multi-curve

D
on
\mathcal{G}
so that
\iota(D)
is homotopic to
C
.

Corollary C
.

Let
\mathcal{G}\hookrightarrow S
be a filling embedded graph endowed with the edge-metric. Then
the functional
\ell_{\mathcal{G}}
is dual to a
geodesic current
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}
which is
1/2
of an integral
multi-curve.

This generalizes the work of Erlandsson
[
30
]
(Corollary
8.7
).

All of these examples give lengths extending continuously to
\Curr(S)

by Bonahon’s bilinear extension of intersection. A result
of Erlandsson-Parlier-Souto
[
28
, Theorem 1.5]

establishes continuous
extensions for arbitrary Riemannian lengths and, more generally, for a
broad class of lengths on Gromov hyperbolic spaces. In the surface
setting, Theorem
A
strengthens this by showing that
many such extensions arise canonically as intersection with a current.

As a further application, we prove in
Section
8.4
that domination of
intersection numbers with all closed curves implies domination of
length spectra for any length pseudo-metric on a closed surface. In
particular, this answers a question of
Neumann-Coto
[
62
, p. 368]
.

For curve functionals arising in algebraic contexts—such as
cross-ratios (Section
10
) or translation
lengths of group actions on real trees
(Section
9
)—it is natural to reformulate the
smoothing condition in purely group-theoretic language, or, equivalently, in terms of
oriented curves.

Let
a,b\in\pi_{1}(S)
be non-trivial elements that are not common
powers. We write
A
for the inverse of
a\in\pi_{1}(S)
. Fix any
hyperbolic structure on
S
, and denote by
\overrightarrow{a}
and
\overrightarrow{b}

the corresponding axes in the universal cover. We say
(\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{b})
are
crossing
if they intersect at a point, and are
parallel
if they are non-intersecting and coherently aligned. (See
Definition
4.7
and
Figure
4.3
for a precise definition.)

Theorem A
′
.

Let
f\colon\pi_{1}(S)\to\mathbb{R}
be a function and
\overline{f}(g)\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}\left(f(g)+f(G)\right)
for every
g\in\pi_{1}(S)
.
Then there exists a dual geodesic current
\mu_{\overline{f}}
on
S
so that

i(\mu_{\overline{f}},[g])=\overline{f}(g)\quad\text{for every }g\in\pi_{1}(S).

if and only if
f
satisfies the following conditions for
a,b\in\pi_{1}(S)
:

(1)

class invariant:
f(a)=f(b)
whenever
a
is conjugate to
b
.

(2)

stability:

f(a^{n})=nf(a)\mbox{ for all }n\geq 1
.

(3)

oriented smoothing:

(1.2)

\displaystyle f(ab)

\displaystyle\geq f(a)+f(b)

\displaystyle\mbox{ if }(a,b)\mbox{ are }\mbox{parallel;}

(1.3)

\displaystyle f(a)+f(b)

\displaystyle\geq f(ab)

\displaystyle\mbox{ if }(a,b)\mbox{ are }\mbox{crossing}.

Condition (3) in Theorem
A′
is formally
weaker than Eq. (
1.1
) of Theorem
A
, as
it captures smoothing only in an oriented sense (see
Section
4
). Nevertheless, when combined with the
remaining hypotheses, it becomes equivalent to the full smoothing
condition for
\overline{f}
; more precisely, equations (
1.2
)
and (
1.3
) together recover smoothing in the
sense of Theorem
A

(Corollary
7.3
).

Inequalities (
1.2
) and (
1.3
) are individually necessary and logically independent (Section
2.1
), but become equivalent for functionals that extend continuously to geodesic currents (Proposition
7.2
). Moreover,
the symmetrization
\overline{f}(g)=\tfrac{1}{2}(f(g)+f(G))
is
essential; without it, the conclusion fails
(Appendix
B
).

Theorem
A
may be viewed as a far-reaching extension
of Feng Luo’s criterion
[
53
]
, which
characterizes those functionals on simple closed curves that arise as
intersection with measured laminations.

In previous work
[
55
]
, we showed that stable
lengths associated to quasi-Fuchsian representations satisfy

quasi-smoothing
, namely smoothing up to a uniform additive
error. Recent work of
Fricker-Furman
[
31
]
(see
also
[
14
]
) proves that this error term cannot, in
general, be eliminated unless the representation is Fuchsian. In the
Fuchsian case, the associated length functional is dual to a geodesic
current
[
10
, Proposition 14]
, and hence
satisfies exact smoothing. Theorem
A
places these
results in a unified framework by identifying smoothing and additivity
as the precise necessary and sufficient conditions for duality with a
geodesic current.
These results identify a common axiomatic structure underlying curve functionals arising from a variety of geometric and dynamical constructions, which we explore further below.

1.1.
Characterization of measured laminations and hyperbolic metrics

As an application of Theorem
A′
, we obtain intrinsic
characterizations of those curve functionals whose dual currents are
measured laminations or hyperbolic Liouville currents.
(Measured laminations are precisely those currents
\alpha

satisfying
i(\alpha,\alpha)=0

[
10
, Proposition 17]
.)

Theorem D
.

Let
f\colon\pi_{1}(S)\to\mathbb{R}
be a function and set

\overline{f}(g)\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}\bigl(f(g)+f(g^{-1})\bigr).

Then there exists a measured lamination, respectively a hyperbolic metric,

\mu_{\overline{f}}
on
S
such that

i(\mu_{\overline{f}},[g])=\overline{f}(g)\quad\text{for every }g\in\pi_{1}(S),

if and only if
f
satisfies the conditions of
Theorem
A′

and, when
(\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{b})
are crossing, the corresponding condition:

\begin{array}[]{c@{\qquad}c}\textbf{Measured lamination {(L)}}&\textbf{Hyperbolic metric {(H)}}\\[4.0pt]
f(a)+f(b)=\max\{f(ab),f(aB)\}.&\lambda(a)\lambda(b)=\lambda(ab)+\lambda(aB),\text{ where }\lambda(g)\coloneqq 2\cosh\!\bigl(f(g)/2\bigr).\end{array}

This theorem is proved in Section
8
.

The
\lambda
-length functional arises naturally in the study of the
skein algebra of a surface; see, for example,

[
69
, Secs. 1–2]
and

[
78
, Sec. 3]
.

We highlight some features of Theorem
D
.

•

Combined with Theorem
A
, it provides
criteria for determining whether a curve functional arises from a
measured lamination or from a hyperbolic metric. It suffices to verify
a countable family of inequalities together with a countable family of
equalities, where the equalities involve
only
crossing pairs of elements in
\pi_{1}(S)
. In particular, in the
hyperbolic case this avoids checking relations coming from
self-crossings. In the setting of trace identities in the

\PSL(2,\mathbb{R})
character variety, the corresponding relations for
self-crossings are more intricate, as they depend on sign choices.

•

It characterizes hyperbolic metrics among
all

curve functionals. In particular, it applies to stable lengths arising
from Green metrics associated to admissible random walks on

\pi_{1}(S)
, suggesting applications to the study of singularity
properties of their hitting measures; see
Section
11.4
.

•

It extends Luo’s characterizations of those
functions on simple closed curves that arise as either intersection with
measured laminations
[
53
, Theorem 1.1, Theorem 1.2]

or as hyperbolic length
functions
[
52
]
.

•

Likewise, we recover the result of the second
author
[
77
]

that measured laminations satisfy max-smoothing.

1.2.
Characterizing small actions on real trees

We next apply Theorem
A
to actions of surface groups on
\mathbb{R}
-trees. We obtain new characterizations of those
\pi_{1}(S)
-actions that are dual to measured laminations. In particular, we give an axiomatic characterization in terms of explicit inequalities on pairs of group elements. Skora’s classical theorem then emerges as a formal consequence, yielding a conceptual and streamlined proof
[
74
]
.

A
\Gamma
-tree
is an isometric action of a finitely generated
group
\Gamma
on an
\mathbb{R}
-tree
T
. For
g\in\Gamma
, its
translation length is

\|g\|=\inf_{x\in T}d(x,gx).

The
characteristic set

T_{g}
of
g\in\Gamma
is the set of
points that are translated the least. Concretely,
an isometry
g
of
T
either has a non-empty set
T_{g}
of fixed
points (
elliptic
) or
has no fixed points and translates points on its invariant
axis
T_{g}
by a definite distance of
\|g\|

(
hyperbolic
). See
[
25
, §1.3]
.

We say the action is
irreducible
if it does not have a global
fixed point,
T
has no ends fixed by all of

\Gamma
, and there are no
\Gamma
-invariant lines (see Section
9
).

An
S
-tree
is a minimal
\pi_{1}(S)
-tree.
An
S
-tree
preserves axis intersection
if whenever two elements

a,b\in\pi_{1}(S)
acting hyperbolically on the tree have intersecting hyperbolic axes in

\widetilde{\Sigma}
, their axes in
T
also intersect.
If the same holds for all characteristic sets (including elements
acting elliptically on
T
),
we say the action
preserves characteristic intersection
.

A fundamental example is the
S
-tree
T_{\lambda}
dual to a measured
lamination
\lambda
, obtained by collapsing
\widetilde{\Sigma}

by the pseudo-metric induced by the transverse

\lambda
-measure.

If two hyperbolic isometries of
T
have axes intersecting in a
non-degenerate arc, we say they
overlap incoherently
if they
translate in opposite directions along the overlap. We prove the following equivalences.

Theorem E
.

Let
(S,T)
be an
S
-tree. The following are equivalent:

(1)

There exists a measured lamination
\lambda
on
S
such that

T
is
\pi_{1}(S)
-equivariantly isometric to
T_{\lambda}
;

(2)

The
S
-tree is irreducible and preserves axis intersection;

(3)

The
S
-tree is irreducible and preserves characteristic intersection;

(4)

The translation length function

\|\cdot\|\colon\pi_{1}(S)\to\mathbb{R}
satisfies:

(a)

For every pair
(a,b)
crossing in
\widetilde{S}
,

(1.4)

\|a\|+\|b\|=\max\{\|ab\|,\|aB\|\};

(b)

For every pair
(a,b)
parallel in
\widetilde{S}
,

(1.5)

\|ab\|=\max\{\|a\|+\|b\|,\|aB\|\}.

(5)

The
S
-tree has no pair of hyperbolic isometries that are
parallel in
\widetilde{S}
and overlap incoherently in
T
.

The equivalence of (A) and (B) recovers
Skora’s theorem
[
74
]
.
All remaining equivalences are new.

Skora also gave another condition
equivalent to duality with a measured lamination
[
75
]
:

(F)

The
S
-tree is
small
, i.e., stabilizers of non-degenerate arcs are cyclic.

For a general finitely generated group
\Gamma
,
Culler-Morgan
[
25
]
identified axioms satisfied by
translation lengths of
\Gamma
-actions on
\mathbb{R}
-trees and asked
whether these axioms characterize such actions among conjugacy-invariant
functions
f\colon\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}
. Parry
[
67
]

answered this affirmatively.
A restatement of these axioms (see Proposition
9.1
)
is that a conjugacy-invariant
function
\|\cdot\|\colon\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}
is the translation length
of a
\Gamma
-tree if and only if for every pair
a,b\in\Gamma
the
maximum among

\|a\|+\|b\|,\qquad\|ab\|,\qquad\|aB\|

is attained at least twice.

When
\Gamma=\pi_{1}(S)
, Theorem
A
refines Parry’s
characterization by isolating precisely which translation lengths arise
from
small
actions on
\mathbb{R}
-trees, or equivalently from
actions dual to measured laminations.

1.3.
Generalized cross-ratios

We next apply Theorem
A
to generalized cross-ratios, thereby placing the constructions of Martone–Zhang
[
57
]
and Burger–Iozzi–Parreau–Pozzetti
[
17
]
, which associate geodesic currents to generalized cross-ratios arising from certain Anosov representations, into a unified framework.

A
generalized cross-ratio
is a function

[\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot]\colon(\partial_{\infty}S)^{(4)}\to\mathbb{R}

on ordered 4-tuples of distinct boundary points satisfying natural
invariance, symmetry, finite additivity, and non-negativity properties
(see Definition
10.1
).
For instance, if
\Sigma
is a hyperbolic metric on
S
, the classical
hyperbolic cross-ratio
[\cdot]_{\Sigma}
obtained from the upper
half-plane model provides such an example.

Every generalized cross-ratio induces an additive curve functional,
called its
period
, denoted
\ell_{[\cdot]}
.
In the hyperbolic case,
\ell_{[\cdot]_{\Sigma}}
coincides with the
hyperbolic length function.

The following theorem shows that periods automatically satisfy the
axioms of Theorem
A
.

Theorem F
.

Let
[\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot]
be a generalized cross-ratio with
associated period
\ell
. Then
\ell
satisfies the hypotheses of
Theorem
A
and therefore induces a geodesic current

\mu_{\ell}
.

In particular, this result recovers
[
17
, Proposition 4.8]
, and provides another avenue to construct geodesic currents associated to compactifications of certain Anosov representations, a trend that has recently garnished notable traction (see, e.g.
[
66
,
65
,
72
]
).

1.5.
Organization

Notation

Meaning

S

closed, connected, orientable topological surface of genus
\geq 2

\Sigma

hyperbolic structure on
S

\pi_{1}(S)

fundamental group of
S

G(S)

oriented geodesics of
\widetilde{S}

\partial_{\infty}S

boundary at infinity of
\widetilde{S}

(\partial_{\infty}S)^{(4)}

4-tuples of ccw distinct ordered points in
\partial_{\infty}S

\mathcal{C}(S)

unoriented multi-curves

\mathcal{C}^{+}(S)

oriented multi-curves

f

curve functional

\Curr(S)

flip-invariant geodesic currents

\Curr^{+}(S)

oriented geodesic currents

C,D

multi-curves on
S

g,h,a,b,x

elements of
\pi_{1}(S)

\overrightarrow{g}

axis of hyperbolic isometry of
g\in\pi_{1}(S)

\Gamma

general group (sometimes Gromov hyperbolic)

g^{-1},G

both denote the inverse of
g\in\Gamma

X

general set

\mathcal{B}

family of right-handed boxes

g\in\Gamma

real tree

T_{g}

g\in\Gamma
acting on
T

Table 1
.
Notation for the most common objects in the paper.

In Section
2
, we describe the hypotheses of Theorem
A
and Theorem
A′
, and place them in the broader setting of curve functionals. We give examples that satisfy these hypotheses, as well as natural ones that do not.

In Section
3
, we introduce the basic background. In particular, we recall the measure-theoretic background and the existence of limits of certain sequences used in the construction of the measure in Theorem
A
.

In Section
4
, we establish basic lemmas on essential crossings and smoothings. We review left and right actions of
\pi_{1}(S)
on
\widetilde{S}
. We relate the topological, algebraic, and geometric notions of smoothing. We prove further results on crossings and parallelism used throughout the paper. We then derive more technical lemmas involving triples of elements of
\pi_{1}(S)
, which form a key step in the proof of Theorem
A
.

In Section
5
, we define the dual measure associated to a curve functional by specifying its values on a dense family of right-handed boxes, which we show form a semi-ring in the measure theoretic sense. The measure is
\pi_{1}(S)
-invariant on this family. We prove that it is non-negative and finitely additive on the semi-ring.

In Section
6
, we finish the construction of the dual geodesic current. In particular, we show that the dual measure is countably additive and defines an oriented geodesic current. By averaging, we obtain a geodesic current. Finally, we show that this current is dual to the original curve functional. We also introduce the notion of asymmetric intersection number. This completes the proof of Theorem
A
. We then prove Theorem
A′
and several variants as corollaries.

In Section
7
, we analyze the different notions
of smoothing (oriented vs. unoriented, connected vs. disconnected). We use
these results to deduce Theorem
A′
from
Theorem
A
; the proof of Theorem
A

does not rely on this section.

In Section
8
, we discuss subclasses of
geodesic currents, including filling currents, multi-curves, measured
laminations, and hyperbolic lengths/Liouville currents. In particular,
we prove Theorem
D
. We
construct currents dual to
embedded graphs. We explain how to obtain dual currents for arbitrary
length metrics.

In Section
9
, we prove Theorem
E
. We recall the necessary background on real trees and group actions.
In Section
10
, we show that period functionals
arising from generalized cross-ratios satisfy the hypotheses of
Theorem
A′
. They therefore induce geodesic
currents; this proves Theorem
F
.
Finally, Section
11
discusses further questions and conjectures.

The Appendices address topics not central to the main proofs. Appendix
A
gives a hyperbolic
parallelogram identity used to characterize hyperbolic lengths.
Appendix
B
explains why asymmetric word metrics are
not dual to right-handed intersection numbers, highlighting the role
of symmetry in Theorem
A
.
Appendix
C
gives an alternative proof of the
necessity of the hypotheses in Theorem
A
and
Theorem
A′
, using hyperbolic geometry on
\widetilde{S}
.

2.
Curve functionals: properties, general context and examples

In this section we state the main technical hypotheses that we require
of our curve functionals, including examples and non-examples for the
hypotheses of Theorem
A
.
In particular, we relate the results of this paper to our previous
work
[
55
]
.

2.1.
Curve functionals and their properties

In our constructions, it will be crucial to work in the setting of oriented curves.

Definition 2.1

(multi-curve)
.

A
concrete multi-curve

\gamma
on a surface
S
is a smooth
oriented 1-manifold without boundary
X(\gamma)
together with an orientation-preserving continuous map
(also called
\gamma
) from
X(\gamma)

into
S
.
X(\gamma)
is not necessarily connected.
We say that
\gamma
is
trivial
if it is
homotopic to a point.
Two concrete multi-curves
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
are
equivalent
if
they are related by a sequence of the following moves:

•

homotopy
within the space of all maps from
X(\gamma)
to
S
;

•

orientation-preserving reparametrization
of the 1-manifold;
and

•

dropping
trivial components.

The equivalence class of
\gamma
is denoted by
[\gamma]
, and we will
call it a
multi-curve
.
If
X(\gamma)
is connected, we will call
\gamma
a
concrete curve
.
A concrete multi-curve
\gamma
is
simple
if
\gamma
is injective, and a multi-curve is simple if it has a concrete representative
that is simple.
We write
\mathcal{C}^{+}(S)
for the space of all multi-curves.
Most of the multi-curves we consider will be oriented. There is a corresponding unoriented notion, which we call an
unoriented multi-curve
. This is defined as above, except that the 1-manifold
X(\gamma)
are taken without orientation, and the equivalence relation is generated by reparameterizations that are not required to preserve orientation.

Notation
.

From now on, for
a,b\in\pi_{1}(S)
, we write
[a][b]
to denote the multi-curve
[a]\cup[b]
.

A few times throught the paper we will allow multi-curves to have weights.

Definition 2.2

(weighted multi-curve)
.

A
weighted multi-curve

C=\bigcup_{i}a_{i}C_{i}
is a multi-curve in
which each connected component is given a non-negative real
coefficient
a_{i}
. If coefficients are not specified, they are
1
.
We add further moves to the equivalence
relation:

•

merging
two parallel components and
adding their weights; and

•

nullifying
, deleting a
component with weight
0
.

For
instance,
C\cup C
is equivalent to
2C
.

Oriented multi-curves are more natural from a group-theoretic perspective, appearing naturally in the study of cross-ratios (Section
10
) and group actions on real trees (Section
9
).
Moreover, to verify smoothing for curve functionals that do not arise from natural length metrics on the surface, it is convenient to work in terms of the relative position of pairs of hyperbolic elements in
\pi_{1}(S)
, where orientation is essential (see Section
4.3
and compare Theorem
A
with Theorem
A′
).

Accordingly, we will
work with oriented geodesic currents, i.e., invariant measures on oriented geodesics of
\widetilde{\Sigma}
(see
Section
3.3
for details). Some authors prefer to work with
unoriented geodesic currents
(see Remark
3.16
), but oriented currents are more general, and appear naturally in algebraic contexts
[
11
,
28
,
20
,
19
]
(as alluded above) as well as higher Teichmüller theory and Anosov representations
[
12
]
.
In practice, the oriented geodesic currents we will be interested in our applications (Section
8
,
10
,
9
) will also be flip invariant, and in this case there is a bijection
[
29
, Exercise 3.1]

\{\text{unoriented geodesic currents}\}\longleftrightarrow\{\text{flip-invariant oriented geodesic currents}\}.

Any flip-invariant oriented geodesic current is determined by its geometric intersection number with all closed curves
[
63
, Théorème 2]
, a fact which fails to be true for general oriented geodesic currents.
The geometric intersection number will play a central role throughout.
By default, the intersection number
i(C,D)
of two multi-curves is
defined, for unoriented curves, as the minimal number of intersections among transversely intersecting representatives. If
C
and
D
are oriented,
the
intersection number is defined by forgetting orientations. See also
Definition
3.20
. The reader should keep in mind the example of the curve functional
i(\mu,\cdot)\colon\mathcal{C}^{+}(S)\to\mathbb{R}
, for a given oriented multi-curve or, more generally, oriented geodesic current
\mu
.

As explained in Appendix
B
, there are asymmetric
variants of intersection number for oriented curves which only record crossings of a given orientation. For such notions, the analogue of the main
duality theorem (Theorem
A
) fails. This phenomenon
also appears in the proof (see Proposition
6.9
).

A curve functional that does not depend
on orientation may equivalently be viewed as a functional on
unoriented multi-curves. Many of our examples will be of this form.

Definition 2.3
.

Let
S
be a
closed
surface, i.e. a compact topological surface without boundary, and let

f\colon\mathcal{C}^{+}(S)\to\mathbb{R}
be a function on the space of oriented multi-curves.
We will refer to such an
f
as a
curve functional
.
(The term “functional” indicates that
f
takes scalar values; it is not assumed to be linear, although in the applications of Theorem
A
it will be additive.)
We introduce several properties that
f
may satisfy.

•

Symmetry.

If
C
and
C^{\prime}
are oriented multi-curves with the same underlying unoriented multi-curve, then

f(C)=f(C^{\prime}).

•

Oriented smoothing.

Let
C
be an oriented curve on
S
, and let
x
be an essential crossing of
C
.
Let
C^{\prime}
denote the oriented smoothing of
C
at
x
.
Then

(2.1)

f\!\left(\vbox{\hbox{$\includegraphics[]{curves-3}$}}\right)\;\geq\;f\!\left(\vbox{\hbox{$\includegraphics[]{curves-4}$}}\right).

(See Definition
4.3
for the notion of
essential crossing; it is a crossing that cannot be removed by
homotopy.) If the inequality is replaced by equality, we obtain
oriented max-smoothing.

•

Additivity.

For oriented curves
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
on
S
,

(2.2)

f(C_{1}\cup C_{2})=f(C_{1})+f(C_{2}).

Let
[a]
denote the conjugacy class of
a\in\pi_{1}(S)
, which, equivalently, can be thought of as the free homotopy class of the oriented curve represented by
a
.

The oriented smoothing condition will be further divided into three
types (See
Definition
4.6
.) Accordingly, we will
say
f
satisfies a particular type of smoothing property if
f
is
non-increasing under such type.

Lemma 2.4
.

A curve functional
f\colon\mathcal{C}^{+}(S)\to\mathbb{R}
satisfies
oriented smoothing iff the following three families of
inequalities are satisfied.

•

Oriented disconnected smoothing.

For any pair of elements
a,b\in\pi_{1}(S)
whose hyperbolic axes intersect,

(2.3)

f([a][b])\geq f([ab]).

•

Oriented connected smoothing.

For any pair of elements
a,b\in\pi_{1}(S)
whose hyperbolic axes are parallel
(i.e., disjoint and coherently oriented),

(2.4)

f([ab])\geq f([a][b]).

•

Power smoothing.

For any element
a\in\pi_{1}(S)
and integers
n,m\geq 1
,

(2.5)

f([a^{n+m}])\geq f([a^{m}][a^{n}]).

If the inequalities are replaced by equalities in all the items of the previous Lemma, we get
oriented max-smoothing
.

Lemma 2.5
.

A curve functional
f\colon\mathcal{C}^{+}(S)\to\mathbb{R}
satisfies
oriented max-smoothing iff it satisfies
Eqs. (
2.3
), (
2.4
),
and (
2.5
) with equality replacing the equalities.

Lemmas
2.4

and
2.5
are proved in
Section
4.3
.

Definition 2.6
.

For
C
an oriented multi-curve,
C^{n}
is the oriented multi-curve with as many components as
C
, in which each
component of
C
is covered by an
n
-fold cover. That is, if

g\in\pi_{1}(S,x)
represents
C
,
g^{n}

represents
C^{n}
.
On the other hand,
nC
is the oriented multi-curve
that consists of
n
parallel
copies of
C
(so with
n
times as many components) or,
in the context of weighted oriented multi-curves,
with weights multiplied by
n
.

Definition 2.7
.

Let
f
be a curve
functional and let
n>0
be an
integer. We say
f
satisfies
stability
if, for an arbitrary oriented multi-curve
C
,

(2.6)

f(C^{n})=f(nC).

A related notion is that of
sub-stability

(2.7)

f(C^{n+m})\geq f(C^{m})+f(C^{n})

for every
m,n\geq 0
.

It is immediate that if a curve functional satisfies additivity, then stability implies sub-stability, and power smoothing and sub-stability are equivalent.

Definition 2.8
.

For an arbitrary oriented
multi-curve
C
, we say
f
satisfies
homogeneity
if

(2.8)

f(nC)=nf(C).

Note that functionals satisfying additivity (Eq. (
2.2
)) automatically satisfy homogeneity.

We emphasize that oriented connected smoothing does not imply oriented
disconnected smoothing, nor vice versa.

For instance, the curve functional
f
assigning to a curve its number
of connected components satisfies oriented disconnected smoothing but
not oriented connected smoothing. Its negative satisfies oriented
connected smoothing but not oriented disconnected smoothing.

Although this example is not stable, one can construct additive and
stable functionals exhibiting exactly one of these two smoothing
properties;
see Example
2.17
and
Lemma
9.15
.
There also exist functionals that satisfy stability and smoothing but
fail additivity; see
Example
2.17
.

On the other hand, Proposition
7.8
shows that
smoothing implies stability.

Slight generalizations of both smoothing and additivity were
introduced in
[
55
]
.

Definition 2.9
.

Let
f
be a curve functional. We define the
following properties.

•

Oriented quasi-smoothing:
There is a constant
R\geq 0
with the
following property. Let
C
be an oriented curve on
S
, and
let
x
be an essential crossing of
C
. Let
C^{\prime}
be the oriented
smoothing of
C
at
x
. Then
f(C)\geq f(C^{\prime})-R
. Schematically, we
have

(2.9)

f\left(\vbox{\hbox{$\includegraphics[]{curves-3}$}}\right)\geq f\left(\vbox{\hbox{$\includegraphics[]{curves-4}$}}\right)-R.

If
R=0
, we recover the oriented smoothing property.

We note that the above properties correspond to what we termed

quasi-smoothing
and
smoothing
in

[
55
, Eqs. (1.2), (1.3)]
. In the present paper,
we distinguish these notions from their unoriented counterparts (see
below).

•

Convex union:
Let
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
be two oriented curves
on
S
. Then

(2.10)

f(C_{1}\cup C_{2})\leq f(C_{1})+f(C_{2}).

If this inequality is strengthened to an equality, we recover additivity (Eq. (
2.2
)).

Theorem
A
considered curve functions on the space of unoriented multi-curves
\mathcal{C}(S)
.

When performing a smoothing for an oriented multi-curve, one could
also choose to reglue in the incoherent orientation. In this case,
there two possible (incoherent) orientations to equip the resulting
curve with. See also Section
4.2
.

Definition 2.10

(smoothing and max-smoothing)
.

Let
C
be an unoriented closed curve, and
C^{\prime}
either of the two smoothings. We say
f\colon\mathcal{C}(S)\to\mathbb{R}
satisfies
smoothing
if
f(C)\geq f(C^{\prime})
, Schematically, we have

f\left(\vbox{\hbox{$\includegraphics[]{curves-0}$}}\right)\geq\max\left\{f\left(\vbox{\hbox{$\includegraphics[]{curves-1}$}}\right),f\left(\vbox{\hbox{$\includegraphics[]{curves-2}$}}\right)\right\}.

If this inequality is strengthened to an equality, we get
the property called
max-smoothing
.

Smoothing is satisfied by many natural functionals, including
intersection functionals
i(\mu,\cdot)
for any geodesic current
\mu

(see
[
55
, Section 4.1]
; cf. Appendix
C
),
as well as curve lengths arising from a length metric structure on
S

(see
[
55
, Section 4.3]
; cf. Section
8.4
).
Further examples are discussed in Section
2.3
.

As before, smoothing and max-smoothing admit algebraic reformulations.

Definition 2.11

(smoothing for oriented curve functionals)
.

Given an
oriented
curve functional
f\colon\mathcal{C}^{+}(S)\to\mathbb{R}
, we will say it satisfies
smoothing
if it satisfies the following hypotheses:

•

symmetry;

•

(disconnected smoothing) For any pair of
elements
a,b\in\pi_{1}(S)
whose hyperbolic axes intersect, we have

f([a][b])\geq\max\,\{\,f([ab]),f([aB])\,\};

if the inequality is
strengthened to an equality we obtain
disconnected max-smoothing
.

•

(connected smoothing) for any pair of elements
a,b\in\pi_{1}(S)
whose hyperbolic axes are parallel, we have

f([ab])\geq\max\,\{\,f([aB]),f([a][b])\,\};

if the inequality is
strengthened to an equality we get
connected max-smoothing
.

•

(power smoothing) and for any element
a\in\pi_{1}(S)
, and any
integers
n,m\geq 1
, we have

(2.11)

f([a^{n+m}])\geq f([a^{m}])+f([a^{n}]).

Given a curve functional on unoriented multi-curves

f\colon\mathcal{C}(S)\to\mathbb{R}
, there is a canonical extension to
oriented multi-curves defined by forgetting orientation:

f^{+}(C)\coloneqq f(\overline{C}),

where
\overline{C}
denotes the underlying unoriented multi-curve.

Conversely, if
f\colon\mathcal{C}^{+}(S)\to\mathbb{R}
is symmetric, then it induces a functional on unoriented multi-curves

\overline{f}\colon\mathcal{C}(S)\to\mathbb{R},\qquad\overline{f}(\overline{C})\coloneqq f(C),

where
C
is any oriented representative of the unoriented
multi-curve
\overline{C}
.

Lemma 2.12
.

The following equivalences hold.

•

Let
f\colon\mathcal{C}(S)\to\mathbb{R}
be an unoriented curve functional.
Then
f
satisfies smoothing (resp. max-smoothing) in the sense of
Definition
2.10
if and only if its extension

f^{+}
satisfies smoothing (resp. max-smoothing) in the sense of
Definition
2.11
.

•

Let
f\colon\mathcal{C}^{+}(S)\to\mathbb{R}
be an
oriented curve functional.
Then
f
satisfies smoothing (resp. max-smoothing) in the sense of
Definition
2.11
if and only if the induced
functional
\overline{f}
satisfies smoothing (resp. max-smoothing)
in the sense of Definition
2.10
.

Similarly, the above equivalences can be stated for quasi-smoothing.

We refer the reader to Sections
4.2

and
4.3
for precise
definitions and proofs of
Lemma
2.4
,

2.5
,
and
2.12
. See in particular
Proposition
4.10
.

2.2.
General context and examples

Here we place our results in the broader framework initiated
in
[
55
]
, explain their relation to recent work in
the literature, and provide examples of curve functionals that do and
do not satisfy the hypotheses of the main theorem.
This section is logically independent of the main arguments and is not
used in the proofs of the principal results.

Definition 2.13

(Quasi-convex, convex, and additive-convex curve functionals)
.

In decreasing order of generality, we consider the following classes of
curve functionals.

(1)

Let
\operatorname{Cont}^{+}(S)
denote the space of continuous functions on
\Curr^{+}(S)
, the space of oriented geodesic currents.

(2)

For fixed
R>0
, let
\mathrm{QConvex}_{R}^{+}(S)
denote the set of curve
functionals satisfying oriented
R
-quasi-smoothing, convex union,
stability, and homogeneity. We refer to this as the space of

oriented
R
-quasi-convex functionals
. Define

\mathrm{QConvex}^{+}(S)\coloneqq\bigcup_{R>0}\mathrm{QConvex}_{R}^{+}(S),

the space of
oriented quasi-convex curve functionals
.

(3)

For
R>0
, let
\mathrm{QConvex}_{R}(S)
be the subset of

\mathrm{QConvex}_{R}^{+}(S)
consisting of symmetric curve functionals that also
satisfy
R
-quasi-smoothing in both the oriented and unoriented
settings. We call this the space of
R
-quasi-convex functionals

and define

\mathrm{QConvex}(S)\coloneqq\bigcup_{R>0}\mathrm{QConvex}_{R}(S),

the space of
quasi-convex curve functionals
.

(4)

Let

\mathrm{Convex}(S)\coloneqq\mathrm{QConvex}_{0}(S)

be the space of
convex curve functionals
.
Under the stronger smoothing assumption of multi-smoothing, such functionals induce convex
functions in train-track coordinates (see
Section
11.6
).

(5)

Let
\mathrm{AConvex}(S)
denote the space of
additive convex curve
functionals
, namely the subset of
\mathrm{Convex}(S)
consisting of
functionals that additionally satisfy additivity.
(The prefix “A” indicates additivity.)

The examples and discussion below show we have strict inclusions

\mathrm{AConvex}(S)\subsetneq\mathrm{Convex}(S)\subsetneq\mathrm{QConvex}(S)\subsetneq\mathrm{QConvex}^{+}(S)\subsetneq\operatorname{Cont}^{+}(S)

2.3.
Examples and non-examples

In previous work, we proved the following result.

Theorem 2.14
.

[
55
, Theorem A]

Let
f\in\mathrm{QConvex}^{+}(S)
. Then there is a unique continuous homogeneous function

\bar{f}\colon\Curr^{+}(S)\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
that extends
f
.

It is straightforward to verify that the properties of symmetry,
smoothing, additivity, and stability—defining the space

\mathrm{AConvex}(S)
—are satisfied by every intersection functional

i(\mu,\cdot)\colon\mathcal{C}^{+}(S)\to\mathbb{R},

where
\mu
is a geodesic current. This was established
in
[
55
, Section 4]
by first verifying the
properties for multi-curves and then extending to arbitrary currents
using the density of weighted curves together with the continuity and
bilinearity of the intersection pairing.
For completeness, we provide an alternative proof in
Appendix
C
, more closely aligned with the arguments
involving lifts to the universal cover.

Many natural notions of length satisfy these properties and are known
to extend continuously to geodesic currents as intersection
functionals. We list several such examples below, along with the
appropriate references.

2.3.1.
Examples

(1)

Intersection number with a fixed geodesic current. See
[
9
, Proposition 4.5]
.

(2)

Hyperbolic lengths
[
10
, Proposition 14]
.

(3)

More generally, length w.r.t. negatively curved Riemannian metrics
[
63
, Théorème 1]
.

(4)

Even more generally, non-positively curved Riemannian metrics

[
24
, Theorem A]
.

(5)

Lengths with respect to negatively curved Riemannian metrics with conical singularities of angle
\geq 2\pi

[
40
, Theorem A]
.

(6)

Lengths with respect to singular Euclidean metrics coming from quadratic differentials
[
27
, Lemma 9]
.

(7)

More generally, non-positively curved Euclidean cone metrics of angle
\geq 2\pi

[
4
, Proposition 3.3]
,
[
22
]
.

(8)

Word length with respect to simple generating sets
[
30
, Theorem 1.2]
.

(9)

Lengths associated to Anosov representations induced by positive generalized cross-ratios
[
57
, Proposition 2.24]
,
[
17
, Theorem 1.6]
. Specifically, Martone-Zhang
[
57
]
showed that a family of representations equipped with positive Hölder generalized
cross-ratios and termed
positively ratioed
, induce curve
functionals, via their
periods
, dual to geodesic currents. They further established that
these functionals satisfy connected smoothing
[
57
, Proposition 4.5]
.
Burger-Iozzi-Parreau-Pozzetti later extended their construction to generalized cross-ratios that need not be continuous.

Theorem
A
subsumes all of the
preceding results. This is explained in
Section
8
for items (1)–(8) and in
Section
10
for item (9). In fact, by Theorem
F
, the period of any generalized cross-ratio satisfies smoothing.

In particular, Section
8.3
shows that
item (8) admits a further generalization to stable lengths associated
to embedded graphs on the surface. Moreover,
Section
8.4
discusses how any curve length
arising from a length metric structure on
S
induces a dual geodesic
current.

2.3.2.
Non-examples

In order to illustrate the complexity of the problem,
we now show examples of many natural functions on curves that do
not
satisfy the
\mathrm{AConvex}
properties (even though many of them still extend continuously to geodesic currents).

(1)

Some curve functionals are not symmetric but nevertheless satisfy
oriented quasi-smoothing, thus providing examples in

\mathrm{QConvex}^{+}(S)
(see also item (3) below). For instance, the stable
lengths of word metrics arising from asymmetric generating sets,
as discussed in
[
55
, Section 4.6]
, furnish such
examples.

(2)

The square root of extremal length satisfies symmetry, smoothing,
stability, convex union, and homogeneity, and therefore provides an
example in
\mathrm{Convex}(S)
. However, it does not satisfy additivity
(see
[
55
, Section 4.8]
). Functionals of this type
extend to geodesic currents by
[
55
, Theorem A]
.

This example illustrates the proper containment
\mathrm{AConvex}(S)\subsetneq\mathrm{Convex}(S).

(3)

Word length with respect to an arbitrary generating set of
\pi_{1}(S)

satisfies additivity (by linear extension), but it need not satisfy
smoothing or stability (see
[
55
, Example 4.10]
,

[
28
, Theorem 1.5]
,

[
30
]
).
Moreover, its stabilized version does not necessarily satisfy smoothing
(see the same example and
[
55
, Theorem B]
).

This illustrates the proper inclusion
\mathrm{Convex}(S)\subsetneq\mathrm{QConvex}_{R}(S)
for
R>0
.

(4)

Another example is provided by stable lengths associated to
quasi-Fuchsian representations. These satisfy quasi-smoothing by

[
55
, Proposition 4.11]
, and satisfy strict
smoothing if and only if the representation is Fuchsian
(see
[
31
, Theorem A]
as well as
[
14
, Proposition 1.8]
. This furnishes another instance of the
proper inclusion
\mathrm{Convex}(S)\subsetneq\mathrm{QConvex}_{R}(S)
for
R>0
.
A quasi-Fuchsian representation
\rho\colon\pi_{1}(S)\to\PSL(2,\mathbb{C})
is a
conjugate of Fuchsian group by a (properly normalized) quasi-conformal
self-homeomorphism of the 2-sphere
f_{\rho}

[
41
, Definition 6.12.2]
. The

quasi-Fuchsian
constant
K
of
\rho
is the smallest quasi-conformal
constant for
f_{\rho}
.

Question 2.15
.

Let
\rho
be a
quasi-Fuchsian representation, and let
R>0
be the
quasi-smoothing
constant
, i.e., the minimal
R>0
so that
\ell_{\rho}\in\mathrm{QConvex}_{R}(S)
.
Does
R\to\infty
as the quasi-Fuchsian constant of
\rho
goes to
\infty
?

(5)

The previous example fits into the broader framework of length
functions arising from higher-rank representations of

\pi_{1}(S)
into a connected real semisimple algebraic group of
non-compact type
G
, such as
\SL(n,\mathbb{R})
.
In fact,
[
57
, Theorem 1.1]
show that such representations
satisfy full smoothing if and only if they are positively ratioed (see item (9) in Examples). Hence, for non-positively ratioed ones (such as quasi-Fuchsian but non-fuchsian) we get non-examples.

(6)

Translation lengths associated to biautomatic structures. Automatic structures provide regular-language normal forms for group
elements and yield efficient solutions to the word problem. Many
geometrically defined groups, including hyperbolic groups, admit such
structures. A biautomatic structure satisfies additional
fellow-travelling properties that ensure compatibility with both left
and right multiplication and imply, in particular, solvability of the
conjugacy problem (see
[
42
, Section 2.2]
).

Given a biautomatic structure
\mathcal{L}
on a surface group

\pi_{1}(S)
, one obtains an associated translation length

\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{L}}
defined via its regular language.
Hughes-Valiunas
[
42
, Proposition 4.2]
showed that
these translation lengths satisfy oriented quasi-smoothing, and used
this property to construct a hierarchically hyperbolic group that is
not biautomatic.

Since this class includes ordinary word length (cf. the previous
item), such translation lengths do not satisfy smoothing in general.

(7)

The square root of the self-intersection number satisfies stability,
homogeneity, and smoothing, but fails to satisfy additivity and even
convex union (see
[
55
, Example 1.12]
).
Thus it provides an example of a curve functional lying outside

\mathrm{QConvex}_{R}^{+}(S)
, despite extending to geodesic currents by
Bonahon’s work
[
9
]
, so it illustrates the proper containtment
\mathrm{QConvex}_{R}^{+}(S)\subsetneq\operatorname{Cont}(S)
. Another example are exotic length functions on surface groups which vanish precisely on simple closed geodesics
[
19
, Section 9]
: these extend to geodesic currents, since they satisfy the bouned-backtracking property (see
[
20
]
and
[
19
, Section 7]
. However, they cannot satisfy
C
-quasi-smoothing for any
C>0
: indeed, we can take sequences of simple elements
a_{n},b_{n}\in\pi_{1}(S)
, with
a_{n},b_{n}
crossing, so that
a_{n}b_{n}
is non-simple and
\ell(a_{n}b_{n})
goes to infinity as
n\to\infty
. This shows they violate disconnected
C
-quasi-smoothing for every
C
.

(8)

Lengths of quotients of embedded graphs
\mathcal{G}
by large subgraphs do not necessarily extend continuously to geodesic
currents
[
11
, Proposition 11]
. In particular, they
do not satisfy quasi-smoothing in general. By considering the
universal cover of such a quotient, we obtain an isometric action of

\Gamma
on a geodesic space
X
with a large normal subgroup acting
trivially. Its stable length then does not satisfy quasi-smoothing.
We now give a direct proof of this more general fact.

Proposition 2.16
.

Suppose
\Gamma=\pi_{1}(S)
acts on a geodesic space
X
by isometries, and
let
\ell
be the stable length of the action. Suppose
\ell
is
non-trivial and there is an infinite normal subgroup
N
of
\Gamma
so that
\ell(b)=0
for all
b\in N
. Then
\ell
does not satisfy quasi-smoothing.

Proof.

Let
a\in\Gamma
, with
\ell(a)>0
, and fix
b\in N
. Then
\frac{1}{2n}ba^{n}ba^{-n}
converges to
a
in the weak
∗
topology, but since
a^{n}ba^{-n}b\in N
, we have
\ell(a^{n}ba^{-n}b)=0
.
Hence,
\ell
cannot extend continuously. To see explicitly that

\ell
does not satisfy quasi-smoothing, find crossing

\overrightarrow{a}
and
\overrightarrow{b}
as above. If quasi-smoothing were satisfied, by
Proposition
4.28
,
Equation (
4.5
), there would exist a constants
R>0

and
s^{\prime}>0
so that for all
n\geq s^{\prime}
, we would have

\ell(ba^{n}ba^{-n})\geq\ell(a^{2n})-R.

But this inequality is violated, since on the one hand, the left hand side is
0
, and, on the other hand, the right hand side is positive for large enough
n
.
∎

(9)

The Lorentzian length associated to an affine action of a Fuchsian
group
[
34
]
does not satisfy quasi-smoothing.
Indeed, although it satisfies symmetry, stability, and additivity

[
33
, p. 1053]
, it takes both positive and negative
values. Proposition
2.19
below shows that this
precludes quasi-smoothing.

Nevertheless, it extends continuously to geodesic currents, as shown
in
[
33
, Section 6.1]
. Strictly speaking, that
result is stated for surfaces with boundary, but the same argument
applies to closed surfaces by
[
48
]
.

As an example of a curve functional that does not extend
continuously to geodesic currents—and hence does not satisfy
smoothing by
[
55
, Theorem A]
—we present the following.

Example 2.17
.

Let
\sqrt{EL}
denote the (square root of) extremal length.
In
[
55
, Section 4.8]
the authors proved that
\sqrt{EL}
satisfies stability, homogeneity, smoothing, and convex union. By
[
55
, Theorem A]
,
\sqrt{EL}
extends continuously to a continuous on geodesic currents.

\sqrt{EL}
does not satisfy additivity, so it is not dual to a
geodesic current. However, we can consider
\sqrt{EL}
as a function
on
connected
curves and extended it additively to a function

G
on (weighted)
multi-curves,
which is thus not equal to
\sqrt{EL}
on multi-curves.
G
cannot extend continuously to currents, since we know
G=\sqrt{EL}
on (weighted) connected curves, which are dense in the space of geodesic currents.
Thus, the purported continuous extension
\overline{G}
would have to satisfy
\overline{G}=\sqrt{EL}
on all geodesic currents, hence on all multi-curves, a contradiction.
It follows from
[
55
, Theorem A]
that
G
cannot satisfy smoothing (in fact, it cannot satisfy quasi-smoothing), since it trivially satisfies convex union (in fact, additivity) and stability.
Specifically,
G
does not satisfy connected smoothing.
Indeed, let
F=\sqrt{EL}
. For a crossing pair
(\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{b})
, we have

F(a+b)\geq F(ab).

Then, by disconnected smoothing of
F
, we have

G(a+b)=G(a)+G(b)=F(a)+F(b)\geq F(a+b)\geq F(ab)=G(ab)

where we used the fact that
F
and
G
take the same values on closed curves; hence,

G
satisfies disconnected smoothing.
This leads to the conclusion that
G
cannot satisfy connected smoothing, i.e. there must be some pair
(\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{b})
parallel (and this so that
[ab]
has a
self-crossing, by Proposition
4.10
), so that

G(ab)<G(a)+G(b)
. In fact, it follows from
[
55
, Theorem A]
that it cannot satisfy connected quasi-smoothing, i.e., this inequality does not hold either even up to additive error.

2.3.3.
Non-negativity

Observe that we do not require in the hypotheses of
Theorem
A
that the curve
functional
f\in\mathrm{AConvex}(S)
is non-negative: in fact it follows
from the other conditions.
We finish this subsection by relating the hypotheses on
f
to non-negativity. In particular, we prove that smoothing and convex union on
f
imply non-negativity.

Proposition 2.18
.

Let
f
be a curve functional satisfying convex union and smoothing. Then
f([a])\geq 0
for any
a\in\pi_{1}(S)
.

Proof.

Let
x\in\pi_{1}(S)
be so that
\overrightarrow{a}
crosses
\overrightarrow{x}
to the
right, so

[a][x]\mathrel{\searrow}[ax]
.
By Lemma
4.15
, we see that

(\overrightarrow{ax},\overrightarrow{a})
L-cross, so
(by unoriented smoothing)
[ax][a]\mathrel{\searrow}[x]
. We thus have

\displaystyle f([a])+f([x])

\displaystyle\geq f([a][x])\geq f([ax])

\displaystyle f([ax])+f([a])

\displaystyle\geq f([ax][a])\geq f([x]).

Adding the equations, we get
f([a])\geq 0
.
∎

Although we will not use it in this paper, we note that a similar
argument with different assumptions also yields positivity.

Proposition 2.19
.

Let
f
be a curve functional satisfying convex union, quasi-smoothing,
stability, and homogeneity. Then
f([a])\geq 0
for any
a\in\pi_{1}(S)
.

Proof.

Let
x\in\pi_{1}(S)
be so that
\overrightarrow{a}
crosses
\overrightarrow{x}
to the
right. As in Proposition
2.18
, we have

\displaystyle[a^{n}][x]

\displaystyle\mathrel{\searrow}[a^{n}x]

\displaystyle[a^{n}x][a^{n}]

\displaystyle\mathrel{\searrow}[x].

Since
f
satisfies quasi-smoothing (oriented and unoriented) as well as convex union, we get

\displaystyle f([a^{n}])+f([x])

\displaystyle\geq f([a^{n}][x])\geq f([a^{n}x])-R

\displaystyle f([a^{n}x])+f([a^{n}])

\displaystyle\geq f([ax][a^{n}])\geq f([x])-R.

Adding the equations, we get
f([a^{n}])\geq-2R
. By stability and
homogeneity, this yields
nf([a])\geq-2R
, and taking
n
large
enough we see that
f([a])\geq 0
.
∎

Remark 2.20
.

The use of both the oriented and the unoriented smoothing hypothesis
is essential in both of the above results. In fact, there exist
natural examples of functions that satisfy the oriented
quasi-smoothing condition (but not the unoriented one), as well as all
the other necessary hypotheses mentioned above, yet still take
negative values. For instance, curve functionals arising from
homogeneous quasi-morphisms have this property; see
Section
11.3
.

3.
Background

3.1.
General measure theory

To ultimately construct the geodesic current in Theorem
A
, we review some measure theory.
Let
X
be a set.

Definition 3.1

(Semi-ring, ring,
\sigma
-algebra)
.

A family
\mathcal{S}
of subsets of a set
X
is called a

semiring
(of sets) if it contains the empty set and, for
every
A,B\in\mathcal{S}
, we have

A\cap B\in\mathcal{S}
and if
A\subset B
the difference

B\backslash A
is a disjoint union of finitely many sets
in
\mathcal{S}
. A family
\mathcal{R}
of subsets of a set
X
is
called a
ring
if it contains the empty set and for all

A,B\in\mathcal{R}
, the sets
A\cap B
,
A\cup B
and

A\backslash B
belong to
\mathcal{R}
. A ring
\mathcal{R}
is an
algebra
if
X\in\mathcal{R}
. An algebra
\mathcal{A}
is called a
\sigma
-algebra
if for any sequence of sets
A_{n}
in
\mathcal{A}
one has
\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}A_{n}\in\mathcal{A}
.
If
\mathcal{F}
is a family of subsets of
X
, let
\sigma(\mathcal{F})
, the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
\mathcal{F}
, be the smallest
\sigma
-algebra containing
\mathcal{F}
.

Lemma 3.2
.

For any semiring
\mathcal{S}
, the collections of all finite unions
of sets in
\mathcal{S}
forms a ring
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S}}
,
the
ring generated by
\mathcal{S}
. Every set in

\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S}}
is a finite union of pairwise disjoint
sets in
\mathcal{S}
.

Proof.

See
[
8
, 1.2.14 Lemma]
.
∎

Definition 3.3
.

A real-valued set function
\mu
defined on a ring
\mathcal{R}
is called
finitely additive
, resp.
countably sub-additive
, if

\mu\Bigl(\bigcup\nolimits_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}\Bigr)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mu(A_{i}),

resp.

\mu\Bigl(\bigcup\nolimits_{i=1}^{\infty}A_{i}\Bigr)\leq\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\mu(A_{i}),

for all pairwise
disjoint sets
A_{i}\in\mathcal{R}

such that
\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}\in\mathcal{R}
(resp.
\bigcup_{i=1}^{\infty}A_{i}\in\mathcal{R}
).

We say
\mu
is
countably additive
if the inequality in countable sub-additivity is
replaced by an equality.

Proposition 3.4

(
[
8
, 1.3.9 Proposition]
)
.

Let
\mu
be a non-negative finitely additive set function on a ring
\mathcal{R}
.
If
\mu
is countably sub-additive then
\mu
is countably additive.

Proposition 3.5

(
[
8
, 1.3.10 Proposition]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a semiring and
\mu:\mathcal{S}\to[0,\infty]
a finitely additive function. Then there exists a unique finitely additive extension

\hat{\mu}:\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S}}\to[0,\infty]
. If, in addition,
\mu
is countably subadditive on
\mathcal{S}
, then
\hat{\mu}
is countably additive on
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S}}
.

Finally, a finitely and countably additive function on a ring can be extended to the
\sigma
-algebra.
Recall that a measure
\mu
on an algebra
\mathcal{A}
is called
\sigma
-finite
if there exists a sequence of
X_{n}\in\mathcal{A}
with
\mu(X_{n})<\infty
and
X=\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}X_{n}
.

Theorem 3.6

(Carathéodory extension theorem,
[
8
, 1.11.8 Theorem]
)
.

Given a ring
\mathcal{R}
on a set
X
and an additive and countably additive non-negative function
\mu
on
\mathcal{R}
.
Then
\mu
extends to a measure on the
\sigma
-algebra

\sigma(\mathcal{R})
generated by
\mathcal{R}
. If, moreover,
\mu

is
\sigma
-finite, this extension is unique.

For any non-negative set function
\mu
defined on a certain class
\mathcal{A}
of subsets of
X
containing
X
, the formula

\mu^{*}(A)=\inf\left\{\sum_{n=1}\mu(A_{n})\mid A_{n}\in\mathcal{A},A\subset\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}A_{n}\right\}

defines a new set function on
every

A\subset X
, called the
outer measure
of
\mu
.

Let
\mu
a finitely additive and countable additive function on a ring
\mathcal{R}
. A set
A\subset X
is called
\mu
-measurable
of for every
E\subset X
one has

\mu^{*}(E\cap A)+\mu^{*}(E\backslash A)=\mu^{*}(E).

Definition 3.7

(Atom of a measure
[
8
, Definition 1.12.7]
)
.

Given a topological space
X
, a nonnegative measure
\mu
and
A\subset X
a measurable subset, we say
A
is an
atom
of
\mu
if the following two conditions are satisfied:

(1)

\mu(A)>0

(2)

For any measurable set
B
properly contained in
A
,
\mu(B)=0
.

In the following definitions, let
X
be a topological space.

Definition 3.8

(Borel
\sigma
-algebra, Borel measure)
.

The
Borel
\sigma
-algebra
is the smallest
\sigma
-algebra containing all open sets in
X
. A
Borel measure
is a measure on
\mathcal{B}(X)
.

Definition 3.9

(Support of a Borel measure)
.

Let
\mu
be a Borel measure on
X
. The
support
of
\mu
is defined as the set of all points
x\in X
for which every open neighbourhood
N_{x}
of
x
has positive measure. We will denote it
\supp(\mu)
.

If
X
is a separable metric space, and
\mu
is a Borel measure, then every atom of
\mu
is singleton union a
\mu
-measurable set of measure zero
[
44
, 2.1.6, Theorem 2]
.

3.2.
Radon and Borel measures

Definition 3.10
.

Let
X
be a locally compact Hausdorff space. A Borel measure
\mu
on
X
is
locally finite
if every point has a neighborhood
U
with
\mu(U)<\infty
.
The measure
\mu
is
inner regular
if, for every open set
U
,

\mu(U)=\sup\{\mu(K):K\subset U,\ \text{$K$ compact}\}.

We say that
\mu
is
outer regular
if, for every Borel set
B
,

\mu(B)=\inf\{\mu(U):B\subset U,\ \text{$U$ open}\}.

The measure
\mu
is
Radon
if it is locally finite, inner regular, and outer regular.

Definition 3.11
.

The
space of measures
\mathcal{M}(X)
on a topological
space
X
is the set of all Radon measures on
X
.

In a general topological space
X
, not all locally-finite Borel
measures are Radon. However, in a locally compact Hausdorff space with
a countable basis, locally-finite Borel measures and Radon measures
are the same
[
5
, Theorem 29.12]
.

We define the following topology in the space of measures.

Definition 3.12
.

The weak
∗
-topology on
\mathcal{M}(X)
is the weakest topology with respect to which the map

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}(X)

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle\mu

\displaystyle\mapsto\int\psi\mu

is continuous for all
\psi\in C_{c}(X)
.

We suppose that
\mathcal{M}(X)
is endowed with the weak
∗
-topology.

3.3.
Geodesic currents

We recall the definition of geodesic current. For a more complete
treatement, we refer the reader
to
[
29
, Chapter 3]
.

Definition 3.13

(Boundary at infinity)
.

Endow
S
with a complete
hyperbolic metric
g
; we denote the pair
(S,g)
by
\Sigma
. Then
we can consider the metric
universal covering
p\colon\widetilde{\Sigma}\to\Sigma
, with

\widetilde{\Sigma}
isometric to the hyperbolic plane.
Two quasi-geodesic rays
c,c^{\prime}\colon[0,\infty)\to\widetilde{\Sigma}
are
said to be
asymptotic
if the Hausdorff distance between their images is finite.
We define

\partial_{\infty}S
, the
boundary at infinity

of
S
, to be the set of equivalence classes of
asymptotic quasi-geodesic rays. This boundary at infinity, which is homeomorphic to
S^{1}
, is
independent of the hyperbolic structure on
S
up to canonical
homeomorphism.

Definition 3.14

(Space of oriented geodesics)
.

Let
G(\Sigma)
denote the
space of oriented geodesics
in

\widetilde{\Sigma}
, i.e.,

G(\Sigma)\coloneqq\partial_{\infty}\Sigma\times\partial_{\infty}\Sigma-\Delta.

Since this is independent of the hyperbolic structure, we will also
write
G(S)
.

There is a natural involution map
\sigma\colon G(S)\to G(S)
, defined by
(a,b)\mapsto(b,a)
, i.e., reversing the orientation of the geodesic. We will sometimes refer to
\sigma
as the
flip map
.

Definition 3.15
.

We define
\Curr^{+}(S)
, the space of
oriented geodesic currents
on
S
,
to be the
space of
\pi_{1}(S)
-invariant, Radon measures on

G(S)
.
We define the space of

geodesic currents
as the space of flip-invariant oriented
geodesic currents on
S
, and denote it by
\Curr(S)
. We endow both
spaces with their natural subspace topologies induced as subsets of

\mathcal{M}(G(S))
.

Remark 3.16
.

Some authors define the space of geodesic currents
\Curr^{u}(S)
as invariant Radon measures on the space of
unoriented geodesics of
\widetilde{\Sigma}
, i.e.,

G^{u}(\Sigma)\coloneqq G(\Sigma)/\mathbb{Z}_{2}

where the action of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is by the orientation reversal involution
\sigma
.
There is a natural homeomorphism (see
[
29
, Exercise 3.1]
)

\Curr^{u}(S)\leftrightarrow\Curr(S).

As a consequence of the discussion in
Section
3.2
, we can replace “Radon” with
“locally-finite Borel” in the above definitions.
The space of geodesic currents enjoys the following nice topological properties.

Proposition 3.17

(
[
29
, Theorem 3.3]
)
.

\Curr^{+}(S)
and
\Curr(S)
are locally compact, metrizable, second-countable, and Hausdorff spaces.

The
\pi_{1}(S)
-invariance of geodesic currents highly constrains their atoms.

Proposition 3.18

(
[
54
, Lemma 8.2.8]
)
.

For
l\in G(S)
, the orbit
\pi_{1}(S)\cdot l
is
discrete in
G(S)
if and only if its projection
p(l)
to
S
is a closed
geodesic.

For
g\in\pi_{1}(S)
, let
\overrightarrow{g}
denote the
hyperbolic axis
of
g
, i.e., the oriented geodesic left invariant by
g
along which
g
translates the least hyperbolic distance (see also Section
4.1
).

For
\gamma
a concrete curve with lift geodesic representative
\overrightarrow{\gamma}
to the
universal cover,
since the orbit
\pi_{1}(S)\cdot\overrightarrow{\gamma}
is discrete and countable, one can define a natural geodesic current associated to the oriented closed curve
C=[\gamma]=[g]
by

(3.1)

\delta_{C}=\sum_{g\in\pi_{1}(S)/\operatorname{Stab}(\overrightarrow{\gamma})}\delta_{g\cdot\overrightarrow{\gamma}},

where
\operatorname{Stab}(l)
denotes the stabilizer of the oriented
geodesic
l
, and
\delta_{l}
denotes the Dirac measure supported at
the oriented geodesic
l\in G(S)
. This definition extends naturally to associate a geodesic current to any weighted curve and, by linearity, to any weighted multi-curve. Moreover, geodesic currents arising from weighted multi-curves (and also those from weighted curves) are dense in the space of geodesic currents
[
9
, Proposition 4.4]
.

It is easy to see that a geodesic
l\in G(S)
can only be an atom of a geodesic current when it is the lift of a closed geodesic
[
54
, Proposition 8.2.7]
.

One can also look at 1-dimensional sets.
For
x\in\partial_{\infty}\Sigma
, its

pencil

P(x)
is the subset of
G(S)
of geodesics with either
endpoint at
x
.
The only
0
-dimensional and
1
-dimensional subsets
that may have non-zero measure are those containing lifts of closed
geodesics.

Proposition 3.19
.

Let
\mu
be a geodesic current. If, for some
x
,
\mu(P(x))\neq 0
, then
P(x)
contains a unique lift
\overrightarrow{\gamma}
of a closed
geodesic and
\mu
has an atom at
\overrightarrow{\gamma}
.

Proof.

For existence of
\overrightarrow{\gamma}
, see, e.g.,

[
26
, Lemma 2.6]
.
For uniqueness, see
[
54
, Corollary 4.2.3]
.
∎

Given
p,q\in\widetilde{S}
we will write
G[p,q)\subset G(S)
for the set of
geodesics intersecting the geodesic segment
[p,q)\subset\widetilde{S}
transversally.

Definition 3.20

(Intersection form of geodesic currents)
.

Let
\mathcal{D}\mathcal{G}(S)\subset\{(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})\mid\gamma_{1}\cap\gamma_{2}=\{\mbox{pt}\}\}
be the set of pairs of oriented geodesics of
\widetilde{S}
intersecting transversely.
There is a continuous equivariant map
(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})\mapsto\gamma_{1}\cap\gamma_{2}
from
\mathcal{D}\mathcal{G}(S)
to
\widetilde{S}
,
so the action of
\pi_{1}(S)
on
\mathcal{D}\mathcal{G}(S)
is properly discontinuous and free. Let

\mathcal{F}
be a fundamental domain of this action. Following
Bonahon
[
9
]
, for any two oriented
currents
\mu,\nu
we define

(3.2)

i(\mu,\nu)\coloneqq\int_{\mathcal{F}}\mu\times\nu.

Bonahon defined the same intersection number in the context of unoriented geodesic currents, but the definition is the same for oriented ones. Moreover, this intersection number remains unchanged upon flipping the orientation of any of its factors.
It enjoys all the other properties of Bonahon’s intersection: it is continuous and extends the geometric intersection of closed curves.

If
\nu=\delta_{C}
, then we will write
i(\mu,C)
for
i(\mu,\delta_{C})
.
In this case, we have

(3.3)

i(\mu,\delta_{C})=\mu(G[q,y\cdot q))

where
y\in\pi_{1}(S)
is an element representing
C
and
q
is an
arbitrary point in

\overrightarrow{y}
.
In the setting of unoriented geodesic currents, Otal

[
63
, Théorème 2]
shows that two
geodesic currents are completely determined by their intersection
numbers with closed curves.
This is no longer true for general oriented geodesic currents; for example,
any oriented geodesic current
\mu
and its orientation flip
\sigma(\mu)
have the same intersection numbers.
See also Definition
6.7
and Appendix
B
for discussions about a version of intersection for oriented closed curves.

Finally, we highlight a special subset of geodesic currents that can be regarded as the interior of the cone of geodesic currents.

Definition 3.21
.

Let
\mu\in\Curr(S)
. We say
\mu
is
filling
, and write

\mu\in\Curr_{\mathrm{fill}}(S)
, if for every non-zero geodesic
current
\nu\in\Curr(S)
, we have
i(\mu,\nu)>0
.

3.4.
Convex functions on the integers

We will need some results guaranteeing the existence of limits of sequences.
We recall some basic results on convexity and existence of limits of
functions on the integers, starting with an old result and then
generalizing to two variables and to less-standard notions of
convexity. We also recall Fekete’s Lemma.

Let
\lim_{n^{+}}f(n)
denote
\lim_{n\to+\infty}f(n)
.

We say
g\colon\mathbb{\mathbb{Z}}\to\mathbb{R}
is
convex
if
2g(n)\leq g(n+1)+g(n-1)
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Similarly,
g\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}
is convex if
2g(n)\leq g(n+1)+g(n-1)
for all
n\geq 2
.

Lemma 3.22
.

Let
g\colon\mathbb{\mathbb{Z}}\to\mathbb{R}
be a convex and bounded function.
Then
\lim_{n^{\pm}}g(n)
exists.

Proof.

Consider
h(n)\coloneqq g(n+1)-g(n)
. Convexity of
g
implies
that
h
is monotone increasing. Thus if
h(n_{0})>0
,
then
g(n)
increases monotonically without bound for
n>n_{0}
,
contradicting boundedness. Therefore
h(n)\leq 0
for all
n
, so
g
is
monotonically decreasing, so has a limit.
∎

Lemma 3.23
.

Let
(a_{n,m})_{n,m\in\mathbb{N}}
be a double sequence that is
bounded and monotone, in the sense that if
n^{\prime}\leq n
and

m^{\prime}\leq m
then

a_{n^{\prime},m^{\prime}}\leq a_{n,m}
. Then the following limits
exist and are equal:

\lim_{n^{+}}\lim_{m^{+}}a_{n,m}=\lim_{m^{+}}\lim_{n^{+}}a_{n,m}=\lim_{n^{+},m^{+}}a_{n,m}=\sup_{n,m\geq 0}a_{n,m}.

This lemma appears in Habil
[
38
, Thm. 4.2]
, but we
include a proof for reference.

Proof.

Let
V=\sup_{n,m}a_{n,m}
. For any
\varepsilon>0
, let

A_{\varepsilon}\coloneqq\{(n,m)\mid a_{n,m}>V-\varepsilon\}
. By
definition of
\sup
, this set is non-empty; say
(n_{0},m_{0})\in A_{\varepsilon}
. By monotonicity,
A_{\varepsilon}
contains all
(n,m)

with
n\geq n_{0},m\geq m_{0}
. Since
\varepsilon
is arbitrary, it
follows that
\lim_{n^{+},m^{+}}a_{n,m}=V
. It also follows that
for
n\geq n_{0}
,
\lim_{m^{+}}a_{n,m}>V-\varepsilon
. (This limit
exists by single-variable monotonicity.) Again we see that the
double limits exist and are equal to
V
.
∎

Definition 3.24
.

A function
g\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}
is
R
-convex

if for all
r\geq R
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
for which the inequality makes
sense, we have
g(n-r)+g(n+r)\geq 2g(n)
. Similarly a
function
g\colon\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{R}
is

(R,S)
-axis-convex
if, for all
n,m
,
g(\cdot,m)
is

R
-convex and
g(n,\cdot)
is
S
-convex. We say that
g
is

axis-convex
if it is
(1,1)
-axis-convex.

Lemma 3.25
.

Let
g\colon\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}
be axis-convex and bounded. Then the following limits exist and are equal:

\lim_{n^{+},m^{+}}g(n,m)=\lim_{n^{+}}\lim_{m^{+}}g(n,m)=\lim_{m^{+}}\lim_{n^{+}}g(n,m).

Proof.

As in Lemma
3.22
, the function
h_{1}(n,m)=g(n+1,m)-g(n,m)
is increasing as a function of
n
by convexity
and hence non-positive by boundedness of
g
. Similarly for

h_{2}(n,m)=g(n,m+1)-g(n,m)
. Thus
g
is monotonically decreasing
and the result follows by Lemma
3.23
.
∎

Lemma 3.26
.

Let
g
be a bounded,
R
-convex function

g\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}
. Then
\lim_{n^{+}}g(n)
exists.

Proof.

For
0\leq k<R
, define
g_{k}(n)\coloneqq g(k+nR)
. Then each

g_{k}
is convex and bounded, and thus by
Lemma
3.22
has a well-defined limit as

n\to\infty
. We must show these limits agree for different
k
.
By
R
-convexity,

g(k+nR)\leq\frac{1}{2}\bigl(g((k-1)+(n-1)R)+g((k+1)+(n+1)R\bigr)
. Taking the limit, we see that

\lim_{n^{+}}g_{k}\leq\frac{1}{2}(\lim_{n^{+}}g_{k-1}+\lim_{n^{+}}g_{k+1})
. The limits are therefore a convex function on
\mathbb{Z}/R\mathbb{Z}
and
thus constant.
∎

Lemma 3.27
.

Let
g\colon\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}
be a
bounded,
(R,S)
-axis-convex function. Then the
following limits exist and are equal:

\lim_{n^{+},m^{+}}g(n,m)=\lim_{n^{+}}\lim_{m^{+}}g(n,m)=\lim_{m^{+}}\lim_{n^{+}}g(n,m).

Proof.

As in Lemma
3.26
, for
0\leq k<R
and
0\leq\ell<R
, let
g_{k,\ell}(n,m)\coloneqq g(k+nR,\ell+mS)
.
Then each
g_{k,\ell}
is axis-convex and thus has a well-defined limit
as
n,m\to\infty
(agreeing with the iterated limits), while the
same averaging argument shows that the limits don’t depend on

k,\ell
and thus the overall limits exist and are equal.
∎

The following result is straightforward.

Lemma 3.28
.

Let
f_{m}\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}
be a sequence of convex functions converging to a function

f\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}
. Then
f
is also convex.

Remark 3.29
.

We use the term
axis-convex
because the condition is not
strong enough to deserve the term convex. For instance, the
function
g(n,m)=-mn
is axis-convex by the above definition, but

g(n+1,m+1)+g(n-1,m-1)-2g(n,m)=-2<0
.

Finally, we will use the following classical lemma on sub-additive sequences.

Lemma 3.30

(Fekete’s Lemma)
.

Let
(a_{n})
be a sequence of real numbers such that, for all
m,n\geq 0
,

a_{m+n}\leq a_{m}+a_{n}\qquad\text{(respectively, }a_{m+n}\geq a_{m}+a_{n}\text{).}

Then

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{a_{n}}{n}=\inf_{n}\frac{a_{n}}{n}\leq a_{1}\qquad\text{(respectively, }\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{a_{n}}{n}=\sup_{n}\frac{a_{n}}{n}\geq a_{1}\text{).}

4.
Crossings and smoothings

4.1.
Left vs right actions of
\pi_{1}(S)

To fix conventions, we briefly recall elementary facts about actions of
\pi_{1}(S,\ast)
on the universal cover
\widetilde{S}
of a surface (or indeed any connected length space). We
follow the convention that path composition works in the natural
order, where a product
\gamma_{1}\cdot\gamma_{2}
of paths in
S
is
defined if
\gamma_{1}(1)=\gamma_{2}(0)
.
Recall that
a\in\pi_{1}(S,\ast)
is the homotopy class of the
oriented curve relative to the basepoint
*
, which we will write
a=(\gamma)
.
We will sometimes follow the
group theory convention of denoting inverses by a capital letter.

Definition 4.1

(Right action)
.

For a covering map
p\colon\widetilde{S}\to S
of
S
and base-point

\ast\in S
, we have a
right
action of
\pi_{1}(S,\ast)
on
the fiber
p^{-1}(\ast)
, defined by, for
x\in p^{-1}(\ast)
, setting

x\cdot(\gamma)=\widetilde{\gamma}(1)
, where
\widetilde{\gamma}
is the
unique lift of
\gamma
with
\widetilde{\gamma}(0)=x
.

We observe
the following.

•

This right action does not depend on a choice of basepoint
on
\widetilde{S}
, and works for any covering space.
1
1

1

One succinct statement of the classification theorem for
covering spaces is that coverings of
S
are in bijection with
sets with a right action of
\pi_{1}(S)
. If we restrict to
connected covering spaces, they
correspond to transitive actions of
\pi_{1}(S)
, which
in turn correspond to conjugacy classes of subgroups. Thus this
right action is just the classification of covering spaces.

•

In the induced length metric on
\widetilde{S}
, the distance between

x
and
x\cdot(\gamma)
is bounded by the length of
\gamma
.

Definition 4.2

(Left action)
.

When
\widetilde{S}
is the universal cover of
S
(or, more generally, any
normal cover of
S
), there is a
left
action of
\pi_{1}(S,\ast)

on all of
\widetilde{S}
by deck transformations. Take
the standard model of
\widetilde{S}
, and lift
\ast\in S
to a basepoint

\widetilde{\ast}\in\widetilde{S}
, which we think about as the constant path. We
write other
points in
\widetilde{S}
as
\widetilde{\ast}\cdot(\gamma)
, where
\gamma
is a
path in
S
with
\gamma(0)=\ast
, considered up to homotopy fixing
the endpoint. The left action is then
defined by

(\gamma_{1})\cdot(\widetilde{\ast}\cdot(\gamma))\coloneqq\widetilde{\ast}\cdot(\gamma_{1}\cdot\gamma)
.

Here we make the following observations.

•

This left action is an isometry of all of
\widetilde{S}
.

•

The left action depends on a lift of

\ast\in S
to a basepoint
\widetilde{\ast}\in\widetilde{S}
.

•

The distance between
x\in\widetilde{S}
and
(\gamma)\cdot x

is unbounded, although since the basement
\widetilde{\ast}
is moved by the
length of
\gamma
we do have a bound on
the translation length.

•

This left action extends to an action on the circle at infinity
\partial_{\infty}S
.

For any non-trivial
g\in\pi_{1}(S,*)
, there is a corresponding
oriented closed curve
[g]
on
S
, corresponding to the conjugacy
class of
g
. We also need to specify different lifts of
[g]
to

\widetilde{S}
. This is conveniently specified by
g
itself:
g
determines
a sequence of points
\{\widetilde{\ast}\cdot g^{k}\mid k\in\mathbb{Z}\}
,
which is a
quasi-geodesic in
\widetilde{S}
. After choosing a hyperbolic structure

\Sigma
on
S
, this quasi-geodesic determines an oriented hyperbolic
geodesic
denoted
\overrightarrow{g}
with endpoints
g^{+}=\lim_{k^{+}}\widetilde{\ast}\cdot g^{k}

and
g^{-}=\lim_{k^{-}}\widetilde{\ast}\cdot g^{k}
. Note that
g

acts on the left on
\widetilde{S}
as a hyperbolic isometry
fixing
\overrightarrow{g}
set-wise. This action
extends continuously to
\overline{\widetilde{S}}\coloneqq\widetilde{S}\cup\partial_{\infty}S
and has north-south (NS) dynamics, with unique
attracting point
g^{+}
and repelling point
g^{-}
: for any point

x\in\overline{\widetilde{S}}
other
than
g^{-}
, we have
\lim_{k^{+}}g^{k}\cdot x=g^{+}
.

The other lifts of
[g]
to
\widetilde{S}
are translates
q\cdot\overrightarrow{g}

of
\overrightarrow{g}
by a
group element
q
; we can either think of this as the geodesic

\overrightarrow{qgQ}
passing within a bounded distance of the points
(qg^{k}Q)\cdot\widetilde{\ast}
;
or we can think of it as the geodesic corresponding to the coset

q\langle g\rangle
, i.e., passing within a bounded distance of the
points
(qg^{k})\cdot\widetilde{\ast}
in the coset. Note these two sequences of
points are within a
bounded distance of each other, since the right action of
\pi_{1}

moves points a bounded distance.

We will frequently draw pictures of group elements
g\in\pi_{1}(S,\ast)

acting on
\mathbb{H}^{2}
. In these pictures, there will be two types of
arrows (see, e.g., Figure
5.1
): arrows
connecting two endpoints of
\partial_{\infty}S
, which
correspond to axes hyperbolic axes
\overrightarrow{g}
of an element

g\in\pi_{1}(S)
, the axis of the
left
action; and arrows
representing paths connecting points on these axes that correspond to
the
right
action on the fiber. As explained above, the
relevant lifts of the basepoints are a quasi-geodesic that are a
bounded distance from the axis; the pictures are clearer if we imagine
the basepoints are actually on the axis.

Figure 4.1
.
Two different translates of
\overrightarrow{g}
captured by the double coset
\langle g\rangle q_{1}^{-1}q_{2}\langle g\rangle.

.

With these preliminaries, we can see that two different lifts of

[g]
(see Figure
4.1
)

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\ell_{1}}

\displaystyle=q_{1}\cdot\overrightarrow{g}

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\ell_{2}}

\displaystyle=q_{2}\cdot\overrightarrow{g},

are related by the isometry
q_{2}q_{1}^{-1}
,

(4.1)

\overrightarrow{\ell_{2}}=q_{2}q_{1}^{-1}\cdot\overrightarrow{\ell_{1}},

while the geometric relation between the two lifts, meaning up to the left

\pi_{1}(S,*)
action, is captured instead by the double coset

(4.2)

\langle g\rangle q_{1}^{-1}q_{2}\langle g\rangle.

Note that the order of the product is reversed relative to
(
4.1
). This double coset is what is relevant for,
e.g., determining if
\overrightarrow{\ell_{1}}
and
\overrightarrow{\ell_{2}}
intersect.

We can express many of our constructions as working with either
elements of
\pi_{1}(S)
(acting by isometries on the universal cover)
or with general hyperbolic elements of the group
\Isom^{+}(\mathbb{H}^{2})
.
(Every element of
\pi_{1}(S)
necessarily acts hyperbolically on

\widetilde{S}
.) When there is a choice, we prefer to state lemmas in terms
of
\Isom^{+}(\mathbb{H}^{2})
, since in principle it is more general, as we do
not need to assume the group elements generate a discrete subgroup.

4.2.
Crossings and smoothings

Loosely speaking, an essential crossing is a crossing of a
multi-curve that can’t be homotoped away. Recall that a multi-curve is
considered up to homotopy (Definition
2.1
).

Definition 4.3
.

A
crossing
of a concrete multi-curve
\gamma
is a pair

(p,q)\in X(\gamma)^{2}
so that

\gamma(p)=\gamma(q)\in S
. We say
\gamma
is
minimal
if
it has only transverse crossings and a minimum number of
them among all representatives of
C=[\gamma]
.
A crossing that appears in some minimal representative is said
to be an
essential crossing
.

Given an essential crossing of a multi-curve, we can do an operation
of “smoothing” in two different ways, each reducing the original
multi-curve to a different one.
We follow
[
56
]
closely.

Definition 4.4

(smoothings)
.

Let
(p,q)\in X(\gamma)^{2}
be an essential crossing of
\gamma

on
S
. To make a
smoothing

\gamma^{\prime}
of
(p,q)
, cut

X(\gamma)
at
p
and
q
and reglue the resulting four endpoints
in one of the two other possible ways, getting a new
1
-manifold

X(\gamma^{\prime})
. The map
\gamma^{\prime}
agrees with
\gamma
; this is
well-defined since
\gamma(p)=\gamma(q)
. In pictures we will
homotop
\gamma^{\prime}
slightly to round out the resulting
corners, and write
\gamma\mathrel{\searrow}\gamma^{\prime}
.

It turns out that “some” in the definition of essential crossing can be replaced by “every”: if there is a crossing
x
in some minimal representative
\gamma
of
C
, then for any other minimal representative
\gamma^{\prime}
there is a corresponding crossing
x^{\prime}
yielding the same smoothings.

Lemma 4.5

(
[
56
, Lemma 2]
)
.

If
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
are homotopic concrete
multi-curves, with
\gamma
minimal and
(p,q)\in X(\gamma)^{2}
an (essential)
crossing of
\gamma
,
then there is a crossing
(p^{\prime},q^{\prime})\in X(\gamma^{\prime})^{2}
so
that the smoothings of
(p,q)
and of
(p^{\prime},q^{\prime})
are homotopic.

As a result of Lemma
4.5
, we can speak of
essential crossings for concrete curves
\gamma
that are not minimal:
a crossing is essential if its two smoothings also arise from a
crossing in some minimal diagram.

We distinguish between two types of smoothing.

Definition 4.6
.

If
\gamma
is oriented, the
oriented smoothing
