is the smoothing that respects the orientation on
X(\gamma)
:

\vbox{\hbox{$\includegraphics[]{curves-3}$}}\mathrel{\searrow}\vbox{\hbox{$\includegraphics[]{curves-4}$}}.

One may also perform the smoothing that does not respect the orientation; we refer to this as the
unoriented smoothing
:

\vbox{\hbox{$\includegraphics[]{curves-3}$}}\mathrel{\searrow}\vbox{\hbox{$\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{\includegraphics[]{curves-4}}$}}.

In this case, the resulting curve does not carry a preferred orientation. Accordingly, unoriented smoothing will only be used in the context of symmetric curve functionals, for which all choices of orientation yield the same value.
In addition, we call a smoothing
connected
or
disconnected
if the
original curve is connected or disconnected.

We thus have four types
of smoothings between non-trivial pairs of elements which are not
common powers. We use group notation that we will expand on and justify
in the next section.

•

Oriented connected smoothing:
As shown in
Figure
4.2a
, parallel oriented geodesics

\overrightarrow{a}
and
\overrightarrow{b}
(on the bottom) give a self-crossing oriented
curve
[ab]
, with
[ab]\mathrel{\searrow}[a][b]
. (The result is
disconnected.)

•

Unoriented connected smoothing:

As shown in
Figure
4.2b
, parallel oriented geodesics

\overrightarrow{a}
and
\overrightarrow{b}
(from the previous case) give a
self-crossing oriented curve
[ab]
with

[ab]\mathrel{\searrow}[aB]
(The result is
connected.)

•

Oriented disconnected smoothing:
As shown in
Figure
4.2c

crossing geodesics
\overrightarrow{a}
and
\overrightarrow{b}
give a crossing
multi-curve
[a][b]
, with
[a][b]\mathrel{\searrow}[ab]
. (The result is connected.)

•

Unoriented disconnected smoothing:
As shown in
Figure
4.2d
, crossing geodesics

\overrightarrow{a}
and
\overrightarrow{b}
give a crossing multi-curve

[a][b]
, with
[a][b]\mathrel{\searrow}[aB]
. (The result is again connected.)

Finally, there is a special case of oriented connected smoothing,
self-crossings of a non-primitive element, that will need to be treated
specially:

•

Power smoothing:

[a^{n+m}]\geq[a^{m}][a^{n}]
for every
a\in\pi_{1}(S)
and every

m,n\geq 1
, as shown in Figure
4.2e
for
m=1,n=2
.

(a)

Oriented connected smoothing:
[ab]\mathrel{\searrow}[a][b]

(b)

Unoriented connected smoothing:
[ab]\mathrel{\searrow}[aB]
.

(c)

Oriented disconnected smoothing:
[a][b]\mathrel{\searrow}[ab]

(d)

Unoriented disconnected smoothing:
[a][b]\mathrel{\searrow}[aB]

(e)

Power smoothing:
[a^{m+n}]\mathrel{\searrow}[a^{m}][a^{n}]
.

Figure 4.2
.
Cases of smoothing.

4.3.
Triality of crossings: topological, geometric and algebraic

Following
[
56
]
, we present a group-theoretic point of view for essential crossings. For
a,b\in\pi_{1}(S,*)
, say that a
factorization
[ab]

reduces
if some (equiv. every) minimal
representative of
[ab]
has a crossing so that one smoothing gives

[a][b]
and the other smoothing gives
[aB]
. Similarly say that

[a][b]

reduces
if some/every minimal representative has a crossing
whose smoothings give
[ab]
and
[aB]
. (This is an abuse of
notation, since the reduction is not invariant under individual
conjugation of
a
and
b
.)

For
a,b\in\pi_{1}(S,*)
, let
a*b
be a 4-valent graph mapped into

S
obtained by taking the union of the paths
a,b
joined at the
basepoint
*
, and let
[a*b]

be its free homotopy class.
Free homotopy means in particular invariance under simultaneous
conjugation:
[a*b]=[caC*cbC]
. A
minimal
representative of
[a*b]

is one with a minimum number of transverse crossings.

Definition 4.7
.

Depending on the (counterclockwise) cyclic order of the endpoints,
we say that a pair
(\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{b})
of geodesics are

•

R-parallel
if they appear in order
a^{+},a^{-},b^{-},b^{+}

(below left);

•

R-anti-parallel
if they appear in order

a^{+},a^{-},b^{+},b^{-}
(below middle) and

•

R-cross
or
\overrightarrow{a}
crosses
\overrightarrow{b}
to the right
if they appear in
order
a^{+},b^{+},a^{-},b^{-}
(below right).

\underset{\text{\normalsize R-parallel}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\includegraphics[]{curves-14}$}}}\qquad\qquad\underset{\text{\normalsize R-anti-parallel}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\includegraphics[]{curves-15}$}}}\qquad\qquad\underset{\text{\normalsize R-cross}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\includegraphics[]{curves-13}$}}}

In each case we say that they are
L-parallel/L-anti-parallel/L-cross if they appear in the reverse
cyclic order, and are parallel/anti-parallel/cross if they appear in
either the above order or its reverse.

We will furthermore say a triple
(\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{b},\overrightarrow{c})
is
R-parallel if both
(\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{b})
and

(\overrightarrow{b},\overrightarrow{c})
are R-parallel, and
similarly for L-parallel and for larger tuples. The other relations
are not transitive so we will not use the extended notation.

Definition 4.8
.

Non-trivial group elements
a,b\in\pi_{1}
are
common powers

if there are non-zero
k,\ell
so that
a^{k}=b^{\ell}
. Since
\pi_{1}

has no torsion, we may assume that
k,\ell
are relatively prime and
then we see this is equivalent to the existence of a
c
so that

a=c^{\ell}
and
b=c^{k}
.

The following justifies the power smoothing
hypothesis (
2.11
).

Proposition 4.9

(
[
56
, Proposition 2.9a]
)
.

If
a=c^{k}
and
b=c^{\ell}
are common powers of a primitive
element
c
with
k,\ell\geq 1
, then the factorization
[ab]

reduces to
[a][b]
.

The following result justifies the oriented/unoriented connected/disconnected smoothing variations of smoothing.
It is an oriented version of the result for smoothing in
[
56
, Proposition 2.9b]
, and its proof goes through verbatim.

Proposition 4.10
.

Let
a,b\in\pi_{1}(S,*)
be non-trivial elements that are not
common powers. Then in the following three trialities the same
possibility holds in each case.

(1)

(Reduction) One of the following reduction happens:

(a)

[ab]\searrow[aB],[a][b]
;

(b)

[aB]\searrow[ab],[a][B]
; or

(c)

[a][b]\searrow[ab],[aB]
.

(2)

(Geometric) For every minimal embedding of
a*b
, around the
4-valent vertex we see in cyclic
order, up to orientation reversal:

(a)

;

(b)

; or

(c)

.

(3)

(Algebraic) The geodesics
\overrightarrow{a}
and
\overrightarrow{b}
are:

(a)

parallel;

(b)

anti-parallel; or

(c)

cross.

We will use Proposition
4.10
without explicit
reference, in particular that if
\overrightarrow{a}
crosses
\overrightarrow{b}
, then

[a][b]\mathrel{\searrow}[ab]\qquad\text{and}\qquad[a][b]\mathrel{\searrow}[aB]

and if
\overrightarrow{a}
is parallel to
\overrightarrow{b}
, then

[ab]\mathrel{\searrow}[a][b]\qquad\text{and}\qquad[ab]\mathrel{\searrow}[aB].

Now we give the proofs of the algebraic rephrasings of oriented smoothing, oriented max-smoothing, and smoothing we gave in Section
2
.

Proofs of Lemmas
2.4
and
2.5
.

The first two items of
Lemma
2.4
(resp. of
Lemma
2.5
) follow by using
Equation (
2.1
) (resp. Equation (
2.1
)
with equality) at the desired essential crossing, and applying each
case of Proposition
4.10
.
The last item follows by Proposition
4.9
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
2.12
.

The first two items of the result follow by using Equation (
1.1
) at the desired essential crossing, applying each case of Proposition
4.10
, and using symmetry of
f
.
The last item follows by Proposition
4.9
.
∎

If we swap
\overrightarrow{a}
and
\overrightarrow{b}
, the type of L/R crossing/parallelism
can change, as recorded below for future reference.

Lemma 4.11
.

Let
a,b\in\Isom^{+}(\mathbb{H}^{2})
be hyperbolic elements.

(1)

If
(\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{b})
R-cross, then
(\overrightarrow{B},\overrightarrow{A})

R-cross and
(\overrightarrow{b},\overrightarrow{a})
L-cross.

(2)

If
(\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{b})
are R-parallel, then

(\overrightarrow{B},\overrightarrow{A})
are R-parallel,
(\overrightarrow{b},\overrightarrow{a})
are
L-parallel, and
(\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{B})
are
R-anti-parallel.

(3)

If
(\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{b})
are R-anti-parallel, then

(\overrightarrow{b},\overrightarrow{a})
are R-anti-parallel,
(\overrightarrow{A},\overrightarrow{B})
are
L-anti-parallel,
and
(\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{B})

are R-parallel.

Proof.

Immediate from the definitions.
∎

4.4.
Parallelism and crossing of hyperbolic axes

We now turn to some elementary lemmas about crossing.

Convention 4.12
.

Intervals on the circle are always written with endpoints in counterclockwise
order. (Thus if
x,y\in\partial_{\infty}\Sigma
are distinct points,

(x,y)\cup(y,x)
is almost the entire circle.)

Lemma 4.13
.

Let
a\in\Isom(\mathbb{H}^{2})
and
(x,y)\subset\partial\mathbb{H}^{2}
. Then
the following are equivalent:

•

a
is hyperbolic with attracting and repelling
endpoint
a^{+}\in(x,y)
and
a^{-}\notin(x,y)
; and

•

a\cdot(x,y)\subsetneq(x,y)
.

Proof.

This is a statement of NS dynamics of hyperbolic elements.
∎

Lemma 4.14
.

Let
a,x\in\Isom^{+}(\mathbb{H}^{2})
be hyperbolic elements that do not share any endpoints.
Then
(\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{x})
R-cross (resp. L-cross) iff

(\overrightarrow{x},a\cdot\overrightarrow{x})
are R-parallel (resp. L-parallel).

Proof.

The statement that
(\overrightarrow{x},a\cdot\overrightarrow{x})
are R-parallel is (by
definition) equivalent to
a\cdot(x^{-},x^{+})\subsetneq(x^{-},x^{+})
,
which is equivalent, by Lemma
4.13
, to
a^{+}\in(x^{-},x^{+})
and

a^{-}\in(x^{+},x^{-})
, which is equivalent to

(\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{x})
R-crossing.
∎

Lemma 4.15
.

Fix
a,b,x\in\Isom^{+}(\mathbb{H}^{2})
hyperbolic elements with distinct
endpoints so that
(\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{x})
and
(\overrightarrow{b},\overrightarrow{x})

R-cross. Then:

(I)

All four pairs
(\overrightarrow{xa},\overrightarrow{x})
,
(\overrightarrow{Xa},\overrightarrow{x})
,
(\overrightarrow{ax},\overrightarrow{x})
, and

(\overrightarrow{aX},\overrightarrow{x})
R-cross.

(II)

(\overrightarrow{ab},\overrightarrow{x})
R-cross.

(III)

More generally, for any
n,k\in\mathbb{Z}
,
(\overrightarrow{ax^{n}},\overrightarrow{x})

and
(\overrightarrow{ax^{n}bx^{k}},\overrightarrow{x})
R-cross.

Proof.

For the first claim, consider the action of the group
elements on
(x^{-},x^{+})
: we have

x^{\pm 1}\cdot(x^{-},x^{+})=(x^{-},x^{+})
and

a\cdot(x^{-},x^{+})\subsetneq(x^{-},x^{+})
, so all four of
ax^{\pm 1}
and

x^{\pm 1}a
map this interval strictly inside itself. By Lemma
4.13
,
all these elements are hyperbolic and R-cross
\overrightarrow{x}
.

For the second claim,
a
and
b
both map

(x^{-},x^{+})
strictly inside itself, so their product does too.

The last claim is an immediate consequence of the first two.
∎

Lemma 4.16
.

Let
a,b\in\Isom^{+}(\mathbb{H}^{2})
be hyperbolic elements. Then:

(I)

If
(\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{b})
R-cross, then
ba
and
bA
are also
hyperbolic, and the pairs

(\overrightarrow{ba},\overrightarrow{ab})
and
(\overrightarrow{aB},\overrightarrow{Ba})
are both
R-parallel, with endpoints as in Figure
4.3a
.

(II)

If
(\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{b})
are R-parallel, then
ab
is hyperbolic
and
(\overrightarrow{ba},\overrightarrow{ab})
R-cross, with endpoints as in
Figure
4.3b
.
In particular, the triples

(\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{ab},\overrightarrow{b})
and
(\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{ba},\overrightarrow{b})
are R-parallel.

(III)

If
(\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{b})
are R-anti-parallel, then
aB
is
hyperbolic and
(\overrightarrow{Ba},\overrightarrow{aB})
R-cross, with endpoints as in Figure
4.3c
.

Proof.

Note that the actions of
a
and
b
on geodesics act by conjugation
on the underlying word. Specifically, this means that we have

\overrightarrow{ba}
\overrightarrow{ab}
\overrightarrow{Ba}
\overrightarrow{aB}
.
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}

The hyperbolic elements
a
and
b
have north-south dynamics. Using
this in the three cases depending on the relation of
\overrightarrow{a}
and

\overrightarrow{b}
, we have the
following.

(I)

If
(\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{b})
R-cross, then we claim that the
endpoints of the various geodesics must be laid out as in
Figure
4.3a
.

(a)

\overrightarrow{a}
and
\overrightarrow{b}
R-cross

(b)

\overrightarrow{a}
and
\overrightarrow{b}
are R-parallel

(c)

\overrightarrow{a}
and
\overrightarrow{b}
are R-anti-parallel

Figure 4.3
.
Cases for relative position
of
\overrightarrow{a}
with
\overrightarrow{b}
, and corresponding
endpoints of products.

To see this, by the dynamics of
a
and
b
on the
circle as shown,
ba
maps the interval

(b^{+},a^{+})
strictly inside itself, so
ba
is
hyperbolic with
(ba)^{+}\in(b^{+},a^{+})
;
similarly
(ba)^{-}\in(b^{-},a^{-})
. The actions of

b
and
a
interchange
(ba)^{-}\leftrightarrow(ab)^{-}
and

(ba)^{+}\leftrightarrow(ab)^{+}
, forcing the configuration shown.
(Note that all four endpoints
(ba)^{\pm},(ab)^{\pm}
are on the
portions of the circle
where
b
and
a
move in opposite directions.)

Since
(\overrightarrow{B},\overrightarrow{a})
also R-cross, the
other conclusion follows.

(II)

If
(\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{b})
are R-parallel, then
ab\cdot(b^{+},a^{+})\subsetneq(b^{+},a^{+})
, so
ab
is
hyperbolic and
(ab)^{+}\in(b^{+},a^{+})
. The same interchange
property then forces

(ab)^{+},(ba)^{+}\in(b^{+},a^{+})
, as well as

(ab)^{-},(ba)^{-}\in(a^{-},b^{-})
. From this we see that

(\overrightarrow{ba},\overrightarrow{ab})
R-cross, as in Figure
4.3b
.

(III)

If
\overrightarrow{a}
and
\overrightarrow{b}
are anti-parallel, we can reduce to
the parallel case by replacing
b
with
B
, arranged as in
Figure
4.3c
.∎

4.5.
Position of axes

\overrightarrow{aB}
and
\overrightarrow{Ba}
in parallel case

If
\overrightarrow{a}
and
\overrightarrow{b}
are R-parallel,
Lemma
4.16
makes no claim about the
relationship of
\overrightarrow{aB}
and
\overrightarrow{Ba}
. Indeed this is not
determined by the hypotheses: they can be crossing or anti-parallel
(Lemma
4.20
).
Nevertheless, we can extract some information about composite pairs.

Fix
a,b\in\Isom^{+}(\mathbb{H}^{2})
so that
\overrightarrow{a}
and
\overrightarrow{b}
are
R-parallel. We know that
ab
and
ba
are
hyperbolic (with crossing axes). Suppose also that
aB
is hyperbolic. (This will be the case, for example, if
a,b\in\pi_{1}(S)
.)

(a)

\overrightarrow{Ba},\overrightarrow{aB}
splitting

(b)

\overrightarrow{aB}
,
\overrightarrow{Ba}
are
b
-sided and antiparallel to each other

(c)

\overrightarrow{aB}
,
\overrightarrow{Ba}
are
b
-sided and crossing

Figure 4.4
.
Topological cases for the location of
\overrightarrow{aB}
and

\overrightarrow{Ba}
when
(\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{b})
are parallel.

Lemma 4.17
.

If
(\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{b})
are parallel,
\overrightarrow{aB}
does not cross
\overrightarrow{a}
or
\overrightarrow{b}
.

Proof.

If
\overrightarrow{aB}
crossed
\overrightarrow{a}
, then
\overrightarrow{(bA)a}=\overrightarrow{b}
would
cross
\overrightarrow{a}
, a contradiction. Similarly
\overrightarrow{aB}
cannot cross
\overrightarrow{b}
.
∎

Lemma 4.18
.

If
(\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{b})
are parallel, for
g\in\{a,b\}
, the
endpoints of
\overrightarrow{aB}
and the endpoints of
\overrightarrow{Ba}
are in the
same connected component of
\partial_{\infty}S-\{g^{+},g^{-}\}
.

Proof.

By the previous lemma, the endpoints of
\overrightarrow{Ba}
are in one
connected component.
Since
a\cdot\overrightarrow{Ba}=b\cdot\overrightarrow{Ba}=\overrightarrow{aB}
, by NS dynamics of
a
and
b
,
the endpoints of
\overrightarrow{aB}
are in the same connected component.
∎

When
\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{b}
are R-parallel and

\big((aB)^{-},(aB)^{+}\big)\subsetneq(b^{+},a^{+})\quad\text{and}\quad\big((Ba)^{-},(Ba)^{+}\big)\subsetneq(a^{-},b^{-}),

as in Figure
4.4a
, we say that

\overrightarrow{aB}
splits
\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{b}
.
Similarly, whenever

\big((aB)^{-},(aB)^{+}\big),\big((Ba)^{-},(Ba)^{+}\big)\subsetneq(b^{-},b^{+}),

as in Figure
4.4b
,
we say that
\overrightarrow{aB}
is on the
b
-side of
\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{b}
,
and we say that it
is on the
a
-side of
\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{b}
if both intervals
are contained in
(a^{+},a^{-})
. When
\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{b}
are L-parallel
the terminology is adjusted accordingly. We will also use this
terminology for powers
\overrightarrow{a^{n}B^{m}}
and
\overrightarrow{B^{m}a^{n}}
, which will
have the same division of cases since
\overrightarrow{a^{n}}
and
\overrightarrow{a}
have
the same axis.

Lemma 4.19
.

If
(\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{b})
are parallel, then
\overrightarrow{aB},\overrightarrow{Ba}
are
exactly one of
a
-sided, splitting, or
b
-sided.

Proof.

This follows from Lemma
4.18
.
∎

Lemma 4.20
.

If
(\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{b})
are parallel, then
(\overrightarrow{aB},\overrightarrow{Ba})
are anti-parallel or cross.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.18
and the fact that
a\cdot\overrightarrow{Ba}=\overrightarrow{aB}
,
it follows that
\overrightarrow{aB},\overrightarrow{Ba}
cannot be nested with respect to
\overrightarrow{a}
.
Then since
a\cdot\overrightarrow{Ba}=\overrightarrow{aB}
, they must both be either parallel or anti-parallel to
\overrightarrow{a}
, so cannot be parallel to each other.
∎

See Figure
4.4c
and Example
4.27
for examples where
(\overrightarrow{Ba},\overrightarrow{aB})
cross.

Lemma 4.21
.

If
(\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{b})
are parallel and
\overrightarrow{aB},\overrightarrow{Ba}
are splitting,
then
(aB)^{\pm}\in(b^{+},a^{+})
and
(Ba)^{\pm}\in(a^{-},b^{-})
.

Proof.

This follows from
a\cdot\overrightarrow{Ba}=b\cdot\overrightarrow{Ba}=\overrightarrow{aB}
and the fact that
a,b

move in opposite directions (cw/ccw) on these intervals. The only way the
a
and
b
actions can end up in the same place is if they move from one interval to the other.
∎

To draw specific examples precisely, consider the common hyperbolic perpendicular of
\overrightarrow{a}

and
\overrightarrow{b}
, which we will denote
R_{1}
. We will also use the
same notation for the reflection through
R_{1}
.
Let
R_{2},R_{3},R_{4},R_{5}
be the lines and corresponding reflections so
that
a=R_{1}R_{2}=R_{4}R_{1}
, and
b=R_{1}R_{3}=R_{5}R_{1}
. Precisely,

R_{2}
and
R_{4}
are perpendicular to
\overrightarrow{a}
, and
R_{3}
and
R_{5}

are perpendicular to
\overrightarrow{b}
.

Since

ab=R_{4}R_{3}
,
ba=R_{5}R_{2}
,
Ba=R_{3}R_{2}
, and
aB=R_{4}R_{5}
, and all of
these elements are hyperbolic, we see
that the
R_{i}
are reflections along pairwise non-intersecting reflection
lines. There are several potential configurations, as shown
in Figure
4.4
.

We next show that by taking powers we can reduce to the splitting
case, starting with a general convergence lemma.

Lemma 4.22
.

Let
g,x\in\PSL(2,\mathbb{R})
, with
x
hyperbolic and

g\cdot x^{+}\neq x^{-}
. Then, for all sufficiently large
n
,
the element
x^{n}g
is hyperbolic and

\lim_{n\to\infty}(x^{n}g)^{+}=x^{+}.

More generally, if
g,h,x\in\PSL(2,\mathbb{R})
with
x
hyperbolic and

hg\cdot x^{+}\neq x^{-}
, then for all sufficiently large
n
, the
element
gx^{n}h
is hyperbolic and

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}(gx^{n}h)^{+}

\displaystyle=g\cdot x^{+},

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}(gx^{n}h)^{-}

\displaystyle=H\cdot x^{-}.

If
x,g,h\in\pi_{i}(S)
, the hypotheses that
g\cdot x^{+}\neq x^{-}
,
resp.
hg\cdot x^{+}\neq x^{-}
, are automatic.

Proof.

This follows from NS dynamics on
\partial_{\infty}S
.
Indeed, choose a neighborhood
V
of
x^{+}
such that

x^{-}\notin g\cdot V
. Then, by NS, there is

n_{0}>0
such that, for every
n\geq n_{0}
,

x^{n}g\cdot V\subsetneq V.

Thus
x^{n}g
is hyperbolic, with

(x^{n}g)^{+}\in x^{n}g\cdot V\subsetneq V.

As
n\to\infty
,
x^{n}g\cdot V\to x^{+}
and so
(x^{n}g)^{+}\to x^{+}
.

For the more general statement, apply the first part of the statement
to
x^{n}hg
and to
X^{n}GH
. Since

g\cdot\overrightarrow{x^{n}hg}=\overrightarrow{gx^{n}h}
and
H\cdot\overrightarrow{X^{n}GH}=\overrightarrow{HX^{n}G}
, we get the claimed result.

If
g,x\in\pi_{1}(S)
, since each point in
\bdy_{\infty}S
is the
endpoint of at most one geodesic, if
g\cdot x^{+}=x^{-}
then also
g\cdot x^{-}=x^{+}
. But this implies that
g
is elliptic of order
2
,
which is impossible in a surface group. Similarly for the more general
statement.
∎

Lemma 4.23
.

Let
\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{b}
be parallel. If
\overrightarrow{a^{n}B^{m}}
splits
\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{b}
, then so does

\overrightarrow{a^{N}B^{M}}
for every
N\geq n
and
M\geq m
.

Proof.

It suffices to prove the claim for
\overrightarrow{a^{n+1}B^{m}}
and
\overrightarrow{a^{n}B^{m+1}}
.
Note that
\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{a^{n}B^{m}}
being parallel implies

(a^{n+1}B^{m})^{+}\in((a^{n}B^{m})^{+},a^{+})\subset(b^{+},a^{+})
.
Similarly,
\overrightarrow{a^{n}B^{m}},\overrightarrow{B}
are parallel, so
(a^{n}B^{m+1})^{-}\in(B^{-},(a^{n}B^{m})^{-})\subset(b^{+},a^{+})

This proves the claim.
∎

Proposition 4.24
.

If
\overrightarrow{a}
and
\overrightarrow{b}
are parallel, then there exists
s>0
such that

\overrightarrow{a^{n}B^{m}}
is splitting for every
n,m\geq s
.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.23
, it suffices to show that there exist
n,m\geq 0

such that
\overrightarrow{a^{n}B^{m}}
is splitting.

Suppose for definiteness that
(\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{b})
are R-parallel.
Choose
m>0
so that

B^{m}\cdot a^{-}\in(b^{+},a^{+}).

Fix such an
m
. As
n\to\infty
, Lemma
4.22
gives

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}(a^{n}B^{m})^{+}

\displaystyle=a^{+},

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}(a^{n}B^{m})^{-}

\displaystyle=B^{m}\cdot a^{-}.

In particular, for
n
sufficiently large, we have

(a^{n}B^{m})^{-}\in(b^{+},a^{+}),

which implies that
\overrightarrow{a^{n}B^{m}}
is splitting.
∎

Remark 4.25
.

A ping-pong argument
[
36
, §8.2.E]

shows that, in the splitting configuration, the subgroup generated by
a
and
b

is free.
Accordingly, Proposition
4.24
is related to the existence
of an exponent
s>0
(depending on
a,b
) such that, for all
m,n\geq s
,
the subgroup generated by
a^{m}
and
b^{n}
is free.

Results of this type hold in much greater generality, for instance for
Gromov hyperbolic groups and for mapping class groups; see, e.g.,

[
32
]
.

From the above analysis, it follows that when
(\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{b})
are
parallel, many composite pairs are parallel. We can extract a
particular result we will use later.

Lemma 4.26
.

Let
a,b\in\pi_{1}(S)
.
If
\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{b}
are parallel and
(\overrightarrow{aB},\overrightarrow{Ba})
cross,
let
\mathcal{A}\coloneqq\{\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{b},\overrightarrow{ab},\overrightarrow{ba}\}

and
\mathcal{B}\coloneqq\{\overrightarrow{aB},\overrightarrow{Ba}\}
. Then either

•

(\overrightarrow{g},\overrightarrow{h})
is parallel for every
\overrightarrow{g}\in\mathcal{A},\overrightarrow{h}\in\mathcal{B}
; or

•

(\overrightarrow{g},\overrightarrow{h})
is anti-parallel for every
\overrightarrow{g}\in\mathcal{A},\overrightarrow{h}\in\mathcal{B}
.

Proof.

Since

(\overrightarrow{aB},\overrightarrow{Ba})
cross, they must be either
a
-sided or

b
-sided.
If they are
a
-sided, then they are either parallel or anti-parallel to
\overrightarrow{a}
.
If they are parallel, since
\overrightarrow{ab}
,
\overrightarrow{ba}
, and
\overrightarrow{b}
are parallel to

\overrightarrow{a}
and on the opposite side of
\overrightarrow{ab}
, we are in the first
case of the statement.
Otherwise we are in the second case.

If they are
b
-sided, the analysis is similar: just swap
a\leftrightarrow B
and
b\leftrightarrow A
, since
(\overrightarrow{B},\overrightarrow{A})
are parallel.
∎

Suppose that
\overrightarrow{Ba},\overrightarrow{aB}
are
b
-sided and cross, as in Figure
4.4c
. If we replace
b
by
B
, we see that
\overrightarrow{ab}
and

\overrightarrow{ba}
can cross even though
\overrightarrow{a}
and
\overrightarrow{b}
are not
parallel (in fact, they are anti-parallel). Let us show a concrete
example where this situation arises. See also the discussion
in
[
56
]
.

Example 4.27
.

To see that the case
b
-sided and crossing actually arises, consider any two parallel
\overrightarrow{g}

and
\overrightarrow{h}
, for instance coming from an appropriately-oriented
pair of disjoint simple closed curves sitting
as the cuffs of an embedded pair of pants.
Define
a\coloneqq hghgh
and
b\coloneqq hgh
. We claim that
\overrightarrow{a}
and
\overrightarrow{b}
are
parallel. First, by
Lemma
4.16
(II)
,
\overrightarrow{hg}
and
\overrightarrow{gh}
cross and
\overrightarrow{h}
and
\overrightarrow{gh}
are
parallel. Applying the lemma again to
(\overrightarrow{h},\overrightarrow{gh})
, we see
that
\overrightarrow{hgh}
and
\overrightarrow{gh}
are parallel, and applying the lemma
once more we see
\overrightarrow{b}=\overrightarrow{hgh}
and
\overrightarrow{a}=\overrightarrow{hghgh}

are parallel, as desired. See Figure
4.5
.
Finally,
aB=(hghgh)(HGH)=hg
and
Ba=(HGH)(hghgh)=gh
, which cross,
as desired.

Figure 4.5
.
The proof that
\overrightarrow{a}\coloneqq\overrightarrow{hghgh}
and

\overrightarrow{b}\coloneqq\overrightarrow{hgh}
are parallel if

\overrightarrow{g},\overrightarrow{h}
are parallel.

4.6.
Fundamental smoothing results

As part of our construction, we will use large powers of some fixed
elements. We collect some propositions guaranteeing essential
crossings in this context.

Proposition 4.28
.

Let
a,b,x\in\pi_{1}(S)
be curves with
\overrightarrow{a}
,
\overrightarrow{b}

crossing
\overrightarrow{x}
to the right. Then there is
s\geq 0
(depending on
a,b,x
)
so that, for all

n,m,r\in\mathbb{Z}
with either
s<r<m-n-s
or
m-n+s<r<-s
, we have

(4.3)

\displaystyle[ax^{n}][bx^{m}]

\displaystyle\mathrel{\searrow}[ax^{n+r}bx^{m-r}]

(4.4)

\displaystyle[ax^{n}bx^{m}]

\displaystyle\mathrel{\searrow}[ax^{n+r}][bx^{m-r}].

There exists
s^{\prime}\geq 0
(depending on
a,x
) so that for all

|n-m|>s^{\prime}
,

(4.5)

\displaystyle[ax^{n}][ax^{m}]

\displaystyle\mathrel{\searrow}[x^{|n-m|}].

If
x
is simple and
a=b
, we may take
s=s^{\prime}=0
. In particular, for
all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,

(4.6)

\displaystyle[ax^{n+1}][ax^{n-1}]

\displaystyle\mathrel{\searrow}2[ax^{n}].

Note that for the allowed values of
r
, Eqs. (
4.3
) and
(
4.4
) move the exponents
n,m
closer to the diagonal

m=n
.

We will make heavy use of the
band model
centered on

\overrightarrow{x}
, meaning the conformal map from
\mathbb{H}^{2}
to a horizontal
strip
(-\infty,\infty)\times[-v,v]
mapping
x^{-}
to infinity along
the negative
x
-axis and
x^{+}
to infinity on the positive
x
-axis.
We choose the
normalized
band model, picking
v
so that the
action of
x
is horizontal translation by
z\mapsto z+1
.

For any geodesic
\overrightarrow{y}
that is parallel to

\overrightarrow{x}
, there is a
window

w(\overrightarrow{y})
cut out by the
endpoints on the band model, the interval between
y^{\pm}
that
misses
x^{\pm}
. Observe that
w(x^{k}\cdot\overrightarrow{y})
is the horizontal
translate of
w(\overrightarrow{y})
by
k
. See Figure
4.6
for
examples.

Lemma 4.29
.

Given
a,b,x\in\pi_{1}(S)
so that
\overrightarrow{a}
and
\overrightarrow{b}
cross
\overrightarrow{x}
to
the right and
g_{1}=x^{k}ax^{\ell}
with
k,\ell\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have

g_{1}^{+}\in w(x^{k}a\cdot\overrightarrow{x})
and

g_{1}^{-}\in w(x^{-\ell}A\cdot\overrightarrow{x})
. Similarly for

g_{2}=x^{k}ax^{\ell}bx^{n}
with
k,\ell,n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have

g_{2}^{+}\in w(x^{k}a\cdot\overrightarrow{x})
and
g_{2}^{-}\in w(x^{-n}B\cdot\overrightarrow{x})
.

Proof.

For the first part, it suffices to show that
\overrightarrow{g_{1}}
crosses

x^{k}a\cdot\overrightarrow{x}
and
x^{-\ell}A\cdot\overrightarrow{x}
to the right,
or equivalently (by translation) that
\overrightarrow{x^{\ell+k}a}
and
\overrightarrow{ax^{k+\ell}}

cross
\overrightarrow{x}
to the right. This follows from
Lemma
4.15
.

For the second part, we similarly use Lemma
4.15
to
see that
\overrightarrow{g_{2}}
crosses
x^{k}a\cdot\overrightarrow{x}
and
x^{-n}B\cdot\overrightarrow{x}
to the right.
∎

Figure 4.6
.
The band model centered on
\overrightarrow{x}
used in
Proposition
4.28
, in a case where
[x]
is simple.
Some lifts of
[x]
are depicted in blue. The central blue lift
is
\overrightarrow{x}
, and we also show translates by
x^{k}a
,
x^{l}A
,
x^{m}b
and
x^{n}B
, together with their windows.
If
[x]
is not simple, the blue lifts can intersect each other.

Proof of Proposition
4.28
.

In each case, we will show that we can choose lifts of the curves to
the normalized band model for which a
relevant crossing is essential. We start with
Eq. (
4.3
), looking first at the windows
w(a\cdot\overrightarrow{x})
and
w(x^{r}b\cdot\overrightarrow{x})
.
Since each window is a
finite size, there is a finite range
[s_{1},s_{2}]
so that
w(x^{r}b\cdot\overrightarrow{x})

is to the left of (resp. to the right of)
w(a\cdot\overrightarrow{x})
if
r<s_{1}

(resp.
r>s_{2}
).
Similarly there
is a range
[s_{1}^{\prime},s_{2}^{\prime}]
so that
w(A\cdot\overrightarrow{x})
is disjoint from

w(x^{r}B\cdot\overrightarrow{x})
if
r\notin[s_{1}^{\prime},s_{2}^{\prime}]
, and the exponents determine the
ordering of the windows. Set

s\coloneqq\max\{s_{2},s_{2}^{\prime},-s_{1},-s_{1}^{\prime}\}
.

Now consider

g_{1}=ax^{n}
and
g_{2}=x^{r}bx^{m-r}
, with
s<r<m-n-s
. We have

r-m<-n-s\leq-n+s_{1}^{\prime},

so, by Lemma
4.29
,
g_{1}^{-}\in w(x^{r-m}B\cdot\overrightarrow{x})

is to the left of
g_{2}^{-}\in w(x^{-n}A\cdot\overrightarrow{x})
, and

s_{2}\leq s<r,

so
g_{1}^{+}\in w(x^{r}b\cdot\overrightarrow{x})
is to the right of
g_{2}^{+}\in w(a\cdot\overrightarrow{x})
. Thus

\overrightarrow{x^{r}bx^{m-r}}
crosses
\overrightarrow{ax^{n}}
to the left. Similarly if

m-n+s<r<-s
, then
\overrightarrow{x^{r}bx^{m-r}}
crosses
\overrightarrow{ax^{n}}
to the
right. In either case,

[ax^{n}][bx^{m}]=[ax^{n}][x^{r}bx^{m-r}]\mathrel{\searrow}[ax^{n+r}bx^{m-r}]

by oriented disconnected smoothing.
For Equation (
4.4
), write
[x^{r}ax^{n}bx^{m-r}]=[gh]
with

g=x^{r}ax^{n}
and
h=bx^{m-r}
,
so
g^{+}\in w(x^{r}a\cdot\overrightarrow{x})
and
g^{-}\in w(x^{-n}A\cdot\overrightarrow{x})
, and
h^{+}\in w(b\cdot\overrightarrow{x})
,
h^{-}\in w(x^{r-m}B\cdot\overrightarrow{x})
.
Hence, if
s<r<m-n-s
, we have
s_{1}<r

so
w(b\cdot\overrightarrow{x})
is to the left of
w(x^{r}a\cdot\overrightarrow{x})
,
while
r-m<-n+s_{1}^{\prime}
, so
w(x^{r-m}B\cdot\overrightarrow{x})
is to the left
of
w(x^{-n}A\cdot\overrightarrow{x})
, and hence
\overrightarrow{h}
and
\overrightarrow{g}
are R-parallel, giving Eq. (
4.4
) by oriented connected smoothing.

We similarly get parallelism if
m-n+s<r<-s
.

To see Eq. (
4.5
), suppose without loss of generality
that
n<m
. Let
s_{0}=\lceil\operatorname{wid}(w(a\cdot\overrightarrow{x}))\rceil

(with width measured in the normalized band model),
and
let
s^{\prime}=2s_{0}-1
. Since
m-n\geq 2s_{0}
, we can pick
r
so that

s_{0}\leq r\leq m-n-s_{0}
. By the above argument,
\overrightarrow{ax^{n}}
and

\overrightarrow{x^{r}ax^{m-r}}
R-cross (since, e.g.,
(ax^{n})^{+}\in w(a\cdot\overrightarrow{x})
and

(x^{r}ax^{m-r})^{+}\in w(x^{r}a\cdot\overrightarrow{x})
, which are disjoint), so we get the
desired smoothing in Eq. (
4.5
) by using unoriented
disconnected smoothing.

In the cases when
x
is simple, two windows are disjoint, nested,
or identical; this implies that
s_{1}=s_{2}
and
s_{1}^{\prime}=s_{2}^{\prime}
.
If in
addition
a=b
, we also see that
s_{1}=s_{1}^{\prime}=0=s
. This gives Eqs. (
4.3
) and (
4.4
), and the special
case Eq. (
4.6
).

To see that with
x
simple we get Eq. (
4.5
) with

s^{\prime}=0
(i.e.,
[ax^{n}][ax^{n+1}]\mathrel{\searrow}[x]
), we look slightly deeper
in the words. The analysis above shows
(ax^{n})^{-}\in w(x^{-n}A\cdot\overrightarrow{x})
, which is to the right of

(ax^{n+1})^{-}\in w(x^{-n-1}A\cdot\overrightarrow{x})
. By
looking at the band model centered on
a\cdot\overrightarrow{x}
(or
equivalently by conjugating by
a
), we see that

(ax^{n})^{+}\in w(ax^{n}a\cdot\overrightarrow{x})\subset w(a\cdot\overrightarrow{x})
. Similarly

(ax^{n+1})^{+}\in w(ax^{n+1}a\cdot\overrightarrow{x})\subset w(a\cdot\overrightarrow{x})
. We therefore have that
(ax^{n})^{+}
is to the left
of
(ax^{n+1})^{+}
, implying that
\overrightarrow{ax^{n}}
crosses
\overrightarrow{ax^{n+1}}
as
desired.
∎

We also need some results about smoothings where we have high powers of
two different elements. We work with three elements
a,x,y\in\pi_{1}(S)

so that
(\overrightarrow{y},\overrightarrow{x},a\cdot\overrightarrow{y})
are R-parallel; this
implies, for instance, that
(a\cdot\overrightarrow{y},a\cdot\overrightarrow{x})
are also
R-parallel.
We again consider the normalized band model
centered on
\overrightarrow{x}
, starting with another lemma about endpoints.

Lemma 4.30
.

For any triple
a,x,y\in\pi_{1}(S)
so that

(\overrightarrow{y},\overrightarrow{x},a\cdot\overrightarrow{y})
are R-parallel, and for

\overrightarrow{g}=\overrightarrow{x^{k}ay^{n}x^{\ell}}
with
k,\ell,n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have

g^{+}\in w(x^{k}a\cdot\overrightarrow{y})
and

g^{-}\in w(x^{-\ell}\cdot\overrightarrow{y})
.

Proof.

Let
v=x^{-\ell}\cdot(y^{-},y^{+})
, the
complement

of
w(x^{-\ell}\cdot\overrightarrow{y})
.
We have

g\cdot v=x^{k}ay^{n}\cdot(y^{-},y^{+})=x^{k}a\cdot(y^{-},y^{+})=w(x^{k}a\cdot\overrightarrow{y})\subset(x^{-},x^{+})\subsetneq v
,
proving the result by Lemma
4.13
.
∎

Proposition 4.31
.

For any triple
a,x,y\in\pi_{1}(S)
so that
(\overrightarrow{y},\overrightarrow{x},a\cdot\overrightarrow{y})
are R-parallel, there exists

s\geq 0
so that for all
r>s
and
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}
,

(4.7)

\displaystyle[ay^{m}x^{n+r}][ay^{m}x^{n-r}]

\displaystyle\mathrel{\searrow}[(ay^{m}x^{n})^{2}]

(4.8)

\displaystyle[ay^{m+r}x^{n}][ay^{m-r}x^{n}]

\displaystyle\mathrel{\searrow}[(ay^{m}x^{n})^{2}].

Furthermore, for
n,m\geq s
,

(4.9)

\displaystyle[ay^{m}x^{n}][A]

\displaystyle\mathrel{\searrow}[y^{m}][x^{n}]

and for
n,m>2s
,

(4.10)

\displaystyle[aY^{m}x^{n}][A]

\displaystyle\mathrel{\searrow}[x^{s}Y^{s}][Ax^{s}aY^{s}][x^{n-2s}][Y^{n-2s}]

Proof.

We first prove Eq. (
4.7
). We will have a number
of windows
w(x^{m}\cdot\overrightarrow{y})
on the top, all of equal width
in the normalized band model, and
w(x^{m}a\cdot\overrightarrow{y})
on the
bottom, also of equal widths (but not necessarily equal to widths on
the top). Take

s\geq\max(\operatorname{wid}(w(\overrightarrow{y})),\operatorname{wid}(w(a\cdot\overrightarrow{y}))).

For
r>s
, consider the elements
g_{1}=x^{n}ay^{m}x^{r}
and
g_{2}=x^{n-r}ay^{m}
. By Lemma
4.30
, we have

\displaystyle g_{1}^{+}

\displaystyle\in w(x^{n}a\cdot\overrightarrow{y})

\displaystyle g_{1}^{-}

\displaystyle\in w(x^{-r}\cdot\overrightarrow{y})

\displaystyle g_{2}^{+}

\displaystyle\in w(x^{n-r}a\cdot\overrightarrow{y})

\displaystyle g_{2}^{-}

\displaystyle\in w(\overrightarrow{y}).

Since
r>s
, the endpoints appear in the cyclic order

(x^{-},g_{2}^{+},g_{1}^{+},x^{+},g_{2}^{-},g_{1}^{-})
on
\partial_{\infty}S
. In
particular
[g_{1}][g_{2}]\mathrel{\searrow}[g_{1}g_{2}]
by oriented disconnected smoothing, as desired.

To see Eq. (
4.8
), apply
Eq. (
4.7
) with the change of variables

a^{\prime}=a
,
y^{\prime}=x
, and
x^{\prime}=ayA
.
This gives the desired result, after possibly increasing
s
.

For Eq. (
4.9
), we consider
g=ay^{m}x^{n}
and
compare its endpoints to those of
a
. By
Lemma
4.30
, we have

g^{-}\in w(x^{-n}\cdot\overrightarrow{y})
, which for large enough
n
is to
the left of
a^{-}\in w(\overrightarrow{y})
. For
g^{+}
,
we again have to look deeper at the endpoints: since

g\cdot(x^{+},x^{-})=ay^{m}\dot{(}x^{+},x^{-})=w(ay^{m}\cdot\overrightarrow{x})
, we have

g^{+}\in w(ay^{m}\cdot\overrightarrow{x})\subset w(a\cdot\overrightarrow{y})
.
Similarly
a^{+}\in w(a\cdot\overrightarrow{x})\subset w(a\cdot\overrightarrow{y})
.
Considering the bands model centered on
a\cdot\overrightarrow{y}
shows that,
for
m
large enough,
g^{+}
is to the right of
a^{+}
, and thus

\overrightarrow{g}
crosses
\overrightarrow{a}
to the right. Thus if
s
is large
enough and
n,m\geq s
we therefore have

[ax^{n}y^{m}][A]=[g][A]\mathrel{\searrow}[x^{n}y^{m}]\mathrel{\searrow}[x^{n}][y^{m}]

by oriented disconnected smoothing and then (by
Lemma
4.16
) oriented connected
smoothing.

Finally, we see Eq. (
4.10
). By
Proposition
4.24
, if
s_{0}
is large enough, for every
s\geq s_{0}

\overrightarrow{Y^{s}x^{s}}
and
\overrightarrow{x^{s}Y^{s}}
split
\overrightarrow{y},\overrightarrow{x}
, and
similarly
\overrightarrow{Ax^{s}aY^{s}}
and
\overrightarrow{Y^{s}Ax^{s}a}
split
A\cdot\overrightarrow{x},\overrightarrow{y}
; see
Figure
4.7
. Furthermore, by a similar but easier window
analysis as for Eq. (
4.9
), if
s
is large enough
and
n>2s
,
\overrightarrow{a}
crosses
\overrightarrow{x^{s}aY^{m}x^{n-s}}
.
For
n,m>2s
, we
then have

\displaystyle[aY^{m}x^{n}][a]

\displaystyle=[x^{s}aY^{m}x^{n-s}][a]

\displaystyle\mathrel{\searrow}[Ax^{s}aY^{m}x^{n-s}]

(\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{x^{s}aY^{m}x^{n-s}})
cross

\displaystyle\mathrel{\searrow}[Ax^{s}aY^{s}][Y^{m-s}x^{n-s}]

(\overrightarrow{Ax^{s}aY^{s}},\overrightarrow{Y^{m-s}x^{n-s}})
parallel

\displaystyle\mathrel{\searrow}[Ax^{s}aY^{s}][Y^{m-s}x^{s}][x^{n-2s}]

(\overrightarrow{Y^{m-s}x^{s}},\overrightarrow{x^{n-2s}})
parallel

\displaystyle\mathrel{\searrow}[Ax^{s}aY^{s}][Y^{m-2s}][Y^{s}x^{s}][x^{n-2s}]

\displaystyle\text{$(\overrightarrow{Y^{m-2s}},\overrightarrow{Y^{s}x^{s}})$ parallel.}\qed

Figure 4.7
.
Configuration
\overrightarrow{Y^{s}x^{s}}
and
\overrightarrow{x^{s}Y^{s}}
splitting
\overrightarrow{y},\overrightarrow{x}
, and

\overrightarrow{Ax^{s}aY^{s}}
and
\overrightarrow{Y^{s}Ax^{s}a}
splitting
A\cdot\overrightarrow{x},\overrightarrow{y}
, used in Proposition
4.31

In practice we will apply Proposition
4.31
with
four group elements.

Corollary 4.32
.

Let
a,b,x,y\in\pi_{1}(S)
be chosen so that
(B\cdot\overrightarrow{x},\overrightarrow{y},a\cdot\overrightarrow{x})
are R-parallel.
Then there exists

s\geq 0
so that for all
r>s
and
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}
,

(4.11)

\displaystyle[ax^{n+r}by^{m}][ax^{n-r}by^{m}]

\displaystyle\mathrel{\searrow}[(ax^{n}by^{m})^{2}]

(4.12)

\displaystyle[ax^{n}by^{m+r}][ax^{n}by^{m-r}]

\displaystyle\mathrel{\searrow}[(ax^{n}by^{m})^{2}].

Furthermore, for
n,m\geq s
,

(4.13)

\displaystyle[ax^{n}by^{m}][ab]

\displaystyle\mathrel{\searrow}[x^{n}][y^{m}]

and for
n,m>2s
,

(4.14)

\displaystyle[ax^{n}bY^{m}][ab]

\displaystyle\mathrel{\searrow}[ax^{s}AY^{s}][Bx^{s}bY^{s}][x^{n-2s}][Y^{n-2s}]

Proof.

The conditions imply that

(\overrightarrow{y},a\cdot\overrightarrow{x},ab\cdot\overrightarrow{y})
are R-parallel.
Apply
x^{\prime}=axA
, and
a^{\prime}=ab
to Eqs. (
4.7
), (
4.8
), (
4.9
) and (
4.14
) in order to get, respectively, Eqs. (
4.11
)
(
4.12
)
(
4.13
) and (
4.10
).
∎

We now check this configuration is easy to find. The following result
is standard.

Lemma 4.33
.

The action of
\pi_{1}(S)
on
\partial_{\infty}S
is minimal,
i.e., for any
p\in\partial_{\infty}S
, the orbit

\pi_{1}(S)\cdot p
is dense.

We care about the orientations, so we need a slightly more precise result.

Lemma 4.34
.

Let
x\in\pi_{1}(S)
.
Given any
z\in\partial_{\infty}S

and any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a lift
g\cdot\overrightarrow{x}
whose
endpoints
g\cdot x^{+}
and
g\cdot x^{-}
are in
(z-\varepsilon,z+\varepsilon)
, and are ordered counter-clockwise (or clockwise)
with respect to the
\partial_{\infty}S
order.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.33
, endpoints of axes of elements of

\pi_{1}(S)
are dense in
\partial_{\infty}S
; choose any
g
so that

g^{+}\in(z-\varepsilon,z+\varepsilon)
. Next choose any
h
so that

\overrightarrow{h}
crosses
\overrightarrow{g}
. Then for sufficiently large
k
,
h^{\pm k}\cdot\overrightarrow{x}
will be in a neighborhood of
h^{\pm}
and in particular will
not cross
\overrightarrow{g}
.
Finally, for large enough
n
, we have
that
g^{n}h^{\pm k}\cdot x^{\pm}\in(z-\varepsilon,z+\varepsilon)
.
Tracing through orientations shows that one of these translates will
be clockwise and the other will be counterclockwise.
∎

Lemma 4.35
.

Given
x,y\in\pi_{1}(S)
, there exist
a,b\in\pi_{1}(S)
so that

(B\cdot\overrightarrow{x},\overrightarrow{y},a\cdot\overrightarrow{x})
are R-parallel.

Proof.

Immediate from Lemma
4.34
.
∎

5.
Boxes and their measures

Our main goal in the paper is to define a measure on the space of geodesics—in fact, a geodesic current—associated to a curve functional
f
.
Roughly speaking, our strategy is to associate a positive number, in a
countably additive way, to a collection of boxes of geodesics that is
sufficiently rich to generate the
\sigma
-algebra of open sets of

G(\Sigma)
.

To define the measure of our elementary boxes, we look at the
functional
f
applied to longer and longer curves
C_{i}
. The
measure of a box is a linear combination of
f(C_{i})
chosen so that
the highest growth term vanishes, and the limit of interest picks out
the next asymptotics. This is reminiscent to the construction of Otal

[
63
]
.

5.1.
Right-handed boxes

We now outline of the construction of the “sufficiently rich family of boxes” on which our measure will be defined.

Let
f\colon\mathcal{C}^{+}(S)\to\mathbb{R}
be a
functional satisfying symmetry, additive union, smoothing and stability.
Fix
(arbitrarily) a simple closed oriented curve
[x]
which for convenience we take passing through the basepoint. The endpoints of the lifts of
[x]
are dense in

\partial_{\infty}S
, by minimality of the action of
\pi_{1}(S)

(reviewed in Lemma
4.33
).

The prototype for
the kind of boxes to which we assign measures is shown in
Figure
5.1
: we have four geodesics, all lifts of
[x]
, with
two lifts
\overrightarrow{\ell_{1}},\overrightarrow{\ell_{2}}
on the left and two
\overrightarrow{r_{1}},\overrightarrow{r_{2}}
on the right,
and consider the window

[\ell_{1}^{-},\ell_{2}^{-})\times[r_{2}^{+},r_{1}^{+})\subset\partial_{\infty}S\times\partial_{\infty}S
as a basic domain
for defining the measure.
(Recall that we give the endpoints of intervals in counterclockwise
order.)

The lifts are defined by a 4-tuple of
elements
[p_{1},q_{1},p_{2},q_{2}]
of
\pi_{1}(S)
by

\ell_{i}=P_{i}\cdot\overrightarrow{x}
and
r_{i}=q_{i}\cdot\overrightarrow{x}
, with
conditions explained below.

Figure 5.1
.
Schematically determining a RH box from group theory data
with different lifts of a single curve
[x]
. There are no
constraints on how the base lift
\overrightarrow{x}
sits with respect to the
other lifts. The other four lifts have constraints in how they
sit; some other possibilities are shown in Figure
5.2
.
Recall from Section
4.1
that the thicker arrows denote
the
right
action of
\pi_{1}
.

The measure that we ultimately assign to the associated windows will
be invariant under

(5.1a)

(changing
p_{i}
preserving
\ell_{i}
)

\displaystyle p_{i}\mapsto xp_{i},

(5.1b)

(changing
q_{i}
preserving
r_{i}
)

\displaystyle q_{i}\mapsto q_{i}x.

These two conditions are clearly necessary since the endpoints of
\ell_{i}
and
r_{i}
are invariant under such transformations.
In fact, ultimately we will take a limit of these
transformations: we look at the limit as
n\to\infty
of
p_{i}\mapsto x^{n}p_{i}

and
q_{i}\mapsto q_{i}x^{n}
.

The measure
\mu
will also be
\pi_{1}(S)
invariant, which translates to
invariance under
(left-)translating all the lifts by
g\in\pi_{1}(S)
:

(5.2)

(p_{1},q_{1},p_{2},q_{2})\mapsto g\cdot(p_{1},q_{1},p_{2},q_{2})\coloneqq(p_{1}G,gq_{1},p_{2}G,gq_{2}).

We will put off imposing this last invariance for the moment.

We now turn to the conditions on the lifts; for
instance, the window

[\ell_{1}^{-},\ell_{2}^{-})\times[r_{2}^{+},r_{1}^{+})
must avoid the
diagonal.

Definition 5.1
.

A
right-handed lift system
(RH lift system) is a collection of elements

(p_{1},q_{1},p_{2},q_{2})
in
\pi_{1}(S)
so that

(1)

p_{1}P_{2}
and
Q_{1}q_{2}
are not powers of
x
;

(2)

for
i,j\in\{1,2\}
,

(\overrightarrow{p_{i}q_{j}},\overrightarrow{x})
R-cross; and

(3)

for sufficiently large
n
,

(\overrightarrow{q_{2}x^{n}p_{1}},\overrightarrow{q_{1}x^{n}p_{2}})
R-cross.

See Figure
5.2
for examples of
RH systems, and Figure
5.3
for a non-example.
We
distinguish three types of RH systems:
type 1
has
no nested lifts of
[x]
;
type 2
has one parallel pair of nested lifts; and

type 3
has two parallel pairs of nested lifts.
Note that since
[x]
is simple, lifts
cannot intersect each other, and nested lifts cannot be anti-parallel
by condition
(2)
.

Figure 5.2
.
Examples of right-handed boxes, of Type 1 (left), Type 2
(middle), and Type 3 (right).

Figure 5.3
.
Non-example of right-handed box. It is a non-example because
\overrightarrow{p_{2}q_{1}}
does not cross
\overrightarrow{x}

essentially (to the right or to the left)

Definition 5.2
.

Every right handed lift system

(p_{1},q_{1},p_{2},q_{2})
determines a

right-handed box
of oriented geodesics in

\partial_{\infty}\Sigma\times\partial_{\infty}\Sigma-\Delta
,
given by

B(p_{1},q_{1},p_{2},q_{2})=[\ell_{1}^{-},\ell_{2}^{-})\times[r_{2}^{+},r_{1}^{+}),

where
\overrightarrow{\ell_{i}}=P_{i}\cdot\overrightarrow{x}
and
\overrightarrow{r_{i}}=q_{i}\cdot\overrightarrow{x}
.
(On occasion, we may write
B_{x}(p_{1},q_{1},p_{2},q_{2})
to make the choice of base element explicit.)
The set of all right-handed boxes is denoted by
\mathcal{B}
.

Remark 5.3
.

Modifying the right-handed lift system by changing
p_{i}
by
Eq. (
5.1a
) or changing
q_{i}
by Eq. (
5.1b
) determines
the same right-handed box. Conversely, choices of the
p_{i}
/
q_{i}

determining the same RH box are related in this way.

The conditions in Definition
5.1
are chosen to
make the following proposition work.

Proposition 5.4
.

A tuple
(p_{1},q_{1},p_{2},q_{2})
determines an RH lift system if and only if
\overrightarrow{\ell_{i}}=P_{i}\cdot\overrightarrow{x}
and
\overrightarrow{r_{i}}=q_{j}\cdot\overrightarrow{x}
. Then the
endpoints
\ell_{1}^{-},\ell_{2}^{-},r_{2}^{+},r_{1}^{+}
appear in that
counter-clockwise order. In particular RH boxes in
\mathcal{B}
avoid the
diagonal.

Proof.

We first note that condition
(1)
guarantees that
\overrightarrow{\ell_{1}}\neq\overrightarrow{\ell_{2}}
and
\overrightarrow{r_{1}}\neq\overrightarrow{r_{2}}
. Also, condition
(2)
implies
that for any
i,j\in\{1,2\}
,
\overrightarrow{x}
and
p_{i}q_{j}\cdot\overrightarrow{x}

are R-parallel (Lemma
4.14
) and thus (by translation by

P_{i}
) that
(\overrightarrow{\ell_{i}},\overrightarrow{r_{j}})
are R-parallel.

To exploit condition
(3)
, we prove a lemma.

Lemma 5.5
.

Suppose
p,q\in\pi_{1}(S)
so that
(\overrightarrow{pq},\overrightarrow{x})
R-cross.
Set
\ell=Pxp
,
r=qxQ
, and
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
set
g_{n}\coloneqq qx^{n}p
. Then

\displaystyle\lim_{n^{+}}g_{n}^{-}

\displaystyle=\ell^{-}

\displaystyle\lim_{n^{+}}g_{n}^{+}

\displaystyle=r^{+}.

Proof.

For the second statement, apply Lemma
4.22
with
x=qxQ
and
g=qp
.
For the first statement, apply it with
x=PXp
and
g=PQ
.
∎

Returning to the proof of Proposition
5.4
, set

g_{i,j,n}\coloneqq q_{j}x^{n}p_{i}
.
Condition
(3)
says that for sufficiently large
n

the endpoints
g_{1,2,n}^{-},g_{2,1,n}^{-},g_{1,2,n}^{+},g_{2,1,n}^{+}

appear in that ccw order, which by Lemma
5.5
implies
that
\ell_{1}^{-},\ell_{2}^{-},r_{2}^{+},r_{1}^{+}
appear in that order, as desired.
∎

Remark 5.6
.

Condition
(3)
is
phrased as it is to ensure translation invariance for boxes of type 2
and type 3:
\overrightarrow{q_{2}x^{n}p_{1}}
and
\overrightarrow{q_{1}x^{n}p_{2}}
might only cross for large
enough
n
.
For boxes of type 1, the crossing is essential for all
n
.

Remark 5.7
.

The reader may wonder why we choose to make the boxes half-open in
the specified way. It turns out we could have made a different choice and it would not matter for the resulting current, as by
Proposition
3.19

the weight of the
boundary of these boxes under any geodesic current is necessarily
zero: the only lift of closed geodesics with an endpoint starting at one of the endpoints of the box is a single lift of
[x]
, and by definition, these lifts are not contained in an RH box. The half-open
choice we made is
more convenient the proof of Proposition
6.5
.

Proposition 5.8
.

The family of right handed boxes
\mathcal{B}
is dense in the space
of rectangles in
G(S)
, in the following sense. For any

v_{1},v_{2},w_{2},w_{1}\in\bdy_{\infty}S
in that cyclic order,
and any
\varepsilon>0
so the respective
\varepsilon
-neighborhoods are
disjoint, there exist
p_{1},p_{2},q_{1},q_{2}\in\pi_{1}(S)
forming a
type 1 RH box
B(p_{1},q_{1},p_{2},q_{2})
so that
p_{i}\cdot x^{-}\in B_{\varepsilon}(v_{i})

and
q_{j}\cdot x^{+}\in B_{\varepsilon}(w_{j})
.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.34
, we can find translates

\overrightarrow{\ell_{i}}=P_{i}\cdot\overrightarrow{x}
and
\overrightarrow{r_{j}}=q_{j}\cdot\overrightarrow{x}
with both endpoints in
the respective
\varepsilon
-neighborhoods of the
v_{i}
and
w_{j}
.
Furthermore, that same lemma lets us arrange the orientations so
that
\overrightarrow{\ell_{i}}
and
\overrightarrow{r_{j}}
are R-parallel, thus forming a
RH lift system of type
1
.
∎

Figure 5.4
.
Approximating a boundary point of the interval of endpoints of a right-handed box

We now turn to invariance under translation by
\pi_{1}(S)
, as in
Eq. (
5.2
). The
\pi_{1}(S)
-orbit of
(p_{1},q_{1},p_{2},q_{2})

can be parameterized by group elements
(a,b,c,d)
related
to the
p_{i}
and
q_{i}
by

(5.3)

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle=p_{1}q_{1}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad b

\displaystyle=p_{1}q_{2}

\displaystyle c

\displaystyle=p_{2}q_{1}

\displaystyle d

\displaystyle=p_{2}q_{2}.

Figure 5.5
.
Parameterizing an RH orbit via elements
a,b,c,d
. The original position of
\overrightarrow{x}
is arbitrary, but the particular lifts determining the box fall into a type 1. Compare with Figure
5.1
.

Compare Figure
5.5
. In this parameterization, we necessarily have
aCbD=1
.
Note that, by Eq. (
4.2
), the relative position
of (say)
\overrightarrow{\ell_{1}}
and
\overrightarrow{r_{1}}
is given by the double coset

\langle x\rangle a\langle x\rangle
.

Note that, for instance, the points
\widetilde{\ast}\cdot p_{1}^{-1}
and
\widetilde{\ast}\cdot q_{1}
are related via
a
by the right action:
(\widetilde{\ast}\cdot p_{1}^{-1})\cdot a=\widetilde{\ast}\cdot q_{1}
.
On the other hand, the lifts of
[x]
give by
\overrightarrow{\ell_{1}}
and

\overrightarrow{r_{1}}
are related via the element
q_{1}p_{1}
by the left action:

q_{1}p_{1}\cdot\overrightarrow{\ell_{1}}=\overrightarrow{r_{1}}
. Furthermore,
(\overrightarrow{\ell_{1}},\overrightarrow{r_{1}})
are R-parallel, which is equivalent (by Lemma
4.14
) to
p_{1}\cdot\overrightarrow{q_{1}p_{1}}=\overrightarrow{p_{1}q_{1}}=\overrightarrow{a}
R-crosses
\overrightarrow{x}
.

The action of translating all lifts by
\pi_{1}(S)
has been baked in to
this alternate parametrization,
but the measure corresponding to the associated window should be
invariant under the following four transformations:

(5.4a)

(translating along
\ell_{1}
)

\displaystyle a\mapsto xa,\quad b\mapsto xb

(5.4b)

(translating along
\ell_{2}
)

\displaystyle c\mapsto xc,\quad d\mapsto xd

(5.4c)

(translating along
r_{1}
)

\displaystyle a\mapsto ax,\quad c\mapsto cx

(5.4d)

(translating along
r_{2}
)

\displaystyle b\mapsto bx,\quad d\mapsto dx

Conversely, given elements
a,b,c,d\in\pi_{1}(S)
with

aCdB=1
, we can
construct four lifts of
[x]
related by Equation (
5.3
), for instance
setting
p_{1}=1
,
q_{1}=a
,
q_{2}=b
, and
p_{2}=cA
.

Definition 5.9

(RH orbit)
.

Let
a,b,c,d\in\pi_{1}(S,\ast)
be such that:

(0
′
)

aCdB=1
;

(1
′
)

aC(=bD)
and
Ab(=Cd)
are not powers of
x
;

(2
′
)

\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{b},\overrightarrow{c},\overrightarrow{d}
cross
\overrightarrow{x}
to the right; and

(3
′
)

for sufficiently large
n
,

\overrightarrow{dx^{n}aC}(=\overrightarrow{cAbx^{n}aC})
crosses

\overrightarrow{cx^{n}}
to the right.

Then we call the datum
[a,b,c,d]
a
right-handed lift orbit
(or
RH orbit
, for short).

As explained above, this determines a
\pi_{1}(S)
-orbit of RH lift systems.
The one non-obvious point to check is that
condition
(3′)
corresponds
to condition
(3)
:

\displaystyle cx^{n}

\displaystyle=p_{2}q_{1}x^{n}

\displaystyle dx^{n}aC

\displaystyle=p_{2}q_{2}x^{n}p_{1}P_{2}

which after conjugating by
P_{2}
yields

\displaystyle q_{1}x^{n}p_{2}

\displaystyle=g_{2,1,n}

\displaystyle q_{2}x^{n}p_{1}

\displaystyle=g_{1,2,n}.

Thus the two crossing conditions are equivalent.

Definition 5.10
.

Let
B=B(p_{1},q_{1},p_{2},q_{2})
be an RH box. The
\pi_{1}(S)
-orbit of
B

[B]\;=\;\{g\cdot B\colon g\in\pi_{1}(S)\},

where we consider the left action.
We write
[B(p_{1},q_{1},p_{2},q_{2})]
to emphasize a chosen representative, or
[B]_{a,b,c,d}
to emphasize the RH orbit. (On occasion we might write
[B_{x}]_{a,b,c,d}
to be explicit about the base element
x
).

Remark 5.11
.

Even though in the sequel we will fix our base element to be
x
once and for all, we observe that flipping an RH orbit with respect to
x
gives a new RH orbit with respect to
x^{-1}
, in the following sense.
Let
[a,b,c,d]
define an RH orbit with respect to the fixed element
x\in\pi_{1}(S)
.
Then the datum
[a^{\prime},b^{\prime},c^{\prime},d^{\prime}]
with
a^{\prime}\coloneqq D
,
b^{\prime}\coloneqq B
,
c^{\prime}\coloneqq C,d^{\prime}\coloneqq A
is an RH orbit with respect to the base element
x^{\prime}\coloneqq X
. Similarly, if
[p_{1},q_{1},p_{2},q_{2}]
is an RH lift system in the RH orbit
[a,b,c,d]
with base element
x
, the datum
[p_{1}^{\prime},q_{1}^{\prime},p_{2}^{\prime},q_{2}^{\prime}]

where
p_{1}^{\prime}\coloneqq Q_{2}
,
p_{2}^{\prime}\coloneqq Q_{1},q_{1}^{\prime}\coloneqq P_{2},q_{2}^{\prime}\coloneqq P_{1}

is an RH system with RH orbit
[a^{\prime},b^{\prime},c^{\prime},d^{\prime}]
and basepoint
x^{\prime}
.
From this, one immediately has

B_{x}(p_{1},q_{1},p_{2},q_{2})=\sigma\bigl(B_{x^{\prime}}(p_{1}^{\prime},q_{1}^{\prime},p_{2}^{\prime},q_{2}^{\prime})\bigr),

where
\sigma
is the flip map.

5.2.
Measures of boxes

We will now start setting up the definition of the measure of an orbit
of right-handed box
[B]_{a,b,c,d}
. In particular, we will define a function

\widehat{\mu_{f}}\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
in terms of a limit
involving the
f
-value of a certain combination of words involving

a,b,c,d,x
. (Recall
\mathcal{B}

denotes the family of right-handed boxes.)

Proposition 5.12
.

Let
B(p_{1},q_{1},p_{2},q_{2})
be a right-handed box so that
[B(p_{1},q_{1},p_{2},q_{2})]=[B]_{a,b,c,d}
.
Then following limit exists and only depends on the
\pi_{1}(S)
-orbit
of the box:

(5.5)

\widehat{\mu_{f}}(B(p_{1},q_{1},p_{2},q_{2}))\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}\lim_{n\to\infty}f([bx^{n}])+f([cx^{n}])-f([ax^{n}])-f([dx^{n}]).

Note this function on boxes (which will eventually become a pre-measure and
then a measure) is automatically (left)
\pi_{1}(S)
-invariant.

We will now prove the limit in Proposition
5.12
exists using the properties
of
f
introduced in Section
2.1
and the
results about how certain words behave under smoothings proven in
Section
4.2
.

We
first look at a key ingredient limit.

Lemma 5.13
.

Let
f
be a curve functional satisfying symmetry, additivity, smoothing, and
stability.
Let
a,x\in\pi_{1}(S)
, with
\overrightarrow{a}
crossing
\overrightarrow{x}
to the right. Then

(5.6)

\lim_{n\to\pm\infty}f([ax^{n}])-f([x^{n}])

exists.

Proof.

By Eq. (
4.3
), with
b=a
, there exists
r>0
so
that, for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,

f([ax^{n-r}])+f([ax^{n+r}])\geq 2f([ax^{n}]).

(If
x
is simple, we can take
r=1
.) Here we used oriented smoothing and
convex union.
Hence,
n\mapsto f([ax^{n}])
is
r
-convex, and so is
f([ax^{n}])-f([x^{n}])=f([ax^{n}])-nf([x])
, using stability of
f
.

By Lemma
4.15
, additivity, and
disconnected smoothing of
f
, we get

f([a])+f([x^{n}])\geq f([ax^{n}])

for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
By Eq. (
4.5
) with
m=0
,
additivity and unoriented disconnected
smoothing of
f
, there is
N\in\mathbb{Z}
(depending on
a,x
) so that, for
|n|>N
,

f([ax^{n}])\geq-f([a])+f([x^{n}]).

This shows that for
|n|>N
,
f
is an
r
-convex and
bounded function on
\mathbb{Z}
, so by Lemma
3.26
, the
limits (
5.6
) exist.
∎

Remark 5.14
.

The limit in Lemma
5.13
need not exist if one
only assumes
f
satisfies quasi-smoothing.
We give an example on the once-holed torus.
Let
S
be the once-punctured torus with generating set
\{a,b,b^{2}\}
,
where
a
and
b
are the standard generators of
\pi_{1}(S)
, chosen so

\overrightarrow{a}
crosses
\overrightarrow{b}
to the right.
Let
f
be the stable word-length with respect to this generating set. Note that
f
satisfies quasi-smoothing instead of smoothing (see
[
55
, Example 4.10]
) for details).
By Lemma
4.15
,
\overrightarrow{ab}
also crosses
\overrightarrow{b}
to the
right.
The expression in the limit in (
5.6
) for the
pair
ab
and
b
is

f([ab^{n+1}])-f([b^{n}])=\begin{cases}1+\frac{n+2}{2}-\frac{n}{2}=2&\mbox{ if }n\mbox{ is even }\\
1+\frac{n+1}{2}-\frac{n+1}{2}=1&\mbox{ if }n\mbox{ is odd }\end{cases}

which does not approach a limit.
This example can be promoted to one on the genus-two surface.

We also need a two-variable version of
Lemma
5.13
. For later use, we allow two
different elements
x,y\in\pi_{1}(S)
.

Lemma 5.15
.

Let
a,b,x,y\in\pi_{1}(S)
be so that

(B\cdot\overrightarrow{x},\overrightarrow{y},a\cdot\overrightarrow{x})
are R-parallel, and
let
f\colon\mathcal{C}^{+}(S)\to\mathbb{R}
satisfy symmetry, additivity, smoothing,
stability, and homogeneity. Then the limits

\displaystyle\lim_{n,m\to\infty}f([ax^{n}by^{m}])-nf([x])-mf([y])

\displaystyle\lim_{n,m\to\infty}f([ax^{n}by^{-m}])-nf([x])-mf([y])

exist.

Proof.

For
n,m\geq 0
, define
g_{\pm}(n,m)\coloneqq f([ax^{n}by^{\pm m}])-nf([x])-mf([y])
. First,
by Corollary
4.32
, there is some
s
so that both

g_{+}
and
g_{-}

are
(s,s)
-axis-convex: apply
f
to the left hand side of
Eqs. (
4.11
) and (
4.12
), and use
convex union, oriented smoothing, stability, and homogeneity. (The extra
linear terms in
g_{\pm}
are irrelevant for convexity.)

Next we check that
g_{\pm}
is bounded.
On the one hand,

[ab][x^{n}][y^{\pm m}]\mathrel{\searrow}[ax^{n}b][y^{\pm m}]\mathrel{\searrow}[ax^{n}by^{\pm m}].

For the first reduction, the hypotheses imply that

(\overrightarrow{x},ba\cdot\overrightarrow{x})
are R-parallel, and thus
(\overrightarrow{ba},\overrightarrow{x})
R-cross by Lemma
4.14
. For the second one, note
that

ax^{n}b\cdot(B\cdot x^{+},B\cdot x^{-})=(a\cdot x^{+},a\cdot x^{-})
;
then a look at the bands model centered on
\overrightarrow{y}
shows that

\overrightarrow{ax^{n}b}
crosses
\overrightarrow{y}
. Applying
f
and using additivity
and oriented smoothing for
g_{+}
and unoriented smoothing for
g_{-}
shows that

g_{\pm}(n,m)\leq f([ab]).

To get a lower bound for
g_{+}
, applying
f
to both sides of
Eq. (
4.13
) and using additivity and unoriented smoothing shows
that, for
n,m
sufficiently large,

g_{+}(n,m)\geq-f([ab]).

To get a (more complicated) lower bound for
g_{-}
, similarly apply
f
to both sides of
Eq. (
4.14
).

Thus by
Lemma
3.27
the limit exists.
∎

Proposition 5.16
.

Let
a,b,x
be arbitrary elements of
\pi_{1}(S)
and
let
f\colon\mathcal{C}^{+}(S)\to\mathbb{R}
satisfy symmetry, additivity, smoothing,
stability, and homogeneity.
If
a
and
b
cross
x
to the right, then

\lim_{n,m\to\infty}f([ax^{n}bx^{m}])-f([ax^{n}])-f([bx^{m}])=0.

Proof.

We must now show that the limits as
n,m\to\infty
of

\displaystyle g(n,m)

\displaystyle=f([ax^{n}bx^{m}])-(n+m)f([x])

and

\displaystyle h(n,m)

\displaystyle=f([ax^{n}])+f([bx^{m}])-(n+m)f([x])

exist and are equal.
First, Lemmas
5.13

and
5.15
imply that the limits
exist.
Equation (
4.3
) says that there are arbitrarily large

n,m
so that
g(n^{\prime},m^{\prime})\leq h(n,m)
with
n^{\prime}+m^{\prime}=n+m
, and
n^{\prime},m^{\prime}

closer to the diagonal. This immediately implies

\lim_{n,m\to\infty}g(n,m)\leq\lim_{n,m\to\infty}h(n,m)
.
(Take

n,m
large enough so that both
h(n,m)
and
g(n^{\prime},m^{\prime})
are within

\varepsilon
of their limiting value.) Conversely,
Equation (
4.4
) implies the opposite inequality.
∎

Proof of Proposition
5.12
.

The existence of the limit in Equation (
5.5
) follows immediately from Lemma
5.13
.
Since this definition depends on a RH orbit
(a,b,c,d)
—which is invariant under the left action of
\pi_{1}(S)
—it suffices to show that
\widehat{\mu_{f}}([B(p_{1},q_{1},p_{2},q_{2})])
depends only on the box
B(p_{1},q_{1},p_{2},q_{2})
.
To this end, by Remark
5.3
, it is enough to prove that the limit is invariant under the transformations described in Equations (
5.4
).
We prove this for Equation (
5.4a
); the other cases are analogous.

The translation in the RH orbit
a\mapsto xa,b\mapsto xb
(Eq. (
5.4a
)) corresponds to a translation of the RH lift system
p_{1}\mapsto xp_{1}
(Eq. (
5.1a
)). Thus,

\displaystyle\widehat{\mu_{f}}(B(xp_{1},q_{1},p_{2},q_{2}))

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\lim_{n\to\infty}\Big(f([bx^{n+1}])+f([cx^{n}])-f([ax^{n+1}])-f([dx^{n}])\Big)

(Eq. (
5.5
))

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\lim_{n,m\to\infty}\Big(f([bx^{m}])+f([cx^{n}])-f([ax^{m}])-f([dx^{n}])\Big)

(Prop.
5.16
)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\lim_{n\to\infty}\Big(f([bx^{n}])+f([cx^{n}])-f([ax^{n}])-f([dx^{n}])\Big)

(Eq. (
5.5
))

\displaystyle=\widehat{\mu_{f}}(B(p_{1},q_{1},p_{2},q_{2})).

Hence,
\mu_{f}
only depends on the RH box and, since the definition of
\mu_{f}
is given in terms of the RH orbit, it only depends on the RH box orbit.
∎

Now we check the hypotheses to apply
Theorem
3.6
to construct a measure, starting with positivity.

Proposition 5.17
.

For a right-handed
box
B\in\mathcal{B}
, we have
\widehat{\mu_{f}}(B)\geq 0
.

Proof.

Set
[B]=[B]_{a,b,c,d}
.
By condition
(3′)
, for sufficiently large
n
,

[bx^{n}][cx^{n}]=[dx^{n}aC][cx^{n}]\mathrel{\searrow}[dx^{n}ax^{n}].

and thus

f([bx^{n}])+f([cx^{n}])-f([dx^{n}ax^{n}])\geq 0.

Then by Proposition
5.16
and taking the
limit as
n\to\infty
, we obtain

\widehat{\mu_{f}}(B)\geq 0.\qed

Remark 5.18
.

The existence of the double limits in Lemma
5.16
(and the other limits in this section) is reminiscent of the existence of
similar limits in the context of the eigenvalue projection function
on reductive Lie groups (see
[
6
, Lemma 7.10]
). See also Section
2.3
for examples of length functions arising from generalized eigenvalues of higher rank representations.

5.3.
Side-by-side and finite additivity

Finally, we will show
\mathcal{B}
is
a semi-ring (in the sense of Definition
3.1
) and

\widehat{\mu_{f}}
is finitely additive.

First we prove that
\widehat{\mu_{f}}
satisfies a notion slightly weaker than finite additivity.

Proposition 5.19
.

If two RH boxes
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
fit together side-by-side as in
Figure
5.6
(or the symmetric version changing
the
p_{i}
), then
B_{1}\cup B_{2}
is a RH box
and
\mu_{f}(B_{1}\cup B_{2})=\mu_{f}(B_{1})+\mu_{f}(B_{2})
.

Proof.

Set WLOG
B_{1}=B(p_{1},q_{1},p_{2},q_{2})
and
B_{2}=B(p_{1},q_{2},p_{2},q_{3})
, and set
\overrightarrow{\ell_{i}}=P_{i}\cdot\overrightarrow{x}
and

\overrightarrow{r_{j}}=q_{j}\cdot\overrightarrow{x}
. The hypotheses imply that

\ell_{1}^{-},\ell_{2}^{-},r_{3}^{+},r_{2}^{+},r_{1}^{+}
occur in that cyclic order,
so in particular
B_{1}\cup B_{2}=B(p_{1},q_{1},p_{2},q_{3})
is also a
RH box. The definition of
\widehat{\mu_{f}}
immediately yields the additivity.
∎

Figure 5.6
.
Side-by-side additivity for boxes

To show that this side-by-side additivity shows that
\widehat{\mu_{f}}
is finitely
additive, and furthermore that the allowable boxes in
\mathcal{B}
form a semi-ring,
we examine exactly which boxes are allowed, making concrete the
constraints in Definition
5.9
. Boxes
are bounded at positive or negative ends of translates of
\overrightarrow{x}
,
i.e., at
P_{i}\cdot x^{-}
(also referred to as
\ell_{i}^{-}
) or at

q_{j}\cdot x^{+}
(also referred to as
r_{j}^{+}
).
Conditions
(1)
and
(3)

together say that
\ell_{2}^{-},\ell_{1}^{-},r_{1}^{+},r_{2}^{+}
are distinct points
in that cyclic order. It remains to understand
Condition
(2)
, which is a
condition on relative positions of the
\overrightarrow{\ell_{i}}
and
\overrightarrow{r_{j}}

for each pair separately.

Consider a single pair

(\overrightarrow{\ell},\overrightarrow{r})=(P\cdot\overrightarrow{x},q\cdot\overrightarrow{x})
. We claim
this pair is R-parallel iff
r^{+}\in(\ell^{-},\ell^{+})
and

\ell^{-}\in(r^{+},r^{-})
. Indeed,
\overrightarrow{\ell}
and
\overrightarrow{r}
can not
cross (since they are both lifts of
[x]
, which is simple), and then
checking the cases shows that
r^{+}\in(\ell^{-},\ell^{+})
iff

(\overrightarrow{\ell},\overrightarrow{r})
are R-parallel or R-anti-parallel, and

\ell^{-}\in(r^{+},r^{-})
iff
(\overrightarrow{\ell},\overrightarrow{r})
are R-parallel or
L-anti-parallel.

To visualize these conditions better, draw the space of oriented
geodesics

G(S)=\partial_{\infty}\Sigma\times\partial_{\infty}\Sigma\setminus\Delta
(Definition
3.14
)
in the
usual way as a square with opposite sides identified. We represent

(r^{+},r^{-})
by drawing a “whisker”: a vertical segment at

x
-coordinate
r^{+}
from
r^{+}
(on the diagonal) to
r^{-}
.
Similarly represent
(\ell^{-},\ell^{+})
by a
horizontal segment at

y
-coordinate
\ell^{-}
from
\ell^{-}
to
\ell^{+}
.
Then
(\overrightarrow{\ell},\overrightarrow{r})

are R-parallel iff these two segments intersect, as in
Figure
5.7
.
Lemma
4.34
says that there are dense sets of these
horizontal and vertical whiskers that extend from the diagonal almost
all the way back to the diagonal.

Figure 5.7
.
Example of the correspondence between right-handed boxes and collections of four whiskers.

Proposition 5.20
.

The set of RH boxes
\mathcal{B}
is a semi-ring, in the sense of
Definition
3.1
.

Proof.

First, the intersection of boxes is obviously a box. Indeed, all
sides of a box are necessarily in the respective whisker, and any
new vertices of the intersection are thus intersections of whiskers.

Second, we must show that if
A,B
are boxes, then
A\backslash B

is a finite union of boxes. In the interesting case,
A\setminus B

is an L-shaped region, as on the left of
Figure
5.8
. (This shows the hardest case of the
orientation of the region.) If necessary, add a horizontal whisker
slightly above the horizontal inner edge of the L, making
sure that it intersects the vertical whisker forming the
other inner edge of the L. The resulting whiskers allow us to
write the L as the union of three boxes, as shown on the right of
Figure
5.8
.
∎

Proposition 5.21
.

The function
\mu_{f}
is finitely additive on boxes.

Figure 5.8
.
Decomposing a difference of boxes. Left: A possible shape
for
A\setminus B
. Right: Adding a horizontal whisker allows us to
write the region as a union of boxes.

Proof.

Given a division
B_{0}=\bigsqcup_{i}B_{i}
, we will show that the

B_{i}
can be further divided so that the result can be joined by
side-by-side additivity to form
B_{0}
. Let
W
be the whiskers
involved in any side of one of the
B_{i}
. We proceed by induction on

\lvert W\rvert
.

Whiskers in
W
can be “long”, passing through
the interior of the upper or right edge
of
B_{0}
, or not. (Such long whiskers necessarily also pass through the bottom
or left edge as well.)
Suppose first that there is no
long whiskers in
W
. Then the partition is in fact trivial, since
the only possible box with no long whiskers that contains a
point near the upper-right corner of
B_{0}
is
B_{0}
itself.

Otherwise, there is at least one long whisker. Pick one such long
whisker and use it to write
B_{0}=B_{1}^{\prime}\sqcup B_{2}^{\prime}
. Subdivide any
of the
B_{i}
that intersect both
B_{1}^{\prime}
and
B_{2}^{\prime}
, in a
side-by-side way, getting sets of boxes
B_{i,1}
and
B_{i,2}
,
which are partitions of
B_{1}^{\prime}
and
B_{2}^{\prime}
respectively. These give
subdivisions of
B_{1}^{\prime}
and
B_{2}^{\prime}
that involve fewer
whiskers. Thus by induction,

\widehat{\mu_{f}}(B_{0})=\widehat{\mu_{f}}(B_{1}^{\prime})+\widehat{\mu_{f}}(B_{2}^{\prime})=\sum_{j}\widehat{\mu_{f}}(B_{j,1})+\sum_{k}\widehat{\mu_{f}}(B_{k,2})=\sum_{i}\widehat{\mu_{f}}(B_{i}).\qed

Figure 5.9
.
Finite additivity of
\mu_{f}
. We can turn an
arbitrary division of a large box into smaller boxes (left) into
one that is side-by-side decomposable (right). Note that we
subdivided the cyan box in the initial division to make this possible.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be the ring generated by the semiring
\mathcal{B}
. By
Proposition
3.5
,
\widehat{\mu_{f}}
is finitely additive
on
\mathcal{R}
.

6.
Construction of the geodesic current

6.1.
Countable additivity

We prove countable sub-additivity of the function
\mu_{f}
by
proving approximation results. We first find controlled sequences of
decreasing nested RH boxes that we can show have measure going to zero.

Definition 6.1
.

Let the RH lift system
(p_{1},q_{1},p_{2},q_{2})
determine a right-handed
box

B=[s_{1},s_{2})\times[t_{2},t_{1})
, where
s_{i}=\ell_{i}^{-}
and
t_{i}=r_{i}^{+}
. Further assume that
\overrightarrow{r_{2}}
is nested inside
\overrightarrow{r_{1}}
,
so the box is of type 2 or 3.

Define the
vertical accordion
(Figure
6.1
),
F(p_{1},q_{1},p_{2},\vec{q_{2}})
of
boxes based at
B
to be
the sequence of boxes, indexed by
k
, with
p_{1},p_{2},q_{1}
constant
and
q_{2}
translated by powers of
r_{1}=q_{1}xQ_{1}
:

p_{1}^{(k)}\coloneqq p_{1},\quad p_{2}^{(k)}\coloneqq p_{2},\quad q_{1}^{(k)}\coloneqq q_{1},\quad q_{2}^{(k)}\coloneqq q_{1}x^{k}q_{1}^{-1}q_{2}.

This defines boxes whose height becomes smaller as
k

grows, with the top endpoint fixed.

Figure 6.1
.
A vertical accordion
F(p_{1},q_{1},p_{2},\vec{q_{2}})
, shown in
the disk (left) and in the whiskers picture (right)

Similarly, if
\overrightarrow{\ell_{1}}
is nested in
\overrightarrow{\ell_{2}}
(so we again have a box of type 2 or 3), define the

horizontal accordion
(Figure
6.2
)
F(\vec{p_{1}},q_{1},p_{2},q_{2})
by

p_{1}^{(k)}\coloneqq p_{1}P_{2}x^{k}p_{2},\quad p_{2}^{(k)}\coloneqq p_{2},\quad q_{1}^{(k)}\coloneqq q_{1},\quad q_{2}^{(k)}\coloneqq q_{1}.

Proposition 6.2
.

Let
F=F(p_{1},q_{1},p_{2},\vec{q_{2}})
or
F(\vec{p_{1}},q_{1},p_{2},q_{2})
be a
vertical or horizontal accordion as above. Then

•

each
F(k)
is a RH box;

•

F(k+1)\subset F(k)
; and

•

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}\widehat{\mu_{f}}(F(k))=0
.

Proof.

We first prove the results in the case of vertical accordions,
starting with checking that
F(k)
is a box. Since
\overrightarrow{r_{2}}
is
nested inside
\overrightarrow{r_{1}}
, the NS dynamics of the group element
r_{1}=q_{1}xq_{1}^{-1}
translate
\overrightarrow{r_{2}}
inside
(r_{1}^{-},r_{1}^{+})
, as depicted
in Figure
6.1
. In particular the cyclic
order of the endpoints doesn’t change, and we also automatically
have
F(k+1)\subset F(k)
.

To show the measures go to zero, we first reformulate the nesting
condition algebraically. Note that

q_{2}Q_{1}\cdot\overrightarrow{r_{1}}=\overrightarrow{r_{2}}
. Thus
\overrightarrow{r_{2}}
and
\overrightarrow{r_{1}}
being nested means
(\overrightarrow{r_{1}},\overrightarrow{r_{2}})
are R-parallel which is equivalent, by Lemma
4.14
, to
(\overrightarrow{q_{2}Q_{1}},\overrightarrow{r_{1}})
R-crossing. This is
in turn equivalent to
Q_{2}\cdot\overrightarrow{q_{2}Q_{1}}=\overrightarrow{Q_{1}q_{2}}

R-crossing
\overrightarrow{x}
; write
e=Q_{1}q_{2}
.

In the RH orbit notation, we then have

\displaystyle a^{(k)}

\displaystyle=p_{1}q_{1}=a

\displaystyle b^{(k)}

\displaystyle=p_{1}q_{2}^{(k)}=p_{1}q_{1}x^{k}q_{1}^{-1}q_{2}=ax^{k}e

\displaystyle c^{(k)}

\displaystyle=p_{2}q_{1}=c

\displaystyle d^{(k)}

\displaystyle=p_{2}q_{2}^{(k)}=p_{2}q_{1}x^{k}q_{1}^{-1}q_{2}=cx^{k}e.

We thus get the family of RH orbits given by

\bigl([B]_{a,ax^{k}e,c,cx^{k}e}\bigr)_{k=0}^{\infty}.

To show this family of RH orbits have measure going to zero,
by definition of
\widehat{\mu_{f}}
, we need to evaluate the nested limit:

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}\bigl(\widehat{\mu_{f}}([B]_{a,ax^{k}e,c,cx^{k}e})\bigr)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\lim_{k\to\infty}\lim_{n\to\infty}f([ax^{k}ex^{n}])+f([cx^{n}])-f([ax^{n}])-f([cx^{k}ex^{n}])

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\lim_{k,n\to\infty}\bigl(f([x^{n}a])+f([x^{k}e])\bigr)+f([cx^{n}])

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-f([ax^{n}])-\bigl(f([x^{n}c])+f([x^{k}e])\bigr)

\displaystyle=0.

Here, we apply Lemma
5.15

(adding and subtracting suitable copies of
f([x^{n}])
and
f([x^{k}])
)
to see the double limit
exists, and then by applying Proposition
5.16
twice we
can break up the limit into canceling terms as desired.

The case of the horizontal accordion is similar, but there are some
confusing inverses so we spell it out briefly.
Since
P_{2}p_{1}\cdot\overrightarrow{\ell_{1}}=\overrightarrow{\ell_{2}}
,
the assumption that
\overrightarrow{\ell_{1}}

is nested inside
\overrightarrow{\ell_{2}}
(i.e.,
(\overrightarrow{\ell_{1}},\overrightarrow{\ell_{2}})
are R-parallel) is equivalent to
\overrightarrow{P_{2}p_{1}}

crossing
\overrightarrow{\ell_{2}}
to the right, which in turn is equivalent to
\overrightarrow{p_{1}P_{2}}

crossing
\overrightarrow{x}
to the right. In particular we have

\overrightarrow{\ell_{1}^{(k)}}=\bigl(p_{1}^{(k)}\bigr)^{-1}\cdot\overrightarrow{x}=\bigl(p_{2}^{-1}xp_{2})^{-k}\cdot\overrightarrow{\ell_{1}}=\ell_{2}^{-k}\cdot\overrightarrow{\ell_{1}},

which by NS dynamics of
\ell_{2}
gives the picture on the left of
Figure
6.2
. In particular all of the
F(k)

are boxes with
F(k+1)\subset F(k)
.

For the limiting computation, we set
h=p_{1}P_{2}
, so that

\displaystyle a^{(k)}

\displaystyle=p_{1}^{(k)}q_{1}=hx^{k}c

\displaystyle b^{(k)}

\displaystyle=p_{1}^{(k)}q_{2}=hx^{k}d

\displaystyle c^{(k)}

\displaystyle=p_{2}q_{1}=c

\displaystyle d^{(k)}

\displaystyle=p_{2}q_{2}=d.

Lemma
5.15
and
Proposition
5.16
apply as before.
∎

Figure 6.2
.
Horizontal accordion
F(\vec{p_{1}},q_{1},p_{2},q_{2})

We now use Proposition
6.2
to prove that
\mu_{f}

is countably additive.
We will need to consider more types of boxes
than the half-open boxes we have been working with so far. For
B
a
right-handed box
[s_{1},s_{2})\times[t_{2},t_{1})
, let

\overline{B}\coloneqq[s_{1},s_{2}]\times[t_{2},t_{1}]
and

B^{\circ}\coloneqq(s_{1},s_{2})\times(t_{2},t_{1})
be the corresponding
closed and open boxes.

(a)

V(B,\varepsilon)
is a down and left extension of an RH
box
B
with mass less than
\varepsilon
, as in Lemma
6.3

(b)

W(B,\varepsilon)
is an
\varepsilon
neighborhood of the upper and right edges of an RH box
B
, as in Lemma
6.4

Figure 6.3
.
Extensions used in the proof of countable sub-additivity

Lemma 6.3
.

For
B=[s_{1},s_{2})\times[t_{2},t_{1})\in\mathcal{B}
a right-handed box and
any
\varepsilon>0
, there is a right-handed box
V(B,\varepsilon)=[s_{1}^{\prime},s_{2})\times[t_{2}^{\prime},t_{1})\in\mathcal{B}
so that

s_{1}^{\prime}<s_{1}
and
t_{2}^{\prime}<t_{2}
and

\widehat{\mu_{f}}(V(B,\varepsilon)\setminus B)<\varepsilon
.

See Figure
6.3a
. That is,
V(B,\varepsilon)
extends
B
slightly down and to the left,
without increasing the mass much.

Proof.

Let
B=B(p_{1},q_{1},p_{2},q_{2})
.
By Proposition
5.8
, there is some
q_{3}
so that
q_{3}\cdot\overrightarrow{x}
is nested inside
q_{2}\cdot\overrightarrow{x}
; in particular

F_{1}=F(p_{1},q_{2},p_{2},\vec{q_{3}})
is a vertical accordion, and for
each
k
,
B\cup F_{1}(k)
is a RH box.
By Proposition
6.2
, there is
k_{1}
large enough
so that

\widehat{\mu_{f}}(F_{1}(k_{1}))<\varepsilon/2
.
Repeat the procedure to find a horizontal
accordion
F_{2}
abutting on
B\cup F_{1}(k_{1})
. Again there is
k_{2}

large enough so that
\widehat{\mu_{f}}(F_{2}(k_{2}))<\varepsilon/2
, and set
V(B,\varepsilon)\coloneqq B\cup F_{1}(k_{1})\cup F_{2}(k_{2})
.
∎

Lemma 6.4
.

For
B=[s_{1},s_{2})\times[t_{2},t_{1})\in\mathcal{B}
a right-handed box and
any
\varepsilon>0
, there is some
s_{2}^{\prime},t_{1}^{\prime}
with

s_{1}<s_{2}^{\prime}<s_{2}
and
t_{2}<t_{1}^{\prime}<t_{1}
so that the region

W(B,\varepsilon)=\bigl([s_{1},s_{2})\times[t_{1}^{\prime},t_{1})\bigr)\cup\bigl([s_{2}^{\prime},s_{2})\times[t_{2},t_{1})\bigr)
is in
\mathcal{R}
and satisfies

\widehat{\mu_{f}}(W(B,\varepsilon))<\varepsilon
.

See Figure
6.3b
. That is,
W(B,\varepsilon)
is a neighborhood of the upper and right
edges of
B
with small mass.

Proof.

Find
q_{3}
so that
q_{3}\cdot\overrightarrow{x}
nested inside

q_{1}\cdot\overrightarrow{x}
and so that
F_{1}=F(p_{1},q_{1},p_{2},\vec{q_{3}})
is a
vertical accordion. Similarly find
p_{3}
so that
P_{3}\cdot\overrightarrow{x}

is nested inside
P_{2}\cdot\overrightarrow{x}
and

F_{2}=F(\vec{p_{3}},q_{1},p_{2},q_{2})
is a horizontal accordion. Then
for sufficiently large
k
, we have
\widehat{\mu_{f}}(F_{1}(k))<\varepsilon/2
and

\widehat{\mu_{f}}(F_{2}(k))<\varepsilon/2
. Take

W(B,\varepsilon)=F_{1}(k)\cup F_{2}(k)
.
∎

In the setting of Lemma
6.4
, also set

W^{\circ}(B,\varepsilon)\coloneqq\bigl([s_{1},s_{2}]\times(t_{1}^{\prime},t_{1}]\bigr)\cup\bigl((s_{2}^{\prime},s_{2}]\times[t_{1},t_{2}]\bigr);

this is an open neighborhood of the upper and right edges of

\overline{B}
in the subspace topology of
\overline{B}
. Note that
neither
W(B,\varepsilon)
nor
W^{\circ}(B,\varepsilon)
is a subset of the
other.

Proposition 6.5
.

The finitely additive measure
\widehat{\mu_{f}}
is countably sub-additive on
\mathcal{B}
.

Proof.

Let
B_{0}
be a right-handed box, and suppose we are given a
countable covering of it:
B_{0}\subset\bigcup_{i=1}^{\infty}B_{i}
. We
must show that

\widehat{\mu_{f}}(B_{0})\leq\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\widehat{\mu_{f}}(B_{i}).

Fix
\varepsilon>0
. For
i\geq 1
, set

V_{i}\coloneqq V(B_{i},\varepsilon/2^{i})
. Also add one more set by setting

V_{0}\coloneqq W(B_{0},\varepsilon)
and
V_{0}^{\circ}\coloneqq W^{\circ}(B_{0},\varepsilon)
.

Then
\{V_{i}^{\circ}\}_{i=0}^{\infty}
cover
\overline{B_{0}}
, since
the
B_{i}
, for
i\geq 1
, cover
B_{0}
,
B_{i}\subset V_{i}^{\circ}
, and

\overline{B_{0}}\setminus B_{0}\subset V_{0}^{\circ}
. Since

\overline{B_{0}}
is compact and the
V_{i}^{\circ}\cap\overline{B_{0}}
are open in the
subspace topology, we have a finite subcover; let
I\subset\mathbb{N}
be the set of
indices included in the subcover, which necessarily contains
V_{0}^{\circ}
.

By finite additivity and since
V_{0}^{\circ}\cap B_{0}\subset V_{0}\cap B_{0}
, we then have

\displaystyle\widehat{\mu_{f}}(B_{0})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i\in I}\widehat{\mu_{f}}(V_{i})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i\in I,i\neq 0}\widehat{\mu_{f}}(B_{i})+\sum_{i\in I,i\neq 0}\widehat{\mu_{f}}(V_{i}\setminus B_{i})+\widehat{\mu_{f}}(V_{0})

\displaystyle<\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\widehat{\mu_{f}}(B_{i})+2\varepsilon.

Since
\varepsilon
was arbitrary, we are done.
∎

We now put everything together to obtain an oriented geodesic current.

Proposition 6.6
.

Let
f\colon\mathcal{C}^{+}(S)\to\mathbb{R}
be a functional on curves satisfying symmetry, additivity, smoothing and stability.
Then
\widehat{\mu_{f}}
extends to an oriented geodesic current
\mu_{f}
.

Proof.

By Propositions
5.12
,
5.20
,

5.21
and
6.5
,
\widehat{\mu_{f}}

is a finitely
additive and countably sub-additive pre-measure on
\mathcal{B}
, and by Proposition
3.5
has a unique finitely additive and countably sub-aditive extension to
\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{B}}
, which we also denote by
\widehat{\mu_{f}}
. By covering
G(S)
by a countable exhaustion consisting of a union of RH boxes, we see
\mu_{f}
is
\sigma
-finite.
Therefore, by Carathéodory extension Theorem
3.6
,

\widehat{\mu_{f}}
can be extend to a unique measure on

\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{R}}
, the
\sigma
-algebra generated
by
\mathcal{R}
; we denote this extension by
\overline{\mu_{f}}
.
Let
\mathcal{A}
be the Borel
\sigma
-algebra of
G(S)
.
Since
\mathcal{R}\subset\mathcal{A}
, we have
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{R}}\subset\mathcal{A}
.
On the other hand, by Proposition
5.8
, the set of elements in

\mathcal{R}
is dense in the subspace of half-open rectangles of

G(S)
. By the standard argument that half-rectangles with rational endpoints generate the Borel
\sigma
-algebra (see, e.g.,
[
8
, Lemma 1.2.11]
), it follows that
\mathcal{A}\subset\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{R}}
, hence
\overline{\mu_{f}}
is a Borel measure.

Note that
\overline{\mu_{f}}
