is also locally finite, since any compact subset
of
G(S)
can be covered by a finite union of the boxes in

\mathcal{R}
, where the measure takes a finite value.
By definition of
\widehat{\mu_{f}}
, the extension
\overline{\mu_{f}}
is

\pi_{1}(S)
-invariant. Finally,
\overline{\mu_{f}}
is non-negative by
Proposition
5.17
.
∎

It is not
a priori
clear from the definition of
\overline{\mu_{f}}
that it is a flip-invariant geodesic current (recall
Definition
3.15
).
In order to obtain a flip-invariant current, we can take

\mu_{f}\coloneqq\overline{\mu_{f}}+\sigma_{*}(\overline{\mu_{f}})

where
\sigma\colon G(S)\to G(S)
is the flip map, and
\sigma_{*}

the induced push-forward map on oriented geodesic currents.

6.2.
Recovering
f
on curves

It remains to show that
\mu_{f}
recovers
f
as an intersection number on curves.

If
y
is a hyperbolic element representing an oriented closed curve

C
in the surface
S
, choose

z\in\partial_{\infty}S\setminus\{y_{-},y_{+}\}
so that

y^{+},y^{-},\,z,\,y\cdot z
are in cyclic order.
Then

(y^{+},y^{-})\times[z,y\cdot z)
is a Borel fundamental domain for the
action of
\langle y\rangle
on the set of geodesics that intersect

\overrightarrow{y}
transversally.

Before proceeding, we discuss an asymmetric version of intersection number between oriented geodesic currents.

Definition 6.7
.

The
asymmetric intersection
number between oriented geodesic currents is defined as

(6.1)

\vec{i}(\mu,\nu)\coloneqq\int_{\overline{\mathcal{F}}}\mathbf{1}_{R}\,d(\mu\otimes\nu),

where
\overline{\mathcal{F}}
is a fundamental domain of the proper and free diagonal action of
\pi_{1}(S)
on
\DG(S)
, obtained by a similar argument as in Definition
3.20
;
the function
\mathbf{1}_{R}\colon\DG(S)\to\mathbb{R}
is defined as

\mathbf{1}_{R}(l,m)\coloneqq\begin{cases}1&\text{if }(l,m)\text{ are R-crossing},\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

From the definition, we have the following relation to the standard intersection number

(6.2)

i(\mu,\nu)=\vec{i}(\mu,\nu)+\vec{i}(\mu,\sigma(\nu)).

For
p,q\in\widetilde{S}
, one can write
\overrightarrow{G[p,q)}\subset G(S)
for the
right-oriented
transversal, i.e., the subset of
oriented geodesics in
G[p,q)
which right cross the bi-infinite
\overrightarrow{l}
oriented geodesic obtained by extending
[p,q)\subset\widetilde{S}
. Similarly, we will use
\overleftarrow{G[p,q)}\subset G(S)
for the
left-oriented
transversal, i.e., the subset of
oriented geodesics in
G[p,q)
intersecting the inverted geodesic
\overrightarrow{L}
to the right. Clearly,
\sigma(\overrightarrow{G[p,q)})=\overleftarrow{G[p,q)}
.
We have the following partition

(6.3)

\displaystyle G[p,q)

\displaystyle=\overrightarrow{G[p,q)}\cup\overleftarrow{G[p,q)}

We can compute intersections with oriented curves as follows.

Lemma 6.8
.

The intersection and asymmetric intersection
of an oriented closed curve
C=[y]
with an oriented geodesic current
\mu
can be computed as

i(\mu,C)=\mu([z,y\cdot z)\times(y^{+},y^{-}))+\mu(\sigma[z,y\cdot z)\times(y^{+},y^{-}))

\vec{i}(\mu,C)=\mu([z,y\cdot z)\times(y^{+},y^{-})).

Proof.

The second claim follows from the observation that, for any
q\in\overrightarrow{y}
,
\overrightarrow{G[q,y\cdot q)}
and
[z,y\cdot z)\times(y^{+},y^{-})
are two fundamental domains for the action of
\langle y\rangle
on the set of geodesics that intersect
\overrightarrow{y}
to the right.
Specifically, if
y\in\pi_{1}(S)
denotes the element representing the oriented curve
C
, then

\mu(\overrightarrow{G[q,y\cdot q)})=\mu([z,y\cdot z)\times(y^{+},y^{-}))

for every
q\in\overrightarrow{y}
and for every
z\notin\{y^{-},y^{+}\}
so that
z,y\cdot z,y^{+},y^{-}
are in the cylic order. See
[
40
, Lemma 4.8]
): the proof only uses
the
\pi_{1}(S)
-invariance of
\mu
.
The same claim follows for the intersection number using Equation
6.2
.
∎

We finish by proving Theorem
A
, first in the following
form.

Theorem 6.9
.

Let
f\colon\mathcal{C}^{+}(S)\to\mathbb{R}
be a curve functional satisfying symmetry, smoothing, additivity and stability, and
\mu_{f},\overline{\mu_{f}}
as above.
For
C
a closed curve, we have

i(\mu_{f},C)=\vec{i}(\overline{\mu_{f}},C)=f(C).

In particular,
\mu_{f}
is unique among flip-invariant geodesic currents.

Proof.

We first prove that
\vec{i}(\overline{\mu_{f}},C)=f(C)
. Given a representative
y\in\pi_{1}(S)
of the conjugacy class
C
, we will find a sequence of
RH boxes to approximating the fundamental domain for
\langle y\rangle
above, and then show that computing the measure of that
fundamental domain yields
f([y])
.

Choose
p,q\in\pi_{1}(S)
so that
(P\cdot\overrightarrow{x},\overrightarrow{y},q\cdot\overrightarrow{x})
are R-parallel, as in Figure
6.4
.
The NS dynamics of the action of
y
imply that, for
m_{1}>m_{2}
and
n_{1}>n_{2}
, the box

B(py^{-m_{1}},py^{-m_{2}},y^{n_{1}}q,y^{n_{2}}q)
form a RH box. We will look
at the following particular family for
n\geq 0
:

\displaystyle p_{1}^{(n)}

\displaystyle\coloneqq p

\displaystyle q_{1}^{(n)}

\displaystyle\coloneqq y^{n}q

\displaystyle p_{2}^{(n)}

\displaystyle\coloneqq py

\displaystyle q_{2}^{(n)}

\displaystyle\coloneqq y^{-(n+1)}q.

Figure 6.4
.
The boxes used in proving that
\mu_{f}
recovers
f
.

In the RH orbit notation, we have

\displaystyle a^{(n)}

\displaystyle=py^{n}q

\displaystyle b^{(n)}

\displaystyle=py^{-(n+1)}q

\displaystyle c^{(n)}

\displaystyle=py^{n+1}q

\displaystyle d^{(n)}

\displaystyle=py^{-n}q.

If we let
z=(P\cdot\overrightarrow{x})^{-}
, and
B_{n}\coloneqq B_{a^{(n)},b^{(n)},c^{(n)},d^{(n)}}
then we have

\vec{i}(\mu,[y])=\overline{\mu_{f}}([z,Y\cdot z))\times(y^{+},y^{-}))\\
\begin{aligned} &=\lim_{n\to\infty}\overline{\mu_{f}}(B_{n})\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\lim_{n\to\infty}\lim_{m\to\infty}f([py^{n+1}qx^{m}])+f([py^{-(n+1)}qx^{m}])-f([py^{n}qx^{m}])-f([py^{-n}qx^{m}])\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\bigl(f([y])+f([y^{-1}])\bigr)=f([y]).\end{aligned}

The first equality follows by the fact that the boxes defined
above converge from below to the
[z,Y\cdot z)\times(y^{+},y^{-})
(the fundamental domain for the
action of
\langle Y\rangle
) as
n\to\infty
, since
B_{n-1}\subset B_{n}
.
At the second equality, we use definition of
\widehat{\mu_{f}}
on RH boxes.
For the third, Lemma
5.15
lets us pass
from the iterated limit to a double limit, and gives a
number of canceling terms of
mf([x])
and
nf([y^{\pm 1}])
; only
two terms survive the cancellation. Then symmetry of
f
gives the result.

The property
i(\mu_{f},C)=f(C)
for any closed curve
C
persists by the definition of
\mu_{f}
and the fact that
\overline{\mu_{f}}
is finitely additive. Therefore,
\mu_{f}
is a geodesic current with the same intersection numbers as the oriented geodesic current
\overline{\mu_{f}}
.

Finally, the uniqueness of the geodesic current
\mu_{f}
is
a result of Otal, geodesic currents are determined by their marked
length spectrum
[
63
, Théorème 2]
.
∎

Remark 6.10
.

In
[
17
, Proposition 4.3]
a geodesic current is induced
from a cross-ratio.
Our construction is more general (in particular, we do not rely on a cross-ratio).
We discuss here why our construction does not follow from their arguments.
Given a dense subset
X\subset\partial_{\infty}S
, they consider the set of
\mathcal{R}(X)
of all proper rectangles of geodesics with endpoints on
X
.
They start from a finitely additive function
r\colon\mathcal{R}(X)\to\mathbb{R}
(defined suitably from a cross-ratio)
that is (1) side-by-side additive: if
R,R_{1},R_{2}\in\mathcal{R}(X)

with
R=R_{1}\cup R_{2}
, with
R_{1},R_{2}
disjoint,
r(R)=r(R_{1})+r(R_{2})
;
and (2) monotonic: if
R,R^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}(X)
with
R\subset R^{\prime}
, we have
r(R)\leq r(R^{\prime})
.

Our function
\mu_{f}
on RH boxes satisfies these properties by Proposition
5.19

and Proposition
5.21
, however, if we take
X
to
be the set of
endpoints of translates of
x
, then
\mu_{f}
is only
defined in a proper subset of
\mathcal{R}(X)

Therefore, we cannot directly apply their result.

Moreover, we need the extra control on the family of RH
boxes to recover the curve functional
f
from the geodesic current.
In Section
10
, we prove that every cross-ratio induces a curve functional (i.e., period) satisfying the hypotheses of our Theorem
A
, and thus our result generalizes
[
17
, Proposition 4.3]
.

6.3.
Proof of Main Theorems
A
A and
A′

Now we prove the two versions of the main theorem as stated in the introduction. The topological version (Theorem
A
) is stated in terms of functionals on unoriented multi-curves. The algebraic version of the
theorem, namely, Theorem
A′
, is phrased in terms of functions on the surface group.

Proof of Theorem
A
.

By Definition
4.4
and Proposition
7.8
, the induced additive and symmetric functional on oriented multi-curves satisfies stability. Hence, by Theorem
6.9
, the result follows.
∎

Proof of Theorem
A′
.

As in the statement, let
\overline{f}(a)\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}\left(f(a)+f(A)\right)
, and extend it linearly to be an
additive
function on multi-curves.

To prove the theorem, we will show that (this extension of)
\overline{f}
satisfies the hypothesis of Theorem
A
, and then apply that Theorem to conclude.

We will first verify smoothing of
\overline{f}
.
We start
with oriented disconnected smoothing, between crossing elements

(\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{b})
, which we can assume R-cross by switching
a,b
if
necessary. We have

\displaystyle\overline{f}(a)+\overline{f}(b)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\bigl(f(a)+f(A)+f(b)+f(B)\bigr)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\bigl(f(ab)+f(AB)\bigr)=\overline{f}(ab).

In the inequality, we used the assumption on
f
and the fact that

(\overrightarrow{A},\overrightarrow{B})
also
R-cross, as well as conjugation invariance for
f(AB)=f((ba)^{-1})
.
By grouping the four terms differently, and using the fact that

(\overrightarrow{b},\overrightarrow{A})
R-cross, we also get
\overline{f}(a)+\overline{f}(b)\geq\overline{f}(bA)
.

Next we turn to oriented connected smoothing of a curve at an
essential self-crossing.
Using Proposition
4.10
, assume the essential
self-crossing is realized by the self-intersection of a curve

[\gamma]=[ab]
, where
(\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{b})
are parallel; by switching
them if necessary, we can assume they are R-parallel, and thus so are

(\overrightarrow{B},\overrightarrow{A})
. Then

\displaystyle\overline{f}(ab)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\bigl(f(ab)+f(BA)\bigr)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\bigl(f(a)+f(b)+f(B)+f(A)\bigr)=\overline{f}(a)+\overline{f}(b).

We have thus deduced
\overline{f}
satisfies oriented connected and disconnected smoothing. Therefore, by Corollary
7.3
below,

\overline{f}
satisfies unoriented connected and disconnected smoothing as well.
Finally, stability implies oriented power-smoothing, i.e.,
f(a^{n+m})\geq f(a^{n})+f(b^{n})
for every
n,m\geq 1
. This establishes smoothing of
\overline{f}
. Hence, smoothing, stability and additivity are satisfied for the function on unoriented curves
\overline{f}
.
We are thus under the
hypotheses of Theorem
A
.
∎

Remark 6.11
.

In fact, we can replace the following hypotheses in Theorem
A′
by the following slightly weaker ones:

•

f
can be replaced by power smoothing (by Proposition
7.8
);

•

oriented smoothing can be replaced by R-oriented smoothing:

(6.4)

\displaystyle f(ab)

\displaystyle\geq f(a)+f(b)

\displaystyle\text{if }(\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{b})\text{ are R-parallel},

(6.5)

\displaystyle f(a)+f(b)

\displaystyle\geq f(ab)

\displaystyle\text{if }(\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{b})\text{ R-cross}.

7.
Relations between types of smoothings

In this section we examine the relationships between different types of smoothing. In particular, we will consider the distinctions between oriented and unoriented smoothing, as well as between connected and disconnected smoothing, and explore how these two attributes interact.

A priori the hypothesis of oriented smoothing on
f
is weaker than
smoothing, since it does not include the hypotheses on unoriented smoothings.
However, it follows from the other hypotheses; the key ingredient is
our previous work in
[
55
]
. We prove the following,
which was crucial to prove the simpler algebraic statement in
Theorem
A′
; it is not necessary for the
proof of Theorem
A
.

Proposition 7.1
.

If
f
satisfies oriented smoothing
(connected and disconnected), convex union,
stability, homogeneity, and symmetry, then
f
satisfies smoothing.

The proof uses the following result, which can be seen as a weak
converse of the main result in our previous paper.

Proposition 7.2
.

Suppose
f
is an symmetric curve functional satisfying
convex union and homogeneity that extends continuously to geodesic currents.
If
f
satisfies disconnected smoothing (resp. connected smoothing) then
f
satisfies connected smoothing (resp. disconnected smoothing).

Proof of
Proposition
7.1
, assuming Proposition
7.2
.

Since
f
satisfies disconnected oriented smoothing and is
symmetric, it also
satisfies disconnected unoriented smoothing. Indeed, let

(\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{b})
R-cross. Then
(\overrightarrow{B},\overrightarrow{a})
R-cross, so by
disconnected oriented smoothing

f([B][a])\geq f([Ba]).

Since
f
is symmetric, we have
f([b][a])=f([B][a])\geq f([Ba])
, as desired.

Since
f
satisfies oriented smoothing, convex union, stability and
homogeneity, it extends continuously and homogeneously
to oriented geodesic currents, by
[
55
, Theorem A]
. Hence by
Proposition
7.2
, it satisfies connected
smoothing, and thus all cases of smoothing.
∎

As an immediate corollary, we have the following.

Corollary 7.3
.

If
f
is symmetric and satisfies oriented smoothing, additivity, and
stability, then
f
satisfies smoothing.

We now turn to the proof of Proposition
7.2
,
starting with some lemmas. The first lets us approximate
disconnected curves by connected curves and vice versa.

Lemma 7.4
.

Given any
g,h\in\pi_{1}(S)
, we have

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\delta_{[g^{n}h^{n}]}}{n}=\delta_{[g]}+\delta_{[h]}

in the weak
∗
-topology of geodesic currents.

Recall that here
\delta_{[g]}
denotes the geodesic current representing the curve
[g]
(Equation (
3.1
)).

Proof.

This is a straightforward fact of the weak
∗
-topology convergence
of geodesic currents and follows from computations similar
to
[
73
, Proposition 5.1]
.
∎

Next we give some extensions of Lemma
4.16

to powers.

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
a,b\in\pi_{1}(S)
so that
\bigl(\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{b}\bigr)
are
R-parallel. Then for all

n\geq 1
, the triple
(\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{a^{n}b^{n}},\overrightarrow{b})
is R-parallel.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on
n
, repeatedly applying
Lemma
4.16
(II)
. The case

n=1
is directly that lemma.
For the inductive step, we have:

\displaystyle\bigl(\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{a^{n-1}b^{n-1}},\overrightarrow{b}\bigr)\text{ R-parallel}

(Induction)

\displaystyle\quad\Rightarrow\bigl(\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{a^{n}b^{n-1}},\overrightarrow{a^{n-1}b^{n-1}},\overrightarrow{b}\bigr)\text{ R-parallel}

(Lemma
4.16
(II)
on

\bigl(\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{a^{n-1}b^{n-1}}\bigr)
)

\displaystyle\quad\Rightarrow\bigl(\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{a^{n}b^{n-1}},\overrightarrow{a^{n}b^{n}},\overrightarrow{b}\bigr)\text{ R-parallel}

\displaystyle\text{(Lemma~\ref{lem:righthandedfoundation}\ref{item:ab-parallel} on
$\bigl(\overrightarrow{a^{n}b^{n-1}},\overrightarrow{b}\bigr)$)}.\qed

Lemma 7.6
.

Let
a,b\in\pi_{1}(S)
so that
\bigl(\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{b}\bigr)
are
R-parallel. Then for all

n\geq 1
,
m\geq 1
the pair
(\overrightarrow{b^{m}a^{m}},\overrightarrow{a^{n}b^{n}})
R-cross.

Proof.

Assume first
m=1
.
We proceed it by induction on
n
.
The base case
is clear by
Lemma
4.16
(II)
. In
general we have

\displaystyle\bigl(\overrightarrow{ba},\overrightarrow{a^{n-1}b^{n-1}}\bigr)\text{ R-cross}

(Induction)

\displaystyle\quad\Rightarrow\bigl(\overrightarrow{ba},\overrightarrow{a^{n-1}b^{n}a}\bigr)\text{ R-cross}

(Lemma
4.15
)

\displaystyle\quad\Rightarrow\bigl(\overrightarrow{ab},\overrightarrow{a^{n}b^{n}}\bigr)\text{ R-cross}

\displaystyle\text{(Conjugate by~$a$)}.

Finally,
Lemma
7.5
and the R-crossing of

\bigl(\overrightarrow{ab},\overrightarrow{a^{n}b^{n}}\bigr)
restrict the
endpoints of
\overrightarrow{a^{n}b^{n}}
to the indicated intervals in
Figure
7.1a
, which implies that
\overrightarrow{a^{n}b^{n}}
crosses

\overrightarrow{ba}
.

For
m\geq 1
, we apply the symmetric argument to show that

\bigl(\overrightarrow{b^{m}a^{m}},\overrightarrow{ba}\bigr)
R-cross with the endpoints of
\overrightarrow{b^{m}a^{m}}
restricted to the intervals in Figure
7.1a
.
∎

(a)

\overrightarrow{a}
and
\overrightarrow{b}
are R-parallel
implies
(\overrightarrow{b^{m}a^{m}},\overrightarrow{a^{n}b^{n}})
R-cross
and
(\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{a^{n}b^{n}},\overrightarrow{b})
are R-parallel. Here the endpoints of the geodesic
\overrightarrow{a^{n}b^{n}}
satisfy
(a^{n}b^{n})^{+}\in(ab^{+},a^{+})
and
(a^{n}b^{n})^{-}\in(ab^{-},b^{-})
, and the endpoints of the geodesic
\overrightarrow{b^{m}a^{m}}
satisfy
(b^{m}a^{m})^{+}\in(b^{+},ba^{+})
and
(b^{m}a^{m})^{-}\in(a^{-},ba^{-})
.

(b)

\overrightarrow{b}
and
\overrightarrow{a}
R-cross implies
\overrightarrow{a^{n}b^{n}}
is R-parallel to
\overrightarrow{ba}
. Note that the geodesic
\overrightarrow{a^{n-1}b^{n}a}
and
\overrightarrow{ba}
are parallel, since
\overrightarrow{a^{n-1}b^{n-1}}
and
\overrightarrow{ba}
are parallel by induction. After conjugation by
a
the result follows.

Figure 7.1
.
Crossings of powers

Lemma 7.7
.

Let
a,b\in\pi_{1}(S)
so that
(\overrightarrow{b},\overrightarrow{a})
R-cross. Then for all

n\geq 1
,
(\overrightarrow{a^{n}b^{n}},\overrightarrow{ba})
are R-parallel, and for every
n\geq 2
, the pair
(\overrightarrow{a^{n}b^{n}},\overrightarrow{ab})
are R-parallel.

Proof.

We proceed it by induction on
n
. The base case is clear by
Lemma
4.16
(I)
.
In general we have

\displaystyle\bigl(\overrightarrow{a^{n-1}b^{n-1}},\overrightarrow{ba}\bigr)\text{ R-parallel}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\bigl(\overrightarrow{a^{n-1}b^{n}a},\overrightarrow{ba}\bigr)\text{ R-parallel}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\bigl(\overrightarrow{a^{n}b^{n}},\overrightarrow{ab}\bigr)\text{ R-parallel}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\bigl(\overrightarrow{a^{n}b^{n}},\overrightarrow{ba}\bigr)\text{ R-parallel}

where we use the inductive hypothesis,
Lemma
4.16
(II)
,
conjugation, and transitivity of R-parallelism. See
Figure
7.1b
.
∎

Proof of Direction 1 of Proposition
7.2
.

Suppose first
f\colon\mathcal{C}^{+}(S)\to\mathbb{R}
satisfies convex union, disconnected smoothing, homogeneity and it extends continuously to geodesic currents.

Given a pair
a,b\in\pi_{1}(S)
with
(\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{b})
parallel (so

[ab]\mathrel{\searrow}[a][b]
and
[ab]\mathrel{\searrow}[aB]
), we show first
f

satisfies oriented connected
smoothing, i.e.,
f([ab])\geq f([a][b])
.

We will start by showing, by induction on
n
, that

(7.1)

2nf([ab])\geq f([a^{2n}b^{2n}]).

The base case
n=1
follows from the fact that if
\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{b}

are parallel,
\overrightarrow{ab}
and
\overrightarrow{ba}
cross, and hence

2[ab]=[ab][ba]\mathrel{\searrow}[ab^{2}a]=[a^{2}b^{2}].

Hence, by homogeneity and disconnected smoothing, the base case follows.
Now, we prove the induction step.

\displaystyle 2nf([ab])

\displaystyle=2(n-1)f([ab])+2f([ab])

\displaystyle\geq f([a^{2(n-1)}b^{2(n-1)}])+f([b^{2}a^{2}])

(Induction)

\displaystyle\geq f([a^{2(n-1)}b^{2(n-1)}][b^{2}a^{2}])

(Convex union)

\displaystyle\geq f([a^{2(n-1)}b^{2(n-1)}b^{2}a^{2}])=f([a^{2n}b^{2n}])

(Lemma
7.6
and disconnected smoothing)

Divide Eq. (
7.1
) by
2n
, let
n
go to infinity, and
use Lemma
7.4
to obtain

f([ab])\geq f([a][b])

which proves oriented connected smoothing.

Second, we prove unoriented connected smoothing: with
a,b
as above,

f([ab])\geq f([aB])
.
We split into two cases, depending on the relative position of
\overrightarrow{aB}

and
\overrightarrow{Ba}
. By
Lemma
4.20
,
(\overrightarrow{aB},\overrightarrow{Ba})
are either anti-parallel
or cross.
We first consider the case when
(\overrightarrow{aB},\overrightarrow{Ba})
are anti-parallel. We will prove by induction that

(7.2)

2nf([ab])\geq f([(aB)^{n}(Ab)^{n}]).

The base case,
n=1
, follows since
(\overrightarrow{ab},\overrightarrow{ba})
cross, hence

[ba][ab]\mathrel{\searrow}[ba(ab)^{-1}]=[(aB)(Ab)].

By homogeneity and disconnected smoothing, we get the base case.
Now, we prove the induction step:

\displaystyle 2nf([ab])

\displaystyle=2(n-1)f([ab])+2f([ab])

\displaystyle\geq f([(aB)^{n-1}(Ab)^{n-1}])+f([(Ab)(aB)])

(Induction)

\displaystyle\geq f([(aB)^{n-1}(Ab)^{n-1}(Ab)(aB)])

(Convex union and disconnected smoothing)

\displaystyle=f([(aB)^{n}(Ab)^{n}]).

where the disconnected smoothing is applied to

[(aB)^{n-1}(Ab)^{n-1}][(Ab)(aB)]\mathrel{\searrow}[(aB)^{n-1}(Ab)^{n-1}(Ab)(aB)].

For this reduction, note that
\overrightarrow{aB}
and
\overrightarrow{Ba}
are
anti-parallel, so
\overrightarrow{aB}
and
\overrightarrow{Ab}
are parallel. Hence, we
can apply Lemma
7.6
to conclude that

\overrightarrow{(aB)^{n-1}(Ab)^{n-1}}
and
\overrightarrow{(Ab)(aB)}
cross.

Divide Eq. (
7.2
) by
2n
, let
n
go to
infinity, and use Lemma
7.4
to get

f([ab])\geq\frac{f([aB][Ab])}{2}=f([aB])

where we used symmetry and homogeneity in the last equality.

Now, still in the unoriented connected smoothing case, suppose

(\overrightarrow{aB},\overrightarrow{Ba})
cross.
If
(\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{b})
are parallel it follows by Lemma
4.26
that either
(\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{bA})
are parallel or
(\overrightarrow{b},\overrightarrow{aB})
are parallel.
In the first case, we have

f([ba])=f([bA)(a^{2})])\geq f([bA])+f(a^{2})\geq f([bA])

where in the first inequality we used oriented connected smoothing, and in the last inequality the fact that
f(a^{2})\geq 0
, by Proposition
2.18
.
In the second case,

f([ab])=f([aB)(b^{2})])\geq f([aB])+f(b^{2})\geq f([aB])

as before. This proves unoriented connected smoothing and thus finishes the proof that disconnected smoothing implies connected smoothing.
∎

Proof of Direction 2 of Proposition
7.2
.

We now prove the second part, namely, if
f\colon\mathcal{C}^{+}(S)\to\mathbb{R}
satisfies symmetry, connected smoothing, homogeneity and continuity, then
f
satisfies disconnected smoothing. The proof is very similar to the first part.

Namely, given
a,b\in\pi_{1}(S)
with
(\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{b})
crossing, we prove, by induction on
n
, the following inequality

(7.3)

f([a^{2n}b^{2n}])\geq 2nf([ab]).

The base case follows by noting that if
(\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{b})
R-cross, then
(\overrightarrow{ba},\overrightarrow{ab})
are R-parallel, and then by connected smoothing

f([a^{2}b^{2}])=f([ab^{2}a])\geq 2f([ab]).

For the induction step, note that

\displaystyle f([a^{2n}b^{2n}])

\displaystyle=f([a^{2}(a^{2(n-1)}b^{2(n-1)})b^{2}])

\displaystyle=f([b^{2}a^{2}(a^{2(n-1)}b^{2(n-1)})])

\displaystyle\geq f([b^{2}a^{2}])+f([a^{2(n-1)}b^{2(n-1)})])

(Oriented connected smoothing)

\displaystyle\geq 2f([ab])+2(n-1)f([ab])

(Induction)

\displaystyle=2nf([ab]).

To apply oriented connected smoothing, we used that
b^{2}a^{2}
and
a^{2(n-1)}b^{2(n-1)}
are parallel, which follows from the fact that
a
and
b
are crossing, and Lemma
7.7
.
Finally, using Lemma
7.4
as above, we get

f([a][b])\geq f([ab])

which proves oriented disconnected smoothing.

Unoriented disconnected smoothing is proved in the same way, replacing

b
by
B
.
∎

Finally, we show that power smoothing and oriented disconnected smoothing imply stability.

Proposition 7.8
.

Let
f\colon\mathcal{C}^{+}(S)\to\mathbb{R}
be a curve functional satisfying:

(1)

additivity

(2)

sub-stability

(3)

oriented disconnected smoothing.

Then
f
satisfies stability:
f(C^{n})=nf(C)
.

Proof.

Let
a\in\pi_{1}(S)
be an arbitary element.
By hypothesis (2)
(sub-stable), we have
f(a^{n+m})\geq f(a^{n})+f(a^{m})
.
By Fekete’s Lemma
3.30
, the limit

F(a)\coloneqq\lim_{n^{+}}\frac{f(a^{n})}{n}

exists, and
f(a)\leq F(a)
. We must show
F(a)\leq f(a)
.

Find
b\in\pi_{1}(S)
so that
(\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{b})
R-cross, which we can find by
density of endpoints of axes of hyperbolic elements in
G(S)
.
Then, applying oriented disconnected smoothing to the R-crossing pair
(\overrightarrow{a^{n}},\overrightarrow{b})
, we get

(7.4)

f(a^{n})+f(b)\geq f(a^{n}b).

We next show that

(7.5)

nf(a)+f(b)\geq f(a^{n}b)

for
n\geq 1
, by induction on
n
.
Indeed, the base case is true by oriented connected smoothing, and the
induction follows from

nf(a)+f(b)\geq f(a^{n-1}b)+f(a)\geq f(a^{n}b)

where in the first step we used the induction step and in the second we used the fact that the pair
(\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{a^{n-1}b})
is R-crossing by Lemma
4.15
.

Finally, by Lemma
4.15
, the pair
(\overrightarrow{B},\overrightarrow{a^{n}b})

is R-crossing for all
n\geq 1
, and hence, by oriented disconnected
smoothing,

(7.6)

f(B)+f(a^{n}b)\geq f(a^{n}).

Combining Eqs. (
7.4
), and (
7.6
),
dividing by
n
and taking the limit when
n
goes to infinity, we get

F(a)=\lim_{n^{+}}\frac{f(a^{n}b)}{n}
. Using Eq. (
7.5
)
we get
f(a)\geq F(a)
. Thus,
F(a)=f(a)
, and hence
f
satisfies stability since
F
clearly does.
∎

Remark 7.9
.

If we modify the statements of
Propositions
7.1

and
7.2
to refer to
C
-quasi-smoothing (for fixed

C>0
), then the proofs
do not
go through. For example, in the
induction from connected to disconnected
oriented smoothing, inequality Eq. (
7.3
) would
have to be replaced by

f([a^{2n}b^{2n}])\geq 2nf([ab])-Cn.

Following the same chain of inequalities that prove the induction does
not yield the desired inequality, since one accumulates one extra
-C

factor. We suspect the modified statement for quasi-smoothing is not
true.

8.
Special classes of geodesic currents

In this section we characterize special classes of geodesic currents as curve functionals, such as filling currents, hyperbolic metrics, measured laminations, integral multi-curves, or currents dual to embedded graphs.

8.1.
Filling currents

Using a result of Burger, Iozzi, Parreau, and Pozzetti, we can give a
characterization of filling geodesic currents.

Corollary 8.1
.

Let
S
a closed surface,

\mu
a geodesic current and
f
the functional on curves associated
to
\mu
, i.e., so that
\mu_{f}=\mu
. The following subsets are in
bijection.

(1)

The set of filling currents (Definition
3.21
).

(2)

The set of functionals
f\colon\mathcal{C}^{+}(S)\to\mathbb{R}
which satisfy symmetry, oriented smoothing,
additivity, and stability, and so that their continuous extension to geodesic currents is positive.

(3)

The set of curve functionals
f\colon\mathcal{C}^{+}(S)\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying symmetry, oriented smoothing, additivity,
and stability for which their systole

\sys(f)\coloneqq\inf\{f(C):C\in\mathcal{C}^{+}(S),\ C\mbox{ non-trivial}\}

is
positive.

Proof.

These equivalences follow from Theorem
A′
and
[
16
, Theorem 1.3]
.
∎

8.2.
Multi-curves

We say that a weighted multi-curve
M
is
integral

if its a weighted multi-curve whose weights are integers. We say that
M
is a
half-integral
multi-curve if its weights are in
\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}
. Note that, by definition, all integral multi-curves are also half-integral.

We have the following criterion for half-integral multi-curves.

Corollary 8.2
.

Let
f
be a curve functional satisfying the hypotheses of Theorem
A
such that
f(C)\in\mathbb{Z}
for every
C\in\mathcal{C}^{+}(S)
. Then
f
is dual to a half-integral multi-curve
\mu=\frac{1}{2}M
.
Moreover,
[\vec{M}]=0
in
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-homology.

Proof.

By Theorem
A
there exists a geodesic current
\mu
so that

i(\mu,C)=f(C)

for every closed curve
C
.
We must show
\mu
is a half-integral multi-curve. By the same argument in the proof of
[
63
, Théorème 1]
, there exists a family of rectangles of geodesics
\mathcal{R}
with dense endpoints in
\partial_{\infty}S
whose measure can be approximated by a sequence of one-half of the difference of sums of intersection numbers. That is, for every
R\in\mathcal{R}
, there exist sequences
A_{n},B_{n},C_{n},D_{n}\in\mathcal{C}^{+}(S)
so that

\mu(R)=\frac{1}{2}\lim_{n^{+}}i(\mu,B_{n})+i(\mu,C_{n})-i(\mu,A_{n})-i(\mu,D_{n}).

Since the expression in the limit is one-half of an integer number for every
n
, for
n
large enough the sequence must be constant (by discreteness of
\mathbb{Z}
).
Hence,
\mu(R)
is one-half of an integer for every
R\in\mathcal{R}
. (For more details about this last argument, see
[
43
]
.)
By
[
17
, Proposition 4.12]
, it follows that
2\mu
must be an integral multi-curve
M
, and hence
\mu=\frac{1}{2}M
is a half-integral multi-curve, for some integral multi-curve
M
.
We furthermore claim that
[\vec{M}]=0
in
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-homology.
Indeed, suppose there exists a simple closed curve
C
so that its
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-homology class

[C]\in H^{1}(S,\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
satisfies
\langle\vec{M},\vec{C}\rangle=1\mod 2
, where
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
denotes the algebraic intersection number. Since algebraic and geometric intersection are equal modulo 2, we have
i(\mu,C)\notin\mathbb{Z}
, a contradiction.
∎

Remark 8.3
.

There are examples of half-integral multi-curves which are not integral yet have integral intersection numbers.
For example, let
\mu
be the geodesic current supported on a
separating simple closed curve. Then
\mu/2
has an integral
length spectrum but is not an integral multi-curve.
For more involved examples, there are certain word-lengths (whose marked length spectrum is integral) that are
dual to a half-integral multi-curve
[
30
, Theorem 1.1]
. See also Section
8.3
.

It is easy to see that if
M=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}C_{i}
, where
\lambda_{i}\geq 0
are non-negative real numbers, then for every closed curve
C
, there exist integer numbers
n_{i}
so that

(8.1)

i(M,C)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}n_{i}.

i.e.,
\{i(\mu,C)\}_{\mathcal{C}(S)}
is contained in a finite rank subgroup of
\mathbb{R}
. In this case, we say
\mu
has
finite rank spectrum
.
This property does not characterize weighted multi-curves, since certain negatively curved metrics that satisfy this condition can be constructed (see
[
1
]
).
Hence, is natural to ask the following two questions.

Question 8.4
.

What is the subspace of geodesic currents with finite rank spectrum?

This last question came up in discussions between Meenakshy Jyothis and the first author.

Question 8.5
.

What conditions on the (marked) length spectrum characterize weighted multi-curves?

The last question was originally posed by Viveka Erlandsson to the first author.

8.3.
Embedded graphs

Related to multi-curves, we also obtain geodesic currents dual to stable lengths associated to embedded graphs in surfaces.

Let
\iota\colon\mathcal{G}\to S
be an
embedded graph
, i.e., an embedding of a finite graph in
S
that is filling, in the sense that the complementary regions are disks, or equivalently
i_{*}
is surjective on
\pi_{1}
.
Endow
\mathcal{G}
with a length metric
g
. Then any closed multi-curve
C
on
S
can be homotoped so that it factors through
\mathcal{G}
, in many different ways. Let
\ell_{\mathcal{G}}(C)
be the length of the smallest
multi-curve
D
on
\mathcal{G}
so that
\iota(D)
is homotopic to
C
. It is easy to see that this length is realized and is positive.

Theorem 8.6
.

Let
\iota\colon\mathcal{G}\to S
be an embedded graph. Then
\ell_{\mathcal{G}}
is dual to a geodesic current
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}
.

Proof.

In
[
55
, 4.4]
we show that such lengths are in
\mathrm{AConvex}(S)
, hence by Theorem
A
they have a dual geodesic current.
∎

If furthermore
\mathcal{G}
is an embedded graph with the edge-metric on
\pi_{1}(S)
(i.e., each edge has weight 1),
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}
must be
1/2
of an integral multi-curve. This generalizes the main result in
[
30
]
, where Erlandsson finds that word-metric arising from
simple generating set
are dual to 1/2 of an integral multi-curve dual. Such generating sets correspond to choosing
\mathcal{G}
to be a rose graph with only one vertex and edges of length 1.

Corollary 8.7
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be an embedded graph with the edge-metric on
\pi_{1}(S)
(i.e., each edge has weight 1). Then
\ell_{\mathcal{G}}
is dual to a geodesic current
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}
which is
1/2
of an integral multi-curve.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{G}
is equipped with the edge-metric,
\ell_{\mathcal{G}}(C)\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Hence, Corollary
8.2
implies the result.
∎

Figure 8.1
.
Example of embedded graph on the torus which does not arise as a simple generating set.

Example 8.8

(Embedded graph but not simple generating set)
.

As an example of an embedded graph which does not arise as a simple generating set, consider a square punctured torus
S^{\prime}
with an embedded two-vertex graph with four edges,
r,g,c,b
(for red, green, cyan, blue), see Figure
8.1
. Since it has more than one vertex, it does not arise from a simple generating set in the sense of Erlandsson.
Indeed, we have

[x]=[rB]=[gC],\qquad[y]=[rG]=[bC]

and thus

\ell([x])=2,\qquad\ell([y])=2.

Moreover,

\ell([xy])=\ell([(rB)(bC)])=\ell([rC])=2,\qquad\ell([xY])=\ell([(gC)(cB)])=\ell([gB])=2.

This phenomenon does not occur for any of the simple generating sets of
\pi_{1}(S^{\prime})\simeq F_{2}
, namely

\{x^{\pm 1},y^{\pm 1}\},\quad\{x^{\pm 1},(xy)^{\pm 1}\},\quad\{x^{\pm 1},(xY)^{\pm 1}\},\quad\{y^{\pm 1},(xY)^{\pm 1}\},\quad\{y^{\pm 1},(xY)^{\pm 1}\},

nor for the analogous generating sets obtained by acting diagonally by the mapping class group.
Indeed, in none of these cases are
x
,
y
,
xy
,
xY
the shortest
elements in
\pi_{1}
with equal length.

Even though this example corresponds to a punctured surface and hence does not fall in the scope of our theorem, one can produce similar examples in genus 2 from this one.

We note that our result says nothing concrete about the dual multi-curve, whereas Erlandsson’s result explicitly constructs it in the specific case of simple generating sets. It would be interesting to describe these multi-curves in general.

8.4.
Length metrics and intersection domination

Let
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in\Curr(S)
. Say
\mu_{1}\leq\mu_{2}
, or
\mu_{2}

dominates

\mu_{1}
, if
i(\mu_{1},C)\leq i(\mu_{2},C)
for all
C\in\mathcal{C}^{+}(S)
.

As an immediate application of Theorem
A
, we investigate how inequalities between intersection numbers of curves constrain the dual currents associated with metrics on the surface.

For example, we answer positively the following question at the end of
[
62
]
.

Question 8.9

(Neumann-Coto, 2001)
.

Let
C_{1},C_{2}
be two multi-curves. If
C_{2}
dominates
C_{1}
, does it follows that
\ell_{g}(C_{1})\leq\ell_{g}(C_{2})
for all Riemannian metrics
g
on
S
?

In fact, give a much stronger result (see Corollary
8.11
below).

First, we show that any length induced by a metric (in fact, by a pseudometric) on
S
has a dual geodesic current.

We first recall the definition of a length induced by a pseudometric on
S
. See
[
13
, Chapter I.1]
for a definition in the context of metrics. A
pseudometric space
is a pair
(X,d)
, where
X
is a set and
d\colon X\times X\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is a function satisfying, for all
x,y,z\in X
,

d(x,x)=0,\quad d(x,y)=d(y,x),\quad d(x,z)\leq d(x,y)+d(y,z).

The
d
-length

\ell(c)
of a continuous path
c\colon[a,b]\to X

is defined as

(8.2)

\ell(c)\coloneqq\sup_{a=t_{0}\leq t_{1}\leq\cdots\leq t_{n}=b}\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}d(c(t_{i}),c(t_{i+1})),

where the supremum is taken over all possible partitions of
[a,b]
(no bound on
n
), with

a=t_{0}\leq t_{1}\leq\cdots t_{n}=b
.
The length of a path
c
is either a non-negative number or it is infinite.

Let
\mathcal{P}=(t_{0},\dots,t_{n})
with
t_{0}\leq\cdots\leq t_{n}
be an arbitrary partition of
I
, and let
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}=(t_{0}^{\prime},\dots,t_{m}^{\prime})
be a refinement of the partition
\mathcal{P}
. By the triangle inequality, for every such partition we have

\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}d(\gamma(t_{i}),\gamma(t_{i+1}))\;\leq\;\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}d(\gamma(t_{j}^{\prime}),\gamma(t_{j+1}^{\prime})).

We say
a concrete curve
c
is
d
-rectifiable
if its
d
-length is finite.
Consider now a pseudometric
(X,d)
on
S
.
For a curve
C
on
S
, we define the
d
-length
of
C
as

\ell_{d}(C)\coloneqq\inf_{\gamma\in C}\ell_{d}(\gamma)

where
\gamma
runs over all the concrete curves in the equivalence class of
C
.
We extend this definition of multi-curves linearly.
We call this the
length induced by
d
.
If there exists at least a
d
-rectifiable concrete curve representative in curve
C
, then
\ell_{d}
induces a curve functional. This is the case, for example, when
(X,d)
is a
length pseudometric
on
S
, i.e.,

d
is given by the infimum of lengths of paths
[
13
, Chapter I.2, Definition 3.1]
. Precisely, this means

d(x,y)=\inf_{\gamma}\{\ell_{d}(\gamma):\gamma(0)=x,\gamma(1)=y,\gamma:[0,1]\to X\}.

After these preliminaries, we can state and prove the following corollary of Theorem
A
.

Corollary 8.10
.

Let
d
be a pseudometric on
S
, with induced length on curves
\ell_{d}
inducing a curve functional. Then there exists a geodesic current
\mu_{d}
dual to
\ell_{d}
.

Proof.

Additivity is satisfied by definition of
\ell_{d}
on multi-curves. It is clear that
\ell_{d}
is a symmetric curve functional, since
d
is symmetric.
The key point is to check that
\ell_{d}
satisfies smoothing.
Let
C
be a multicurve with an essential crossing
x
, and let
C^{\prime}
be obtained by smoothing
C
at
x
.

Fix
\varepsilon>0
. Since
\ell_{d}
is a curve functional,
\ell_{d}(C)<\infty
for every multi-curve
C
. By definition of
\ell_{d}(C)
, there exists a concrete representative
\gamma
of
C
with an essential crossing
x^{\prime}
of the same homotopy type as
x
such that

\ell_{d}(\gamma)\leq\ell_{d}(C)+\varepsilon.

In particular,
\gamma
is
d
-rectifiable.
Let
\gamma^{\prime}\in C^{\prime}
be the concrete multi-curve obtained by smoothing
\gamma
at
x^{\prime}
.

By abuse of notation, let
\gamma\colon I\to X
be a continuous
d
-rectifiable concrete curve parameterizing the concrete multi-curve
\gamma
where
I
is a finite union of intervals. Let
t^{\prime}\neq t^{\prime\prime}
be points in (possibly different components of)
I
such that
\gamma(t^{\prime})=\gamma(t^{\prime\prime})=x^{\prime}
correspond to the essential crossing. Let
\gamma^{\prime}\colon I^{\prime}\to X
be a continuous parametrization of the
d
-rectifiable concrete curve
\gamma^{\prime}
such that

\gamma(I)=\gamma^{\prime}(I^{\prime})

and
I^{\prime}
is another finite union of intervals.

Let
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
be the refinement of
\mathcal{P}
obtained by inserting
t^{\prime}
and
t^{\prime\prime}
.

Taking the supremum over all partitions, we obtain

\displaystyle\ell_{d}(\gamma)=\ell_{d}(\gamma^{\prime}).

By definition of
\ell_{d}(C^{\prime})
as an infimum over representatives of
C^{\prime}
, we have

\ell_{d}(C^{\prime})\leq\ell_{d}(\gamma^{\prime})=\ell_{d}(\gamma)\leq\ell_{d}(C)+\varepsilon.

Since
\varepsilon>0
was arbitrary, it follows that

\ell_{d}(C^{\prime})\leq\ell_{d}(C),

so
\ell_{d}
satisfies smoothing.
Thus, by Theorem
A
, there exists a geodesic current
\mu_{d}
dual to
\ell_{d}
.
∎

Corollary 8.11
.

Let
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}
be two geodesic currents. If
\mu_{2}
dominates
\mu_{1}
, then

\ell_{d}(\mu_{1})\leq\ell_{d}(\mu_{2})
for all length pseudometrics
d
on
S
.

Proof.

By Corollary
8.10
, for every length pseudometric
d
on
S
, there exist a dual geodesic current
\mu_{d}
for the induced curve functional
\ell_{d}
.
Approximating the current
\mu_{d}
by weighted multi-curves and using continuity of intersection number on currents the result follows.
∎

We continue with some remarks.

Remark 8.12
.

Given a geodesic current
\mu
and a choice of hyperbolic metric
\Sigma
on
S
, there is an induced invariant pseudometric on
\widetilde{\Sigma}
defined via

d_{\mu}^{\Sigma}(x,y)=\frac{1}{2}\left(\mu(G[x,y))+\mu(G(y,x])\right).

See
[
16
, Section 4]
and
[
26
]
. This pseudometric is
straight
(
[
16
, Proposition 4.1]
) in the sense that if
x,y,z\in l
for any
l\in G(\Sigma)
, and
x<y<z
in the natural order, then

d_{\mu}^{\Sigma}(x,z)=d_{\mu}^{\Sigma}(x,y)+d_{\mu}^{\Sigma}(y,z).

(The
d_{\mu}^{\Sigma}
pseudometric need not be continuous with respect to the topology of
\Sigma
.)
Hence, Corollary
8.10
shows that for every invariant
pseudometric
d
on
\widetilde{\Sigma}
inducing a curve functional, there
exists a straight invariant pseudometric
d_{\mu}^{\Sigma}
on

\widetilde{\Sigma}
so that
\ell_{d_{\mu}^{\Sigma}}(C)=\ell_{d}(C)
for every
closed curve
C
.

Remark 8.13
.

Suppose
(X,d)
is a length pseudometric structure on
S
. Let
\widetilde{d}
be the lift of the metric
d
to the universal cover
\widetilde{X}
of
X
, where
\pi_{1}(X)
acts by isometries. Then we have the equality
[
15
, 8.3.13]

\ell_{d}(C)=\inf_{x\in\widetilde{X}}\widetilde{d}(x,gx)\eqqcolon\tau_{\widetilde{d}}(g),

where
[g]=C
. The quantity
\tau_{\widetilde{d}}(g)
is the
\widetilde{d}
-translation length
of
g
.
From the fact that
\ell_{d}
satisfies smoothing (Proposition
7.8
), it follows that
\ell_{d}(C^{n})=n\ell_{d}(C)
, and hence,

\ell_{d}(C)=\lim\frac{1}{n}\ell_{d}(C^{n})=\lim\frac{1}{n}\inf_{x\in\widetilde{X}}\widetilde{d}(x,g^{n}x)=\lim\frac{1}{n}\widetilde{d}(x,g^{n}x)\eqqcolon L_{\widetilde{d}}(g)

for every
x\in\widetilde{X}
, and every
g\in\pi_{1}(S)
so that
[g]=C
.
The quantity
L_{\widetilde{d}}(g)
is the
\widetilde{d}
-stable length
of
g
.
The equality between translation length and stable length does not hold in general even in the setting of geodesic Gromov hyperbolic spaces
[
23
, Proposition 6.4]
.

8.5.
Measured laminations

We will now characterize measured laminations among curve functionals.

We will need a relation about the measure of sets intersecting pairs of geodesic segments.

We introduce the following notation. Given two half-closed half-open
geodesics segments
[x,w)
and
[y,z)
, let
\mu(G([x,w),[y,z)))
be the set of bi-infinite geodesics in
G(S)
intersecting
[x,w)
and
[y,z)
transversely. We will refer to sets of this type as
double transversals
. Given a geodesic segment
[x,y)
, a geodesic
\gamma
intersects
x
transversely if
x\in\gamma
and
[x,y)\nsubseteq\gamma
.

Let
x,y,z,w\in\widetilde{\Sigma}
appear counter-clockwise as vertices of an
embedded convex geodesic quadrilateral on
\widetilde{\Sigma}
.

Lemma 8.14
.

Given the setup described above, we have

\mu(G([x,w),[y,z)))+\mu(G((x,w],(y,z]))=\mu(G[x,z))+\mu(G[w,y))-\mu(G([w,z))-\mu(G([x,y)).

Proof.

This is
[
26
, Lemma 6.2]
.
∎

We also need the following hyperbolic geometry lemma, about
hyperbolic parallelograms
(Appendix
A
). It can be seen as a refinement of
[
61
, Lemma 5.6]
.

Consider Figure
8.2
.

Proposition 8.15
.

Consider two elements
a,b\in\pi_{1}(S)
with hyperbolic axes
\overrightarrow{a}
and
\overrightarrow{b}
that intersect at a point
O
, and translation lengths
\ell(a),\ell(b)
.
We define the following four points:

•

P
is the point on
\overrightarrow{a}
obtained by moving forward from
O
by distance
\ell(a)/2
;

•

Q
is the point on
\overrightarrow{b}
obtained by moving backward from
O
by distance
\ell(b)/2
;

•

Q^{\prime}
is the point on
\overrightarrow{b}
obtained by moving forward from
O
by distance
\ell(b)/2
;

•

P^{\prime}
is the point on
\overrightarrow{a}
obtained by moving backward from
O
by distance
\ell(a)/2
.

Then:

(1)

The segment
(Q,P]
is on the line
\overrightarrow{ab}
and has length
\ell(ba)/2
.

(2)

The segment
(P^{\prime},Q^{\prime}]
is on the line
\overrightarrow{ba}
and has length
\ell(ba)/2
.

(3)

The segment
(Q^{\prime},P]
is on the line
\overrightarrow{bA}
and has length
\ell(bA)/2
.

(4)

The segment
(P^{\prime},Q]
is on the line
\overrightarrow{Ab}
and has length
\ell(Ab)/2
.

Furthermore,
G((Q,P])\cup a\cdot G((P^{\prime},Q^{\prime}])
is a Borel fundamental domain for the
action of
\langle ab\rangle
on
G(\overrightarrow{ab})
.
Similarly,
G((P,Q^{\prime}])\cup b\cdot G((Q,P^{\prime}])
is a Borel fundamental domain for
the action of
\langle bA\rangle
on
G(\overrightarrow{bA})
.

Figure 8.2
.
Hyperbolic geometry of the smoothing between two distinct curves.

Proof.

We prove the first case.
For
z
any of the five points in the statement, let
r_{z}
be
the hyperbolic rotation by angle
\pi
fixing
z
. Then
a
, as
acting on the hyperbolic plane, is
r_{P}r_{O}
, while
b=r_{O}r_{Q}
.
(Recall that a composition
r_{z}r_{w}
of two rotations by
\pi
is a
translation on the line
\overleftrightarrow{zw}
by twice the
distance between
z
and
w
.)
It follows that
ab=r_{P}(r_{O})^{2}r_{Q}=r_{P}r_{Q}
, from which the statement about
(Q,P]

follows. The other cases are similar.

For the assertion on fundamental domains, we note that the segment

(P^{\prime},Q^{\prime}]
on the axis of
\overrightarrow{ba}
, is sent by
a
to a segment

(P,R]
, where
R=a(Q^{\prime})
appears forward of
P
in according to the
orientation of
\overrightarrow{ab}
, and the segment
(Q,R]
is a fundamental
domain for the action of
ab
on its axis
\overrightarrow{ab}
. Thus
\{\overrightarrow{ab}\}\times G((Q,R])=\{\overrightarrow{ab}\}\times(G((Q,P])\cup a\cdot G((P^{\prime},Q^{\prime}]))
is a fundamental domain for the action of
ab
on

\DG(\Sigma)
, as desired.
The other claim is similar.
∎

Using it, we characterize measured laminations among curve
functionals.
We start with a lemma, that first requires defining some sets of interest.

Given elements
a,b\in\pi_{1}(S)
which R-cross,
consider the hyperbolic parallelogram
H
as in Proposition
8.15
, bounded by the vertices
Q,P,P^{\prime},Q^{\prime}
.
Consider the sets

•

R_{a,b}^{+}\coloneqq G([Q,P),[P^{\prime},Q^{\prime}))
, and
(R_{a,b}^{\perp})^{+}\coloneqq G([P^{\prime},Q)\times[Q^{\prime},P))
,

•

R_{a,b}^{-}\coloneqq G((Q,P],(P^{\prime},Q^{\prime}])
, and
(R_{a,b}^{\perp})^{-}\coloneqq G((P^{\prime},Q]\times(Q^{\prime},P])

consisting, respectively, of geodesics crossing from the segment of
H
contained in
\overrightarrow{ab}
to the open segment of
H
contained in
\overrightarrow{ba}
, and from the segment of
H
contained in
\overrightarrow{a^{-1}b}
to the one in
\overrightarrow{ba^{-1}}
.
Consider also the boxes of geodesics:

•

B_{a,b}^{+}=(a^{+},b^{-}]\times[a^{-},b^{+})
and
(B_{a,b}^{\perp})^{+}=(b^{-},a^{-}]\times[b^{+},a^{+})
,

•

B_{a,b}^{-}=[a^{+},b^{-})\times(a^{-},b^{+}]
and
(B_{g,h}^{\perp})^{-}=[b^{-},a^{-})\times(b^{+},a^{+}]
.

We have
R_{a,b}^{\pm}\subset B_{a,b}^{\pm}
and
(R_{a,b}^{\perp})^{\pm}\subset(B_{a,b}^{\perp})^{\pm}
.

We will resort to this setup several times in the proofs below.
The following lemma says that when taking large powers of
a
and
b
, the corresponding rectangles approximate boxes of geodesics.

Figure 8.3
.
Rectangle
R_{3}^{+}
and box
B_{a,b}^{+}
.

Lemma 8.16
.

Given elements
a,b\in\pi_{1}(S)
which R-cross,
let
B_{a,b}^{\perp},(B_{a,b}^{\pm})^{\perp},R_{a,b}^{\pm},(R_{a,b}^{\pm})^{\perp}
as above. Suppose
\mu
is an arbitrary geodesic current.

For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
R_{n}^{\pm}\coloneqq(R_{a^{n},b^{n}})^{\pm}
and
(R_{n}^{\perp})^{\pm}\coloneqq(R_{a^{n},b^{n}}^{\perp})^{\pm}
.
Then:

(1)

R_{n}^{\pm}\subset R_{n+1}^{\pm}
, and
\bigcup_{n\geq 1}R_{n}^{\pm}=B_{a,b}^{\pm}
and
(R_{n}^{\perp})^{\pm}\subset(R_{n+1}^{\perp})^{\pm}
and
\bigcup_{n\geq 1}(R_{n}^{\perp})^{\pm}=(B_{a,b}^{\perp})^{\pm}
.

(2)

We have
\lim_{n}\mu(R_{n}^{\pm})\to\mu(B_{a,b}^{\pm})
and
\lim_{n}\mu((R_{n}^{\perp})^{\pm})=\mu((B_{g,h}^{\perp})^{\pm})
.

(3)

If we let
c_{n}=a^{n}b^{-n}
and
d_{n}=a^{n}b^{n}
, we have

(8.3)

\mu(R_{n}^{+})+\mu(R_{n}^{-})=f(a^{n})+f(b^{n})-f(c_{n}),

and

(8.4)

\mu((R_{n}^{\perp})^{+})+\mu((R_{n}^{\perp})^{-})=f(a^{n})+f(b^{n})-f(d_{n}).

(4)

We have
\mu(\partial B_{a,b})^{\pm})=0
and
\mu(\partial(B_{a,b}^{\perp})^{\pm})=0
.

Proof.

Item (1) is by construction of
R_{n}^{\pm}
.
Item (2) follows by Item (1) and lower semi-continuity of measures.
Item (3) follows by Lemma
8.14
and Proposition
8.15
. Finally, Item (4) follows by Lemma
3.19
, since the vertices of the boxes
(B_{a,b})^{\pm}
and
(B_{a,b}^{\perp})^{\pm}
are the endpoints of the elements
a,b
, and the geodesics
\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{b}
are not contained in either of these boxes.
∎

Recall that a geodesic current is a measured lamination if and only if for every open box of geodesics
B
, we have
\min\{\mu(B),\mu(B^{\perp})\}=0
(see, e.g.,
[
17
, Proposition 2.1]
).
We now prove Theorem
D
, whose statement we recall.

Theorem 8.17
.

Let
f\colon\pi_{1}(S)\to\mathbb{R}
be a function and set
\overline{f}(g)\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}\bigl(f(g)+f(g^{-1})\bigr).

Then there exists a measured lamination

\mu_{\overline{f}}
on
S
such that

i(\mu_{\overline{f}},[g])=\overline{f}(g)\quad\text{for every }g\in\pi_{1}(S),

if and only if
f
satisfies the conditions of
Theorem
A′

and disconnected max-smoothing:

(8.5)

\displaystyle f(a)+f(b)

\displaystyle=\max\{f(ab),f(aB)\}

\displaystyle\mbox{ if }(a,b)\mbox{ are }\mbox{crossing}.

Proof.

Suppose first
f
satisfies the conditions in the hypotheses.
By Theorem
A′
, there exists a geodesic current
\mu_{f}
so that
f(C)=i(\mu_{f},C)
for all closed curves
C
.
It remains to show that
\mu_{f}
is a measured lamination.
Given elements
a,b\in\pi_{1}(S)
which R-cross,
consider the hyperbolic parallelogram
H
at the center of
Figure
8.2
, and the notation and setup of Lemma
8.16
.
By the hypothesis of max-smoothing, for every
n
, we have
\mu((R_{n}^{\perp})^{+})=\mu((R_{n}^{\perp})^{-})=0
or
\mu(R_{n}^{+})=\mu(R_{n}^{-})=0
, by Eq. (
8.3
) and Eq. (
8.4
).
Since each sequence of sets
(R_{n}^{\pm})
and
(R_{n}^{\perp})^{\pm}
is nested, if
\mu(R_{1}^{\pm})\neq 0
, then
\mu(R_{n}^{\pm})\neq 0
for all
n\geq 1
and thus
\mu((R_{n}^{\perp})^{\pm})=0
for all
n\geq 1
.
Hence,
\mu(B_{a,b}^{\pm})=0
or
\mu((B_{a,b}^{\perp})^{\pm})=0
.
By density of endpoints of closed geodesics, these boxes have vertices on a dense set of points of
\partial_{\infty}S
.
The pairs of the diagonal endpoints of the family of boxes
B_{a,b}^{\pm}
(as
a,b
runs over crossing pairs in
\pi_{1}(S)
) are dense in the space of geodesics. Hence, we can find sequences of crossing pairs
a_{n},b_{n}\in\pi_{1}(S)
so that if
B_{n}^{\pm}\coloneqq B_{a_{n},b_{n}}^{\pm}
, we have
B_{n}^{\pm}\subset B_{n+1}^{\pm}
,
(B_{n+1}^{\perp})^{\pm}\subset(B_{n}^{\perp})^{\pm}
,
\bigcup_{n}B_{n}^{\pm}=B
, and
\bigcap_{n}(B_{n}^{\perp})^{\pm}=B
.
Then, by lower and upper continuity of measures, respectively, we get
\lim_{n}\mu(B_{n}^{\pm})=\mu(B)
and
\lim_{n}\mu((B_{n}^{\perp})^{\pm})=\mu(B^{\perp})
. Hence,
\mu(B)=0
or
\mu(B^{\perp})=0
, and thus
\mu
is a measured lamination.
Now we prove the other implication, by proving the contra-positive.
If disconnected max-smoothing is
not
satisfied for the elements

a,b
, then by Equations (
8.3
)
and (
8.4
), both:

•

\mu(R_{a,b}^{+})>0
or
\mu(R_{a,b}^{-})>0
;

•

and

\mu((R_{a,b}^{\perp})^{+})>0
or
\mu((R_{a,b}^{\perp})^{-})>0

For every
\gamma\in R_{a,b}^{+}\cup R_{a,b}^{-}
and every
\gamma^{\prime}\in(R_{a,b}^{\perp})^{+}\cup(R_{a,b}^{\perp})^{-}
,
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
cross. Consequently, we have
i(\mu,\mu)>0
,
and hence
\mu
is not a measured lamination.
∎

The above result shows that if one knows
f
is dual to a geodesic
current, then it is only necessary to check disconnected max-smoothing
to conclude
f
is a measured lamination, i.e., one does not have to
check essential
self
-crossings of connected components of multi-curves. It is however true that intersection
number with a measured lamination also satisfies connected
max-smoothing.
We state this result along the lines of Theorem
A
.

Theorem 8.18
.

A curve functional
f
is dual to a measured lamination if and only if it satisfies additivity and the
max-smoothing property
:
For any essential crossing of a multi-curve
C
, we have

(8.6)

f\left(\vbox{\hbox{$\includegraphics[]{curves-0}$}}\right)=\max\left\{f\left(\vbox{\hbox{$\includegraphics[]{curves-1}$}}\right),f\left(\vbox{\hbox{$\includegraphics[]{curves-2}$}}\right)\right\}.

Proof.

Suppose
f
is a curve functional satisfying max-smoothing and additivity. Max-smoothing clearly implies smoothing, as well as disconnected max-smoothing. Thus, by Theorem
D
,
f
is dual to a measured lamination. On the other hand, if
f
is dual to a measured lamination, Theorem
D
implies disconnected max-smoothing. Connected max-smoothing follows from tracing through the proof of Proposition
7.2
, using the fact that
i(\mu,\cdot)
is continuous, symmetric, additive and stable, and noting that if
f
satisfies disconnected max-smoothing, all the inequalities become equalities in the proof of disconnected smoothing implies connected smoothing.
∎

The fact that measured laminations satisfy max-smoothing was proven by
the second author in
[
77
]
using
different techniques.

8.6.
Liouville currents

Bonahon gives an algebraic characterization for a geodesic current
\mu
to be a Liouville current
\mathcal{L}_{\Sigma}
for a marked hyperbolic structure
\Sigma\in\Teich(S)
, the
Teichmüller space
of
S
.

Theorem 8.19

(
[
10
, Theorem 13]
)
.

A geodesic current
\mu\in\Curr(S)
is
\mu=\mathcal{L}_{\Sigma}
for some
\Sigma
if and only if, for any four points
x,y,z,w\in\partial_{\infty}S
, we have

e^{-\mu([x,y]\times[z,w])}+e^{-\mu([y,z]\times[w,x])}=1.

Whether the endpoints of the intervals are included or excluded in the
above statement is immaterial, since it follows from the hypothesis that the current is non-atomic.

We use this to prove the hyperbolic part of Theorem
D
.

Theorem 8.20
.

Let
f\colon\pi_{1}(S)\to\mathbb{R}
be a function and set
\overline{f}(g)\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}\bigl(f(g)+f(g^{-1})\bigr).

Then there exists a hyperbolic Liouville current

\mu_{\overline{f}}
on
S
such that

i(\mu_{\overline{f}},[g])=\overline{f}(g)\quad\text{for every }g\in\pi_{1}(S),

if and only if
f
satisfies the conditions of
Theorem
A′

and whenever
(\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{b})
cross, we have

(8.7)

\displaystyle\lambda(a)\lambda(b)=\lambda(ab)+\lambda(aB),\text{ where }\lambda(g)\coloneqq 2\cosh\!\bigl(f(g)/2\bigr).

Proof.

The proof is similar to the lamination part.
If
\mu
is hyperbolic Liouville current then smoothing, additivity, stability follow from the fact these are necessary conditions for a geodesic current, as shown in Appendix
C.1
. The relation in Equation (
8.7
) follows from the relation between the hyperbolic lengths of the sides of a hyperbolic parallelogram (see Lemma
A.2
) applied to the hyperbolic parallelogram
H
at the center of
Figure
8.2
, and the fact that the hyperbolic Liouville current is the dual current for the hyperbolic length, by
[
10
, Proposition 14]
.

For the other direction, by the assumptions on
f
, there exists a
geodesic current
\mu_{f}
so that
f(C)=i(\mu_{f},C)
for every closed
curve
C
.
It remains to show that
\mu_{f}
is a Liouville current, which is where Equation (
8.7
) will play a role.
Given elements
a,b\in\pi_{1}(S)
which R-cross, consider the hyperbolic parallelogram
H
at the center of
Figure
8.2
, and the notation and setup of Lemma
8.16
.

Now, we compute

e^{\frac{-\mu(R_{n}^{+})-\mu(R_{n}^{-})}{2}}+e^{\frac{-\mu((R_{n}^{\perp})^{+})-\mu((R_{n}^{\perp})^{-})}{2}}=e^{\frac{-(A_{n}+B_{n})+C_{n}}{2}}+e^{\frac{-(A_{n}+B_{n})+D_{n}}{2}}=e^{\frac{-(A_{n}+B_{n})}{2}}(e^{\frac{C_{n}}{2}}+e^{\frac{D_{n}}{2}}).

On the other hand, when
n
goes to infinity, we have
e^{\frac{A_{n}}{2}}\sim\lambda(a_{n})
,
e^{\frac{B_{n}}{2}}\sim\lambda(b_{n})
,
e^{\frac{C_{n}}{2}}\sim\lambda(c_{n})
and
e^{\frac{D_{n}}{2}}\sim\lambda(d_{n})
, where
\sim
means that the ratio of the left hand side to the right hand side tends to 1 as
n
tends to infinity. Applying Equation (
8.7
) for
a_{n},b_{n}
we get

\lambda(a_{n})\lambda(b_{n})=\lambda(c_{n})+\lambda(d_{n}).

A computation shows
\lambda(c_{n})+\lambda(d_{n})\sim e^{\frac{C_{n}}{2}}+e^{\frac{D_{n}}{2}}
, and hence we get

e^{\frac{A_{n}}{2}}e^{\frac{B_{n}}{2}}\sim e^{\frac{C_{n}}{2}}+e^{\frac{D_{n}}{2}}.

Therefore, we have

\lim_{n\to\infty}e^{\frac{-\mu(R_{n}^{+})-\mu(R_{n}^{-})}{2}}+e^{\frac{-\mu((R_{n}^{\perp})^{+})-\mu((R_{n}^{\perp})^{-})}{2}}=1.

By Lemma
8.16
(2) we also get
\lim_{n}\mu(R_{n}^{\pm})=\mu(B_{a,b}^{\pm})
and
\lim_{n}\mu((R_{n}^{\perp})^{\pm})=\mu((B_{a,b}^{\perp})^{\pm})
.
Letting
B_{a,b}
and
B_{a,b}^{\perp}
denote the corresponding open versions of these boxes, we obtain the relation

e^{-\mu(B_{a,b})}+e^{-\mu(B_{a,b}^{\perp})}=1

for any intersecting pair
a,b\in\pi_{1}(S)
.
As in the proof of the lamination part of Theorem
D
, we use the density of
B_{a,b}
in the space of boxes of geodesics as
a,b
run over intersecting pairs in
\pi_{1}(S)
. Hence, given any open box
B
, we can find, for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
a sequence of intersecting pairs
a_{n},b_{n}\in\pi_{1}(S)
so that if we let
B_{n}\coloneqq B_{a_{n},b_{n}}
, we have
\lim_{n}\mu(B_{n})=\mu(B)
and
\lim_{n}\mu(B_{n}^{\perp})=\mu(B^{\perp})
. Specifically,

e^{-\mu(B)}+e^{-\mu(B^{\perp})}=1.

Therefore, Theorem
8.19
shows
\mu
is a Liouville current.
∎

9.
Skora’s theorem:
\mathbb{R}
-trees dual to measured laminations

As an application of Theorem
A
, we prove
Theorem
E
,
giving new
characterizations of small actions of surface groups on

\mathbb{R}
-trees and reproving a classical theorem of
Skora
[
74
]
.

9.1.
Finitely generated groups acting on real trees

Real trees.

A
real tree
or
\mathbb{R}
-tree
is a non-empty metric space
T
such that for
all
x,y\in T
, the intersection of all connected subsets containing
both
x
and
y
is isometric to the interval
[0,d(x,y)]
. This
intersection is denoted

[x,y]
, and called the
arc
connecting
x
and
y
.
When an arc is a singleton, we say it is
degenerate
, otherwise we say it is
non-degenerate
.
An equivalent way to characterize real trees is as geodesic metric
space
X
satisfying the
0
-hyperbolic Gromov point condition
:
for any four-tuple of points
x,y,z,w\in X
,
the maximum among the following three quantities appears (at least) twice:

d(x,z)+d(y,w),\quad d(x,y)+d(z,w),\quad d(x,z)+d(y,w).

A
ray
in a real tree is the image of an isometric embedding of
the ray
[0,\infty)
. If
p
is the image of
0
under this embedding,
the ray
emanates
from
p
. The
space of ends
of a metric
space
T
is the limit of the inverse system
\pi_{0}(T-B)
where
B

ranges over the closed and bounded subsets of
T
. Fix a point
p
in
an
\mathbb{R}
-tree
T
. For every end of
T
, there is a unique
ray
emanating from
p
that meets every component of the inverse system
corresponding to the end.
Hence, there is a one-to-one correspondence between
rays emanating from
p
and ends of
T
.

Given two distinct
ends of
T
, the corresponding rays meet in an interval with
p
as
endpoint. The union of these two rays minus the interior of their
intersection is a
line
in
T
. Conversely, given any line in
T
, the
two oppositely oriented rays emanating from any point in the line
determine two distinct ends of the tree, and these ends do not depend
on the choice of point. We will say this line
joins
the two
ends. It follows from the definition of real trees that there is exactly
one line connecting
any two distinct ends. We can alternately define a line directly as the image of an isometric map from
\mathbb{R}
.

Actions on real trees.
If
\Gamma
is a finitely generated
group, a

\Gamma
-tree
is an
\mathbb{R}
-tree
T
together with a group
action
\Gamma\curvearrowright T
by isometries.
There are two types of isometries of real trees: those with fixed
points, called
elliptic
, and those without fixed points, called

hyperbolic
.
Given
g\in\Gamma
, let
\|g\|\coloneqq\inf_{x\in T}d(x,gx)
, and
let
T_{g}\coloneqq\{x\in T:d(x,gx)=\|g\|\}
be the

characteristic set
of
g
; this is a closed, non-empty subtree
of
T
invariant under the action of
g
.
If
g
is elliptic, then
\|g\|=0
, and
T_{g}
is the
set of fixed points of
g
. The converse is also true: if
\lVert g\rVert=0
, then
g
has fixed points
[
25
, Section 1.3]
.

If
g
is hyperbolic, then
\|g\|>0
,
T_{g}
is isometric to the
real line, and
g
translates along
T_{g}
by distance
\|g\|
; in this
case we call
T_{g}
the
axis
of
g
.

The translation lengths of any
\Gamma
-tree can be characterized by the following relation, reminiscent of the
0
-hyperbolic condition.
Even though we will not use it in the proofs, it is helpful to keep it in mind;
it can be viewed as a simpler repackaging of axioms due to Culler and
Morgan
[
25
, Section 1.11]
, as proved by Parry (
[
67
, Equation (0.2)]
and
[
67
, Main Theorem]
).

Proposition 9.1
.

Let
\Gamma
be a finitely generated group and
f\colon\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}
be a conjugacy class invariant function.
Then
f
is the translation length function of
a
\Gamma
-tree if and only if for any pair
g,h\in\Gamma
, the maximum among the following three quantities appears (at least) twice:

(9.1)

\|g\|+\|h\|,\quad\|gh\|,\quad\|gH\|.

Proof.

The fact that the translation length of a
\Gamma
-tree
satisfies the condition in (
9.1
) follows from
more precise relations developed
in
[
25
,
68
]
, which we will write
as a sequence of lemmas below. Specifically, for hyperbolic pairs

(g,h)
, it follows from Lemma
9.5
; if
g
is
elliptic and
h
hyperbolic, by Lemma
9.7
; and, if
both are elliptic, by Lemma
9.8
.

The other implication follows by a deep result of Parry
[
67
]
. It is straightforward to show that the condition in Equation (
9.1
) implies the Culler-Morgan axioms assumed in the main characterization theorem of Parry, and, hence, it also
characterizes translation lengths of actions on real trees among all conjugacy class invariant functionals.
∎

A
\Gamma
-tree with translation length function

\|\cdot\|
is of one of four
types
[
25
, Page 583]
:

•

Global fixed point
:
\|\cdot\|
is zero on all of
\Gamma
; equivalently,
\Gamma
fixes a global point in
T
.

•

Fixed end:

\|\cdot\|
is non-trivial but zero on
[\Gamma,\Gamma]
;
equivalently
\Gamma
fixes an end of
T

[
25
, Theorem 2.2,
Corollary 2.3]
.

•

Dihedral:

\|\cdot\|
is non-trivial on
[\Gamma,\Gamma]
, but

\|[g,h]\|=0
for all
g,h
hyperbolic in
T
; equivalently

\Gamma
fixes a line of
T
inverting
orientation
[
25
, Theorem 2.4]
.

•

Irreducible:

\|[g,h]\|\neq 0
for some
g,h
hyperbolic in
T
.

A
reducible
action of
\Gamma
is one that is not irreducible,
i.e., it is fixed end, dihedral, or has a global fixed point.

We now recall and recast some classical results on
\mathbb{R}
-actions, drawing on
[
25
,
68
]
.

Consider two hyperbolic elements
g,h\in\Gamma
with axes in the
\mathbb{R}
-tree given respectively by
T_{g},T_{h}
. Let
\Delta\coloneqq T_{g}\cap T_{h}
. If
\Delta
is a non-degenerate interval (i.e., non-empty and not a singleton) we distinguish two types of overlap:

•

if
g
and
h
translate in the same direction along
\Delta
, we say
that they
overlap coherently
;

•

if
g
and
h
translate in opposite
directions along
\Delta
, we say that they
overlap incoherently
.

Following
[
25
, Section 3.2]
, we say that
g,h
form a
good pair
if

•

they are both hyperbolic;

•

their axes in the tree intersect and their overlap is a non-degenerate arc;

•

they overlap coherently; and

•

the length of
T_{g}\cap T_{h}
is strictly less than
\min\{\|g\|,\|h\|\}
.

Lemma 9.2

(
[
25
, Remark 3.3]
)
.

If
g,h
are hyperbolic and
T_{g},T_{h}
meet in an arc of positive but
finite length, then there exist integers
m>0
and
n
so that
g^{m}

and
h^{n}

form a good pair of isometries.

Proof sketch.

The sign of
n
is chosen so that the overlap is coherent, and the magnitudes of
m,n
are chosen
to ensure
m\|g\|
and
n\|h\|
are both greater than the length of
T_{g}\cap T_{h}
.
∎

We next recall a useful criterion that implies reducibility.

Lemma 9.3

(
[
25
, Lemma 2.1]
)
.

If there exists a hyperbolic element
g\in\Gamma
so that for all hyperbolic
h\in\Gamma
we have
T_{g}\cap T_{h}\neq\emptyset
, then the action is reducible.

Next, we show that good pairs yield pairs of non-intersecting
hyperbolic elements.

Lemma 9.4
.

For any
\Gamma
-tree, if
g,h
form a good pair, then
gH
and
Hg
are hyperbolic isometries of
T
and
T_{gH},T_{Hg}
do not intersect.

Proof.

This is contained in the proof
of
[
25
, Lemma 3.4]
. See Figure
9.1
, where the distance
\Delta
is strictly positive and the position of the relevant points follow from the identities
Hg\cdot(G\cdot y)=H\cdot y
and
gH\cdot(h\cdot x)=g\cdot x
.
∎

Figure 9.1
.
Axes of
gH,Hg
in
T
are disjoint when
g,h
form a good pair

We now look at the relative positions of the characteristic sets of a pair of elements
g,h\in\Gamma
, distinguishing the cases that arise in Proposition
9.1
.

Lemma 9.5
.

Let
g,h
be hyperbolic isometries with axes
T_{g}
and
T_{h}
.
We have the following cases, depending on the relative position of
T_{g},T_{h}
.

(1)

(Intersecting axes)

T_{g}\cap T_{h}\neq\emptyset
if and only if
\|g\|+\|h\|=\max\{\|gh\|,\|gH\|\}
, as shown in Figure
9.2
.
Moreover,

(a)

T_{g}\cap T_{h}
is a single point iff
\|gh\|=\|gH\|=\|g\|+\|h\|
;

(b)

g,h
overlap coherently iff

\|g\|+\|h\|=\|gh\|>\|gH\|
. In this case,
T_{g}\cap T_{h}=T_{gh}\cap T_{hg}
; and

(c)

g,h
overlap incoherently iff

\|g\|+\|h\|=\|gH\|>\|gh\|
. In this case,
T_{g}\cap T_{h}=T_{gH}\cap T_{Hg}
.

(2)

(Disjoint axes)
T_{g}\cap T_{h}=\emptyset
iff

\|gh\|=\|gH\|=\|g\|+\|h\|+2d(T_{g},T_{h}),

as shown in Figure
9.3
.
In this case,
T_{gh},T_{g}
overlap coherently
in a segment of positive length and

T_{gh},T_{hg}
overlap incoherently on a segment of length

d(T_{g},T_{h})
.

Figure 9.2
.
Relative position of intersecting axes in a tree, from

[
68
, Fig. 3]
. See also
[
25
]
.

Figure 9.3
.
Relative position of disjoint axes in a tree, , from

[
68
, Fig. 2]
. See also
[
25
]
.

Proof.

The proof of the translation length equalities and inequalities of (1)
and (2),
as well as the equality
T_{g}\cap T_{h}=T_{gh}\cap T_{hg}

in case (1b), is in
[
25
, Sections 1.5, 1.8]
;
case (1c) is parallel.
The result of part (2) about
T_{gh},T_{g}
overlapping in a segment of positive length can be found in the proof of
[
25
, Theorem 2.7]
.
The statement about the axes
T_{gh},T_{hg}
overlapping incoherently in the parallel axes case is
[
25
, Remark 3.5]
.
∎

We will also use the following.

Lemma 9.6

(
[
25
, Lemma 6.2]
)
.

Let
T
be an arbitrary
\Gamma
-tree.
If
g,h
are hyperbolic isometries of
T
and satisfy
\|g\|+\|h\|=\|gh\|
(or
equivalently, overlap coherently or in a point), then for all positive integers
m,n
, we have

\|g^{m}h^{n}\|=m\|g\|+n\|h\|.

We will need analogous statements for pairs
g,h\in\Gamma
where one
or both group elements are allowed to be elliptic. These have been
analyzed in
[
68
]
. We state them here in the
form that will be most convenient for us, making
Proposition
9.1

more
apparent.

Lemma 9.7
.

Let
T
be a
\Gamma
-tree. Suppose
g
is elliptic and
h
is
hyperbolic. Then exactly one of the following holds depending on the
relative position of the axes
T_{h}
and
T_{ghG}
:

\begin{array}[]{c@{\qquad}c@{\qquad}c}\text{\emph{(1) coherent overlap or point;}}&\text{\emph{(2) incoherent overlap;}}&\text{\emph{(3) disjoint}}\\[5.16663pt]
\|gh\|=\|h\|\geq\|gH\|;&\|gH\|=\|h\|>\|gh\|;&\|gh\|=\|gH\|>\|h\|.\end{array}

Furthermore, case (3) happens exactly when
T_{g}
and
T_{h}
are
disjoint, and implies
T_{gh}
and
T_{hg}
overlap incoherently.

Proof.

The statement is a condensed version of
[
68
, Proposition 1.7]
, where we have omitted most specifics about relative positions of axes. The statement there is divided into two cases depending on the relative position of
T_{g},T_{h}
.
If
T_{g}\cap T_{h}\neq\emptyset
, then
I\coloneqq T_{h}\cap T_{ghG}
is a non-empty interval and contains
T_{g}\cap T_{h}
.
Then, the inequalities follow depending on the overlap between
T_{h}
and
T_{ghG}
.
If
T_{g}\cap T_{h}=\emptyset
, then the equality of lengths in
case (3) is stated explicitly
in
[
25
, Section 1.5]
. The fact that

T_{ghG}\cap T_{h}=\emptyset
in this same case follows
from
[
25
, Fact 1.7]
.
The last claim about case (3) follows from the analysis in
[
68
, Proposition 1.7(2)]
, in particular, Figure 8 therein. There it is shown that if
z
denotes the point in
T_{g}
closest to
T_{h}
, then
T_{hg}
contains the segment
[z,h(z)]
. Since
T_{g}=T_{G}
for any
g\in\Gamma
, a symmetric argument applied to
G
and
H
shows that
T_{HG}
contains the segment
[z,H(z)]
. In particular, if
y
denotes the closest point of
T_{h}
to
z
, both
T_{hg}
and
T_{HG}
contain the segment
[z,y]
, and overlap in that segment coherently. Hence,
hg
and
gh
overlap in that segment incoherently.

Since each case is exclusive, the triality depending on the relative position of
T_{h}
and
T_{ghG}
follows.
∎

Lemma 9.8
.

Let
T
be an arbitrary
\Gamma
-tree. If
g,h
are both elliptic
isometries, we have
\lVert gh\rVert=\lVert gH\rVert
and
\|gh\|>0
if and only if

T_{g}\cap T_{h}=\emptyset
. Moreover, if
T_{g}\cap T_{h}=\emptyset
,
then
T_{gh},T_{hg}
overlap incoherently along the shortest connecting arc between
T_{g}
and
T_{h}
.

Proof.

The statement is contained
in
[
68
, Proposition 1.8]
, except the equality

\|gh\|=\|gH\|
, and the statement about incoherent overlap, which we outline here. If
T_{g}\cap T_{h}\neq\emptyset
, then
\|gh\|=\|gH\|=0
. Otherwise, let
S=[x,y]
denote
the minimal segment connecting
T_{g}
and
T_{h}
; we already know
S\subset T_{gh}
by Paulin’s result. Since
T_{h}=T_{H}
, we also have
S\subset T_{gH}
. We therefore have

\lVert gh\rVert=d(y,gh(y))=d(y,g(y))=d(y,gH(y))=\lVert gH\rVert.

Finally, we prove the statement about incoherent overlap. Let
g(S)\cap S
be the subsegment
[x,z^{\prime}]\subset S
fixed by
g
, and
h(S)\cap S
the subsegment
[z,y]\subset S
fixed by
h
. By
[
68
, Figure 9]
, the subarc
[z,g(z)]=[z,gh(z)]\subset T_{gh}
and the subarc
[z^{\prime},h(z^{\prime})]=[z^{\prime},hg(z^{\prime})]\subset T_{hg}
overlap incoherently along the non-degenerate subsegment
[z,z^{\prime}]
.
∎

Finally, the following result allows us to convert incoherent overlaps into coherent ones.

Lemma 9.9

(
[
37
, Fait, page 854]
)
.

Let
T
be an arbitrary
\Gamma
-tree.
Let
g,h
be two hyperbolic elements of
\Gamma
so that

T_{ghG}
and
T_{h}
do not share a ray, and let
I
be a non-degenerate arc contained
in
T_{h}
. Then for large enough
k\geq 0
,
h^{k}gh^{k}
is hyperbolic
and its axis contains
I
, translating along the same direction as
h
.

We say that a
\Gamma
-tree
(\Gamma,T)
is
minimal
if
T
is
non-trivial and there is no proper subtree of
T
that is
\Gamma
-invariant.
If
\lVert\cdot\rVert
is non-zero then by

[
25
, Proposition 3.1]
there is a
unique minimal invariant subtree.
(If
\lVert\cdot\rVert
is zero then there
is a global fixed point and so the action is never minimal.)
The length function for the
restriction to this minimal invariant subtree is equal to the length
function of the original tree. Hence, without loss of generality, one
can always restrict to the action on the minimal invariant subtree
without changing the length functional. Note, however, that if the translation lengths of two
\Gamma
-trees are
equal, that does not necessarily imply the trees are equivariantly
isometric, but the exceptions all have fixed ends
[
25
, Theorem 3.7]
.

9.2.
Surface group actions on real trees

In this section, we assume
\Gamma=\pi_{1}(S)
. An
S
-tree
is
a
\mathbb{R}
-tree
T
with a minimal action of
\pi_{1}(S)
.
It is standard that a non-zero measured lamination
\lambda
defines a
(dual) irreducible
S
-tree (see
[
60
]
).
It follows from the construction that the translation length of

g\in\pi_{1}(S)
on

T_{\lambda}
is equal to

i(\lambda,[g])
, where
[g]
denotes the free homotopy class of a
closed curve associated to
g
.
Given a
S
-tree
T
, if there is a measured lamination
\lambda
and
\pi_{1}(S)
-equivariant isometry
\phi\colon T_{\lambda}\to T
, we say that
T
is
dual to

\lambda
.

Recall that for
\Sigma
a fixed hyperbolic structure on
S
, the
corresponding hyperbolic axis in
\widetilde{\Sigma}
is denoted
\overrightarrow{g}
;
be careful to distinguish this from the characteristic set
T_{g}
in
the action on
T
.

In checking whether an
S
-tree is dual to a measured
lamination, there are several relevant conditions on the action:

•

The action is irreducible, not dihedral or fixed end.

•

The action is
small
: the stabilizer of any
(non-degenerate) arc is virtually cyclic.

•

The action
preserves axis intersection
: for any pair

g,h\in\pi_{1}(S)
acting hyperbolically on
T
, if

\overrightarrow{g},\overrightarrow{h}
cross then
T_{g}\cap T_{h}\neq\emptyset
.

•

The action
preserves characteristic intersection
: for any pair

g,h\in\pi_{1}(S)
, if

\overrightarrow{g},\overrightarrow{h}
cross then
T_{g}\cap T_{h}\neq\emptyset
. (Note
that
g
and/or
h
may be elliptic in action on
T
.)

Morgan-Otal
[
59
]
showed that
S
-trees dual to measured laminations
satisfy the first three. They also showed that if an
S
-tree
is small and preserves axis intersection, it is dual to a measured
lamination, and asked if the “small” condition could be dropped.

In a followup, Skora noted that dihedral

S
-trees give counter examples (preserving axis intersection while not
being dual to a measured lamination), but showed that irreducible

S
-trees that preserve axis intersection are dual to measured
laminations
[
74
]
.

In this section, we prove Theorem
E
, in particular showing
that Skora’s theorem
[
74
]
follows readily from
our Theorems
A
and the lamination part of

D
and classical considerations
from actions on real trees.

We note that in a later followup, Skora showed that an
S
-tree with
a small action is also
dual to a measured lamination
[
75
]
.
It follows that the action being small is equivalent to it being irreducible and preserving axis intersection.

In turn, we also give three new characterizations of
S
-trees
dual to measured laminations (from Theorem
E
):

•

Irreducible
S
-trees that preserve characteristic intersection.

•

S
-trees satisfying R-oriented smoothing:

(9.2)

\displaystyle\|ab\|

\displaystyle\geq\|a\|+\|b\|

\displaystyle\mbox{ if }(a,b)\mbox{ are }\mbox{R-parallel;}

(9.3)

\displaystyle\|a\|+\|b\|

\displaystyle\geq\|ab\|

\displaystyle\mbox{ if }(a,b)\mbox{ are }\mbox{R-crossing}.

•

S
-trees where no parallel pair
(\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{b})
has incoherent overlap in the tree.

We start by proving a geometric criterion of reducibility for axis preserving actions.

Lemma 9.10
.

Suppose that an
S
-tree preserves axis intersection. If the
S
-tree
is irreducible, then for all hyperbolic elements
g\in\pi_{1}(S)
,
there exist a hyperbolic element
h\in\pi_{1}(S)
so that
\overrightarrow{h}

R-crosses
\overrightarrow{g}
and
T_{g}\cap T_{h}
is bounded.

Proof.

We will prove the contra-positive. Namely, if there exists a hyperbolic
element
g
so that for every hyperbolic element
h
with
\overrightarrow{h}
R-crossing

\overrightarrow{g}
, the intersection
T_{g}\cap T_{h}
is unbounded, we will deduce
that the action must be reducible. (Note
T_{g}\cap T_{h}
cannot be
empty since the action on
T
is axis-preserving.)

By Lemma
9.3
, it suffices to show that for any hyperbolic element
h
so that
(\overrightarrow{g},\overrightarrow{h})
are parallel (or anti-parallel),
T_{g}\cap T_{h}\neq\emptyset
.

For the sake of contradiction, suppose
(\overrightarrow{g},\overrightarrow{h})
are parallel and
T_{g}\cap T_{h}=\emptyset
. By density of axes of hyperbolic elements, let
k\in\pi_{1}(S)
so that
\overrightarrow{k}
R-crosses
\overrightarrow{g}
and
\overrightarrow{h}
, as in Figure
9.4a
. Since the
S
-tree preserves axis intersection,
T_{k}
intersects both
T_{g}
and
T_{h}
. Thus, by hypothesis,
T_{g}\cap T_{k}
is unbounded.

By Lemma
8.16
, for
n
large enough,

\overrightarrow{h^{n}k^{n}}
R-crosses
\overrightarrow{g}
(in addition to crossing
\overrightarrow{h}
). See
Figure
9.4b
for an illustration of the
purported situation in
T
. In
particular, since the action preserves axis intersection,
T_{h^{n}k^{n}}
and

T_{g}
must intersect. By assumption on
g
, this intersection is unbounded.
We claim this forces
T_{g}
and
T_{h}
to intersect. Indeed, the length of the overlap of
T_{h^{n}k^{n}}
cannot be strictly larger or smaller than that of the overlap of
T_{k}
and
T_{h}
(see Figure
9.4b
for an illustration of a “strictly larger overlap”), since otherwise, from the fact that
T_{h^{n}k^{n}}
and
T_{g}
intersect, this would create a loop. This implies that as soon as
T_{k}
and
T_{h^{n}k^{n}}
intersect, they do so in a ray. If
T_{k}
and
T_{h}
also overlap in a ray, then
T_{g},T_{h}
must overlap in the same ray, and hence
T_{g}\cap T_{h}\neq\emptyset
, a contradiction. Otherwise, the intersection of
T_{k}
and
T_{h}
is bounded. But then this forces the intersection of
T_{h^{n}k^{n}}
and
T_{g}
to be bounded, which is a contradiction.

If
(\overrightarrow{g},\overrightarrow{h})
are anti-parallel, proceed similarly with the parallel pair
(\overrightarrow{g},\overrightarrow{H})
.
∎

We are now ready for the main component of Theorem
E
.

Theorem 9.11
.

Let
T
be an irreducible
S
-tree that preserves axis
intersection. Then:

•

T
preserves characteristic intersection; and

•

\|\cdot\|
satisfies max-smoothing.

(a)

Configuration of axes on
\widetilde{S}

(b)

Configuration of axes on
T

Figure 9.4
.
The proof of Lemma
9.10

Note that, in light of Proposition
9.1
, to prove
Theorem
9.11
it suffices
to prove smoothing, i.e., that the multicurve with the crossing to be
smoothed—whichever of
[gh]
,
[gH]
, or
[g][h]
it arises
in—realizes one of
the translation lengths appearing in the maximum of the three.
In practice, in the proof below we will verify max-smoothing directly.

There is one particular fact that we will use multiple times in
different cases, so we pull it out as a lemma in advance. This lemma
is the key place where we invoke irreducibility.

Proposition 9.12
.

Let
T
be an irreducible
S
-tree that preserves axis
intersection. Then, for
g,h\in\pi_{1}(S)
, it cannot happen that

(\overrightarrow{g},\overrightarrow{h})
are parallel while
T_{g},T_{h}
intersect incoherently.

Proof.

If the overlap is bounded, according to
Lemma
9.2
there exists
m>0,n>0
so that

g^{m}
and
h^{-n}
form a good pair, and hence, by
Lemma
9.4
,
T_{g^{m}h^{n}}
and
T_{h^{n}g^{m}}

do not intersect. On the other hand, since
\overrightarrow{g},\overrightarrow{h}
are
parallel, it follows by Lemma
4.16
that

\overrightarrow{g^{m}h^{n}}
and
\overrightarrow{h^{n}g^{m}}
cross, which by preservation of
axis intersection implies
T_{g^{m}h^{n}}
and
T_{h^{n}g^{m}}
intersect, a
contradiction.

Suppose now
T_{g},T_{h}
have unbounded and incoherent overlap. We will use
the assumption of irreducibility to construct another pair with
bounded overlap. Suppose, without essential loss of generality,
that
(\overrightarrow{g},\overrightarrow{h})
are R-parallel,
and that
T_{g}\cap T_{h}
includes the attracting end of
T_{g}
. By
Lemma
9.10
, there exists

c_{0}\in\pi_{1}(S)
acting hyperbolically on
T
so that

T_{c_{0}}\cap T_{g}
is empty or bounded, and so that

(\overrightarrow{c_{0}},\overrightarrow{g})
R-cross.
By
Lemma
9.9
, if we set
c_{1}=g^{k}c_{0}g^{k}
for

k\geq 0
large enough, the overlap between
T_{c_{1}}
and
T_{g}

is coherent and, by Lemma
4.15
,
\overrightarrow{c_{1}}
still
R-crosses
\overrightarrow{g}
. Letting
c_{2}=g^{n}c_{1}g^{-n}
for
n
large
enough, we can ensure two things. On the one hand, the endpoints of

\overrightarrow{c_{2}}
are near
g^{+}
on the boundary of the disk and thus

(\overrightarrow{c_{2}},\overrightarrow{h})
are
parallel. On the other hand, the same conjugation also ensures
that the (still bounded and coherent) intersection between

T_{c_{2}}
and
T_{g}
is near the attracting end of
T_{g}
and thus

T_{c_{2}}
has bounded, incoherent intersection with
T_{h}
. Thus

c_{2}
and
h
form a pair that is parallel in the disk and
intersect incoherently on the tree, a contradiction.
See Figure
9.5
for a diagram of the
configuration.
∎

(a)

Configuration of axes on
\widetilde{S}
. There are other
possibilities for the position of

\overrightarrow{c_{0}}
and
\overrightarrow{c_{1}}
with respect to
\overrightarrow{h}
; only

\overrightarrow{c_{2}}
must be as shown.

(b)

Configuration of axes on
T

Figure 9.5
.
